On Counting Propositional Logic Thanks: This work has been partially supported by the ErC CoG “DIAPASoN”, GA 818616.
Melissa Antonelli, Ugo Dal Lago, Paolo Pistone

Abstract
We study counting propositional logic as an extension of propositional logic with counting quantifiers. We prove that the complexity of the underlying decision problem perfectly matches the appropriate level of Wagner’s counting hierarchy, but also that the resulting logic admits a satisfactory proof theoretical treatment. From the latter, a type system for a probabilistic λ \lambda -calculus is derived in the spirit of the Curry-Howard correspondence, showing the potential of counting propositional logic as a useful tool in several fields of theoretical computer science.



1
Introduction

Among the many intriguing relationships existing between logic and
computer science, we can certainly mention the ones between classical
propositional logic, on the one hand, and computational complexity, the
theory of programming languages, and many other branches of computer science, on the other.
As it is well known, indeed, classical propositional logic provided the
first example of an
NP
-complete problem
[
13
]
.
Moreover, formal systems for classical and
intuitionistic propositional logic correspond to type systems for

\lambda
-calculi and related formalisms
[
28
,
58
]
.

These lines of research evolved in several directions, resulting in
active sub-areas of computer science, in which variations of
propositional logic have been put in relation with complexity classes
other than
P
and
NP
or with type systems other than
simple types.
For example, the complexity of deciding
quantified

propositional logic formulas was proved to correspond to the polynomial
hierarchy
[
38
,
39
,
59
,
69
,
1
,
11
]
.
As another example, proof systems for propositional
linear

logic or
bunched
logic have inspired resource-conscious type systems in which duplication and sharing are taken into account and appropriately dealt with through the type system
[
43
,
65
]
.

Nevertheless, some developments in both computational complexity
and programming language theory have not found a precise
counterpart in propositional logic, at least so far.
One such development has to do with counting classes as introduced
by Valiant
[
64
]
and Wagner
[
66
,
67
,
68
]
.
The counting hierarchy is deeply related to randomized complexity
classes, such as
PP
, and has been treated logically by means of
tools from descriptive complexity and finite model
theory
[
34
]
.
Still, there is no counterpart for these classes in the sense of propositional logic,
at least to the best of the authors’ knowledge.
On the other hand, type systems for randomized
\lambda
-calculi
and guaranteeing various forms of termination properties have
been introduced in the last years
[
14
,
10
,
3
]
.
Again, although these systems are related to simple, intersection,
and linear dependent types,
no Curry-Howard correspondence is known for them.

This paper aims at bridging the gap by introducing a new quantified
propositional logic,
called counting propositional logic (
CPL
, for short).
The quantifiers of
CPL
are inherently quantitative,
as they are designed to
count
the number of values
of the bound propositional variables satisfying the argument formula.
We study the proof theory of
CPL
together with its relations to
computational complexity and programming language theory,
obtaining two main results.
The first achievement consists in showing that
deciding prenex normal forms is complete for the counting
hierarchy’s appropriate level,
in the spirit of the well-known correspondence between
quantified propositional logic and the polynomial hierarchy.
The second result is the introduction of a type system for a
probabilistic
\lambda
-calculus based on counting quantifiers,
together with the proof that this system guarantees the
expected form of termination property.
Beyond offering a conceptual bridge with type systems
from the literature,
our system effectively suggests that the Curry-Howard
correspondence can have a probabilistic counterpart.

Along the way, many side results are proved, and a somehow
unusual route to our two main achievements is followed.
After motivating the introduction of counting propositional logic
in Section
2
, two sections are devoted to its syntax,
semantics and proof theory.
Specifically, Section
3
deals with
univariate
CPL
, namely the fragment in which counting
quantification is nameless as there is only one,
global counting variable.
In this case, the underlying model and proof theory turn out
to be simple,
but the correspondence with computational complexity is limited
to the class
\textsf{P}^{\sharp\textsf{P}}
.
A sound and complete proof system in the form of a one-sided,
single-succedent sequent calculus on labelled formulas
is the main result here.
The multivariate logic, in which many variables are available,
is treated in Section
4
by generalizing the
corresponding results from Section
3
.
In Section
5
, the complexity of the decision problem
for multivariate
CPL
is proved to correspond to the whole
counting hierarchy.
The proof proceeds by a careful analysis of prenex normal forms
for the logic,
which by construction have precisely the shape one needs to
match Wagner’s complete problems
[
67
]
.
Section
6
is devoted to the introduction and discussion
of a type system whose type derivations can be seen as proofs
in
CPL
, and in which typability ensures a probabilistic
termination property.
Finally, an extension of the syntax for formulas of
CPL

arithmetic is discussed in Section
7
.

2
On Counting Quantifiers, Computational
Complexity, and Type Systems

When considering the class
PL
of propositional logic formulas,
checking any such formula for satisfiability is the paradigmatic

NP
-complete problem,
while the subclass of
PL
consisting of tautologies is, dually,

coNP
-complete.
How could we capture these two classes by means of one
single logical concept, rather than having to refer to satisfiability
and validity?
The answer consists in switching to quantified propositional
logic
[
38
,
39
,
59
,
69
,
11
]
,
in which existential and universal quantification over propositional
variables are available
from within
the logic.

Satisfiability corresponds to the truth of closed, existentially
quantified formulas in the form

\exists x_{1}\dots\exists x_{n}A

(where
A
is quantifier-free),
while validity stands for the truth of closed, universally quantified
formulas in the form
\forall x_{1}\dots\forall x_{n}A
(where

A
is quantifier-free).
For example, the formula

\exists x_{1}\exists x_{2}\exists x_{3}\big((x_{1}\land\lnot x_{2})\lor(x_{2}\land\lnot x_{3})\lor(x_{3}\land\lnot x_{1})\big)

expresses the fact that the formula
(x_{1}\land\lnot x_{2})\lor(x_{2}\land\lnot x_{3})\lor(x_{3}\land\lnot x_{1})
is
satisfied by
at least
one model.
As it is well known, all this can be generalized to the whole
polynomial hierarchy,
\mathsf{PH}
, where each level of the hierarchy
is characterized by a quantified propositional formula
(in prenex normal form) with the corresponding
number of quantifier alternations.
Here, universal and existential quantifications play the
role of the acceptance condition in the machines defining the
corresponding complexity class.

But what happens if other kinds of quantifications,
still on propositional variables,
replace the standard universal and existential quantifications?
Would it be possible to be more
quantitative
in the
sense of, say, Wagner’s counting quantifiers
[
67
]
?
Apparently, this question does not make much sense,
as the variables one would like to quantify over
only take
two
possible values.
However, this problem can be overcome by permitting to
simultaneously quantify over
all
the propositional variables
which are free in the argument formula.
In other words, the interpretation of a formula
A
is not the
single truth-value that
A
gets in one given valuation,
but the set of
all
valuations making
A
true.
If we let
\llbracket A\rrbracket
denote such a set, then we can
interpret a quantified formula of the form
\mathbf{C}^{q}A
as saying that

A
is true in
at least a fraction
q\cdot 2^{n}
of the possible

2^{n}
assignments to its
n
variables.
As an example, one could assert that
a formula like

\mathbf{C}^{\frac{1}{2}}\big((x_{1}\land\lnot x_{2})\lor(x_{2}\land\lnot x_{3})\lor(x_{3}\land\lnot x_{1})\big)

is valid because
(x_{1}\land\lnot x_{2})\lor(x_{2}\land\lnot x_{3})\lor(x_{3}\land\lnot x_{1})

is true in at least
4
(actually, in
6
) of its
2^{3}=8
models.

By the way, the aforementioned counting quantifier is very reminiscent
of the operators on classes of languages
which Wagner introduced in his
seminal works on the counting hierarchy
[
66
,
67
]
.
Still, the logical status of
such counting operations has not been investigated further, at least
not in the realm of propositional logic (see Section
8

for more details).
This is precisely one part of the study developed in this paper:
the syntax, semantics, and proof theory of the resulting logic,
called counting propositional logic,
are fully explored, together with
connections with computational complexity.
A glimpse of this is given in
Section
2.1
below, while Section
2.2
is
devoted to the applications of our counting logic to
programming language theory,
which will be further studied in Section
7
.
The development of a Curry-Howard-style correspondence in
framework of randomized computation is the main
result of this work.

2.1
From Counting Quantifiers to the Counting Hierarchy, and Back

In computational complexity theory, the problem of checking if a
formula of
PL
is true in at least half of its models is
called
\mathtt{MajSAT}
.
This problem is known to be complete for
PP
,
the class of decision problems which can be solved in
polytime with an error probability strictly less than
\frac{1}{2}
.

PP
is also related to
\textsf{P}^{\sharp\mathtt{SAT}}
, the class of counting
problems associated with the decision problems in
NP
,
since
\textsf{P}^{\textsf{PP}}=\textsf{P}^{\sharp\mathtt{SAT}}

(and both these classes contain the whole
polynomial hierarchy, by Toda’s Theorem
[
60
,
61
]
).
In Section
3
, we introduce the
univariate
counting
propositional logic,
\textsf{{CPL}}_{0}
, a logic containing a counting quantifier

\mathbf{C}^{q}
(where
q
is a rational number between
0
and
1
),
such that, as previously explained,
\mathbf{C}^{q}A

intuitively means that “
A
is
true in at least
q\cdot n
of its
n
models”.
We prove that, in addition to providing a purely logical description
of
PP
-complete problems, such as
\mathtt{MajSAT}
,

\textsf{{CPL}}_{0}
-validity can be decided in
\textsf{P}^{\textsf{PP}}=\textsf{P}^{\sharp\mathtt{SAT}}
.

However, our study of counting quantifiers does not stop here.
As it is known, a full hierarchy of complexity classes
comprising counting problems,
the so-called
counting hierarchy
,
was (independently) introduced in
[
66
,
67
]

and
[
1
]
in analogy with the polynomial
hierarchy, by letting

\mathsf{CH}_{0}=\textsf{P}\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\mathsf{CH}_{n+1}=\textsf{PP}^{\mathsf{CH}_{n}}.

A typical problem belonging to this hierarchy (in fact one which is
complete for
\mathsf{CH}_{2}=\textsf{PP}^{\textsf{PP}}
) is the problem
\mathtt{MajMajSAT}

of, given a formula of
PL
, call it
A
,
containing two disjoint sets
\mathbf{x}
and
\mathbf{y}
of variables,
determining whether for the majority of the valuations of
\mathbf{x}
,
the majority of the valuations of
\mathbf{y}
makes
A
true.
In Section
4
, we introduce the
multivariate

counting propositional logic,
CPL
, a logic in which propositional
variables are grouped into disjoint sets using
names
,

a,b,c,\dots
, and where one can form
named
counting
quantified formulas, such as
\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}A
,
with the intuitive meaning that “
A
is
true in at least
q\cdot n
of the
n
valuations of the variables of name

a
”.
A problem like
\mathtt{MajMajSAT}
is thus captured by
formulas of
CPL
in the form
\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}\mathbf{C}^{r}_{b}A
.
Our main result, in Section
5
, is a
characterization of the full counting hierarchy by
CPL
.
This amounts at
(1) proving that any formula of
CPL
is equivalent to one in

prenex form

\mathbf{C}^{q_{1}}_{a_{1}}\dots\mathbf{C}^{q_{k}}_{a_{k}}A
,
where
A
is quantifier-free, and
(2) showing that the prenex formulas with
k
nested counting
quantifiers characterize the level
k
of the counting hierarchy.

2.2
A Logical Foundation of Type Systems for Randomized Calculi

Propositional formulas are not only instances of problems.
Indeed, they can also be seen as abstractions specifying
the intended behavior of higher-order programs.
As an example, the formula

(A\rightarrow B)\rightarrow C
can be seen
as the type of a program
t
taking in input a function of type

(A\rightarrow B)
and producing in output an object of type

C
.
This analogy between formulas and types can be generalized
to programs and proofs, becoming a formal correspondence,
known as the Curry-Howard correspondence.
If the underlying program has probabilistic features,
it can well happen that, for example,
the program
t
above produces in output an element of type

C

only half of the times
,
or that
t
requires that the input function takes in input an object
of type
A
only
with probability at most equal to

\frac{3}{4}
.
Indeed, some type systems specifically designed for
so-called randomized
\lambda
-calculi exist in which types are
enriched with quantitative information about the probability of
behaving according to the underlying type,
this way somehow lifting the probabilistic monad up to the
level of types
[
14
,
10
,
3
]
.

Can all this be justified from a logical viewpoint, in the spirit of
the aforementioned Curry-Howard correspondence?
The literature does not offer much in this direction.
Thanks to
CPL
, however, the answer to the question above
becomes positive, as we will show in Section
6
.
Just to give you a glimpse of what waits for you there,
if we “enrich” the formula

(A\rightarrow B)\rightarrow C
,
with counting quantifiers, for example,
in the following way,

(\mathbf{C}^{\frac{3}{4}}_{a}A\rightarrow B\big)\rightarrow\mathbf{C}^{\frac{1}{2}}_{b}C

(1)

we obtain a type which can be used to capture probabilistic behaviors.
In fact, the subscripts
a
and
b
very nicely correspond
to probabilistic events, in the sense of the recent work by
Dal Lago et al. on decomposing probabilistic

\lambda
-calculi
[
15
]
.
Remarkably, the resulting type system not only enjoys
subject reduction,
but can also be proved to normalize in the expected way,
for example any term typable as (
1
)
(when applied to an argument of the appropriate type)
normalizes to a value having type
C

with probability at least equal to
\frac{1}{2}
.

3
Univariate Counting Propositional Logic

In this section, we introduce propositional counting logic in a
simplistic version, called
\textsf{{CPL}}_{0}
.
We present a sound and complete proof system for
\textsf{{CPL}}_{0}

and show that derivability in this logic is in the class
\textsf{P}^{\sharp\mathtt{SAT}}
.
In the next Section
4
, we will introduce the whole
propositional counting logic,
CPL
,
as a generalization of
\textsf{{CPL}}_{0}
which additionally
provides a characterization of each layer of the
counting hierarchy, Section
5
.

3.1
From Valuations to Measurable Sets

In standard propositional logic, the interpretation of a formula
containing propositional atoms,
p_{1},\dots,p_{n}
, call it
A
,
is a truth-value,
\llbracket A\rrbracket_{\theta}
, depending on a valuation

\theta:\{p_{1},\dots,p_{n}\}\to 2
(where
2=\{0,1\}
).
Yet, what if one defined the semantics of
A
as consisting
of
all
valuations making
A
true?
Since propositional formulas can have an arbitrary number
of propositional values,
it makes sense to consider maps
f:\omega\to 2
, instead of a finite valuation.
Hence, the interpretation of a formula, call it
A
,
should be the set
\llbracket A\rrbracket\subseteq 2^{\omega}

made of all
f\in 2^{\omega}
“making
A
true”.
In fact, it is not difficult to see that such sets
are
measurable sets
of the standard Borel algebra

\mathcal{B}(2^{\omega})
. First, an atomic proposition
p_{i}
is
interpreted as the
cylinder set
,
[
8
]

\mathrm{Cyl}(i)=\{f\in 2^{\omega}\mid f(i)=1\}

(2)

of all
f\in 2^{\omega}
, such that
f(i)=1
.
Then, the interpretation of
non-atomic propositions is provided by the standard
\sigma
-algebra
operations of complementation, finite intersection and finite union.

In this setting, we define two counting operators,
by adapting the notion of counting quantifier presented
in
[
66
,
67
]
.
In doing so, given a formula
A
, it becomes possible to
express that
A
is satisfied by,
for example
at least half
or
by
strictly less than half
of its models.
Semantically, this amounts at checking that

\mu(\llbracket A\rrbracket)\geq\frac{1}{2}
or
\mu(\llbracket A\rrbracket)<\frac{1}{2}
,
where
\mu
denotes the standard Borel measure
on
\mathcal{B}(2^{\omega})
.
Generally speaking, for all rational
numbers
q\in\mathbb{Q}_{[0,1]}
,
we can define two new formulas

\mathbf{C}^{q}A
and
\mathbf{D}^{q}A

(depending on no propositional atom) with the
intuitive meaning that
\mathbf{C}^{q}A
is valid when

\mu(\llbracket A\rrbracket)\geq q
and
that
\mathbf{D}^{q}A
is valid when
\mu(\llbracket A\rrbracket)<q
.
This leads to the following definition:

Definition 3.1

(Semantics of
\textsf{{CPL}}_{0}
)
.

The
formulas
of
\textsf{{CPL}}_{0}
are defined by the grammar below:

A,B:=\mathbf{i}\;\;\mbox{\Large{$\mid$}}\;\;\lnot A\;\;\mbox{\Large{$\mid$}}\;\;A\land B\;\;\mbox{\Large{$\mid$}}\;\;A\lor B\;\;\mbox{\Large{$\mid$}}\;\;\mathbf{C}^{q}A\;\;\mbox{\Large{$\mid$}}\;\;\mathbf{D}^{q}A

where
i
is an arbitrary natural number and
q
is a rational number
from the interval
[0,1]
.
For each formula
A
of
\textsf{{CPL}}_{0}
, its
interpretation

\llbracket A\rrbracket\in\mathcal{B}(2^{\omega})

is a measurable set inductively defined as follows:

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathbf{i}\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Cyl}(i)

\displaystyle\llbracket\lnot A\rrbracket

\displaystyle=2^{\omega}-\llbracket A\rrbracket

\displaystyle\llbracket A\land B\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\llbracket A\rrbracket\cap\llbracket B\rrbracket

\displaystyle\llbracket A\lor B\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\llbracket A\rrbracket\cup\llbracket B\rrbracket

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathbf{C}^{q}A\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}2^{\omega}&\text{ if }\mu(\llbracket A\rrbracket)\geq q\\
\emptyset&\text{ otherwise }\end{cases}

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathbf{D}^{q}A\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}2^{\omega}&\text{ if }\mu(\llbracket A\rrbracket)<q\\
\emptyset&\text{ otherwise }\end{cases}

The notion of logical equivalence is defined in a standard way:
two formulas, call them
A
and
B
,
are
logically equivalent
,
A\equiv B
,
when
\llbracket A\rrbracket=\llbracket B\rrbracket
.
A formula, call it
A
, is
valid

when
\llbracket A\rrbracket=2^{\omega}
, and
invalid

when
\llbracket A\rrbracket=\emptyset
.
For all classical tautologies
A
,
each formulas of the form
\mathbf{C}^{q}A
is valid, whereas
for any formula of
\textsf{{CPL}}_{0}
, call it
B
, formulas of the form

\mathbf{C}^{0}B
are valid, see Lemma
20

in Appendix
A.1
.

Counting quantifiers are somehow non-standard.
Indeed, they are inter-definable but not dual, as:
1
1

1

For the detailed proof, see Lemma
21
in Appendix
A.1
.

\mathbf{C}^{q}A\equiv\lnot\mathbf{D}^{q}A\qquad\qquad\mathbf{D}^{q}A\equiv\lnot\mathbf{C}^{q}A.

(3)

Remarkably, using the these quantifiers,
it is also possible to express that a formula is satisfied
by
strictly more than
q
or by
no more than
q
,
for example, given a formula
A
, this can formalized as
(respectively)
\mathbf{D}^{1-q}\lnot A
and
\mathbf{C}^{1-q}\lnot A
.
In order to further clarity the semantics of
\textsf{{CPL}}_{0}
,
let us consider a few examples.

Example 1
.

Let
A=B\lor C
, where
B,C
are respectively
the formulas
\mathbf{0}\land\lnot\mathbf{1}
and
\lnot\mathbf{0}\land\mathbf{1}
.
As it can easily
be checked, the two measurable sets
\llbracket B\rrbracket
and

\llbracket C\rrbracket
are disjoint and have both measure
\frac{1}{4}
.
Hence

\mu(\llbracket A\rrbracket)=\mu(\llbracket B\rrbracket)+\mu(\llbracket C\rrbracket)=\frac{1}{2}
,
which means that the formula
\mathbf{C}^{\frac{1}{2}}A
is valid.

Example 2
.

Let
A=B\lor C
, where
B,C
are respectively
the formulas
(\mathbf{0}\land\lnot\mathbf{1})\vee\mathbf{2}
and
(\lnot\mathbf{0}\land\mathbf{1})\lor\mathbf{2}
.
In this case the two
sets
\llbracket B\rrbracket
and
\llbracket C\rrbracket
have
both measure
\frac{5}{8}

(in fact,
5
of their
8
models satisfy them) but they are not disjoint, and

\llbracket B\rrbracket\cap\llbracket C\rrbracket=\mathrm{Cyl}(2)
,
which has measure
\frac{1}{2}
.
Hence,

\mu(\llbracket A\rrbracket)=\mu(\llbracket B\rrbracket)+\mu(\llbracket C\rrbracket)-\mu\big(\mathrm{Cyl}(2)\big)=\frac{3}{4}
, which means that the formula
\mathbf{D}^{1}A
is valid.

3.2
The Proof Theory of
\textsf{{CPL}}_{0}

In order to obtain a sound and complete proof system for
\textsf{{CPL}}_{0}
,
a one-sided, single-succedent, and
labelled sequent calculus is introduced.
Notice that, although the quantitative semantics for our counting logic
appears as very natural,
the definition of a complete deductive system is not so trivial.
The language of our calculus is defined by
sequents made of
labelled formulas
,

b\rightarrowtail A
,
b\leftarrowtail A
,
where
A
is a formula of
\textsf{{CPL}}_{0}
and
b
is Boolean formula.
Intuitively, a labelled formula
b\rightarrowtail A
is true when
the (measurable) set of valuations making
b
true
is included in
\llbracket A\rrbracket
, and similarly,
a labelled formula
b\leftarrowtail A
is true when

\llbracket A\rrbracket
is included in the set of valuations making

b
true.

In order to formally define the language of this calculus,
we will start by introducing Boolean formulas.

Definition 3.2

(Boolean Formulas)
.

Boolean formulas
, starting from a countable set of atoms,

x_{0},x_{1},x_{2},\dots
, are defined by the following grammar:

b,c:=x_{i}\;\;\mbox{\Large{$\mid$}}\;\;\top\;\;\mbox{\Large{$\mid$}}\;\;\bot\;\;\mbox{\Large{$\mid$}}\;\;\lnot b\;\;\mbox{\Large{$\mid$}}\;\;b\land c\;\;\mbox{\Large{$\mid$}}\;\;b\lor c

The
interpretation
\llbracket b\rrbracket\in\mathcal{B}(2^{\omega})
of a Boolean
formula
b
is defined inductively as follows:

\displaystyle\llbracket x_{i}\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Cyl}(i)

\displaystyle\llbracket\neg b\rrbracket

\displaystyle=2^{\omega}\ –\ \llbracket b\rrbracket

\displaystyle\llbracket\top\rrbracket

\displaystyle=2^{\omega}

\displaystyle\llbracket b\land c\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\llbracket b\rrbracket\cap\llbracket c\rrbracket

\displaystyle\llbracket\bot\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\emptyset

\displaystyle\llbracket b\lor c\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\llbracket b\rrbracket\cup\llbracket c\rrbracket

Let
b\vDash c
and
b\mathrel{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vDash$}}}\vDash c

be shorthands for, respectively,

\llbracket b\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket c\rrbracket
and

\llbracket b\rrbracket=\llbracket c\rrbracket
.
Then,
external hypotheses
and
labelled formulas

can be introduced.

Definition 3.3

(External Hypotheses)
.

An
external hypothesis
is an expression of one of the following forms:
b\vDash c
,
b\mathrel{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vDash$}}}\vDash c
or
\mu(\llbracket b\rrbracket)\triangleright q
, where
\triangleright\in\{\geq,\leq,<,>,=\}
,
b,c
are Boolean formulas, and
q\in\mathbb{Q}_{[0,1]}
.

Definition 3.4

(Labelled Formulas)
.

A
labelled formula
, call it L,
is an expression of the form

b\rightarrowtail A
or
b\leftarrowtail A
,
where
b
is a Boolean formula and
A
is a formula of
\textsf{{CPL}}_{0}
.

The measure of the interpretation of a Boolean formula,
\mu(\llbracket b\rrbracket)
, can be related to the number
\sharp\mathtt{SAT}(b)
of the valuations making
b
true as follows:

Lemma 1
.

For each Boolean formula, call it
b
, with propositional variables,

x_{0},\dots,x_{n-1}
,

\mu(\llbracket b\rrbracket)={\sharp\mathtt{SAT}(b)}\cdot{2^{-n}}.

Proof.

To any valuation
\theta:\{x_{0},\dots,x_{n-1}\}\to 2
we can
associate a measurable set
X(\theta)\in\mathcal{B}(2^{\omega})
by
letting

X(\theta)=\{f\mid\forall i<n,f(i)=\theta(x_{i})\}=\bigcap_{i=0}^{n-1}\mathrm{Cyl}(i)^{\theta(x_{i})}

where
\mathrm{Cyl}(i)^{\theta(x_{i})}
is
\mathrm{Cyl}(i)
if
\theta(x_{i})=1
and

\overline{\mathrm{Cyl}(i)}
otherwise.
One can check by induction that for
all Boolean formula
b
,
\llbracket b\rrbracket=\bigcup_{\theta\vDash b}X(\theta)
.
Then, since for all distinct
\theta,
and
\theta^{\prime}
,

X(\theta)\cap X(\theta^{\prime})=\emptyset
, we deduce

\sharp\mathtt{SAT}(b)\cdot 2^{-n}=\sum_{\theta\vDash b}2^{-n}=\sum_{\theta\vDash b}\mu(X(\theta))=\mu(\bigcup_{\theta\vDash b}X(\theta))=\mu(\llbracket b\rrbracket)
.
∎

A
labelled sequent
of
\textsf{{CPL}}_{0}
is a sequent of the form
\vdash L
.
The proof system for
\textsf{{CPL}}_{0}
is defined by the rules displayed
in Fig.
1
.
We let
\vdash_{\textsf{{CPL}}_{0}}L
denote the fact that

\vdash L
is derivable by those rules.
Notice that some of the rules have semantic statements among their
premisses, something which can be seen as unsatisfactory.
However, as it will become clear in Subsection
3.3
,
such premisses make sense from a computational viewpoint:
they correspond to the idea that, when searching for a proof,
one might need to call for an
oracle
,
a central concept in the definition of counting complexity classes.
The presence of such external hypothesis does not make our proof
theory trivial and, in fact, proof search is sometimes convoluted.
Derivations are defined in the standard way.
We illustrate in Fig.
2
a
simple example of derivation in
\textsf{{CPL}}_{0}
.

Initial Sequents

b\vDash x_{n}

\mathsf{Ax1}

\vdash b\rightarrowtail\mathbf{n}

x_{n}\vDash b

\mathsf{Ax2}

\vdash b\leftarrowtail\mathbf{n}

\vdash d\rightarrowtail A

\vdash b\rightarrowtail A

\vdash b\leftarrowtail A

\vdash b\rightarrowtail\neg A

\lnot{c}\vDash b

\vdash b\leftarrowtail\neg A

\vdash b\rightarrowtail A

\mathsf{R1}_{\vee}^{\rightarrowtail}

\vdash b\rightarrowtail A\vee B

\vdash b\rightarrowtail B

\mathsf{R2}^{\rightarrowtail}_{\vee}

\vdash b\leftarrowtail A\vee B

\vdash b\leftarrowtail A

\vdash b\leftarrowtail B

\mathsf{R}_{\vee}^{\leftarrowtail}

\vdash b\leftarrowtail A\vee B

\vdash b\rightarrowtail A

\vdash b\rightarrowtail B

\mathsf{R}_{\wedge}^{\rightarrowtail}

\vdash b\rightarrowtail A\wedge B

\vdash b\leftarrowtail A

\textsf{R1}_{\wedge}^{\leftarrowtail}

\vdash b\leftarrowtail A\wedge B

\vdash b\leftarrowtail B

\mathsf{R2}_{\wedge}^{\leftarrowtail}

\vdash b\leftarrowtail A\wedge B

Counting Rules

\mu(\llbracket b\rrbracket)=0

\mathsf{R_{\mu}^{\rightarrowtail}}

\vdash b\rightarrowtail A

\mu(\llbracket b\rrbracket)=1

\mathsf{R_{\mu}^{\leftarrowtail}}

\vdash b\leftarrowtail A

\vdash c\rightarrowtail A

\mu(\llbracket c\rrbracket)\geq q

\mathsf{R}_{\mathbf{C}}^{\rightarrowtail}

\vdash b\rightarrowtail\mathbf{C}^{q}A

\vdash c\leftarrowtail A

\mu(\llbracket c\rrbracket)<q

\mathsf{R}_{\mathbf{C}}^{\leftarrowtail}

\vdash b\leftarrowtail

\vdash c\leftarrowtail A

\mu(\llbracket c\rrbracket)<q

\mathsf{R}_{\mathbf{D}}^{\rightarrowtail}

\vdash b\rightarrowtail\mathbf{D}^{q}
A

\vdash c\rightarrowtail A

\mu(\llbracket c\rrbracket)\geq q

\mathsf{R}_{\mathbf{D}}^{\leftarrowtail}

\vdash b\leftarrowtail

\mathbf{D}^{q}
A

Figure 1
:
Proof system for
\textsf{{CPL}}_{0}
.

\mathsf{Ax1}

\mathsf{R1}_{\vee}^{\rightarrowtail}

\vdash x_{1}\rightarrowtail\mathbf{1}\vee\mathbf{2}

Figure 2
:
Derivation
\vdash\top\rightarrowtail\mathbf{C}^{\frac{3}{4}}(\mathbf{1}\lor\mathbf{2})
.

As anticipated, the given proof system is sound and complete with respects of the semantics of
\textsf{{CPL}}_{0}
.
Validity of labelled formulas and sequents is defined as
follows:

Definition 3.5

(Validity)
.

A labelled formula
b\rightarrowtail A

(
resp.
b\leftarrowtail A)

is
valid
, denoted
\vDash b\rightarrowtail A

(resp.
\vDash b\leftarrowtail A
), when

\llbracket b\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket A\rrbracket

(resp.
\llbracket A\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket b\rrbracket
).
A sequent

\vdash L
is
valid
, denoted
\vDash L
,
when it contains a valid labelled formula.

Remark 1
.

For any formula of
\textsf{{CPL}}_{0}
, call it
A
,
one can construct a Boolean formula
b_{A}

such that
\llbracket A\rrbracket=\llbracket b_{A}\rrbracket
. The
only non-trivial cases are
A=\mathbf{C}^{q}B
and

A=\mathbf{D}^{q}B
. In the first case we let
b_{A}=\top

if
\mu(\llbracket B\rrbracket)\geq q
and
b_{A}=\bot
if

\mu(\llbracket B\rrbracket)<q
, and similarly for the second case.

The soundness of the proof system can be proved by a standard
induction on derivation height.
2
2

2

The complete proof is offered in the corresponding Proposition
2
in Appendix
A.2
.

Proposition 2

(Soundness)
.

If
\vdash_{\textsf{{CPL}}_{0}}L
holds, then
\vDash L
holds.

The completeness proof is more complex.
It relies on the introduction of a
decomposition relation
,

\leadsto
, between finite sets of sequents.
Reduction
\leadsto
is in its turn defined basing on a
relation
\leadsto_{0}
between sequents and
finite sets of sequents,
which is generated by clauses like the following ones:

\displaystyle\text{if }b\vDash c\lor d,\quad\vdash b\rightarrowtail A\lor B\

\displaystyle\leadsto_{0}\ \{\vdash c\rightarrowtail A,\vdash d\rightarrowtail B\}

\displaystyle\quad\vdash b\rightarrowtail A\land B\

\displaystyle\leadsto_{0}\ \{\vdash b\rightarrowtail A,\vdash b\rightarrowtail B\}

\displaystyle\text{if }\mu(\llbracket c\rrbracket)\geq q,\quad\vdash b\rightarrowtail\mathbf{C}^{q}A\

\displaystyle\leadsto_{0}\ \{\vdash c\rightarrowtail A\}

\displaystyle\text{if }\mu(\llbracket c\rrbracket)<q,\quad\vdash b\rightarrowtail\mathbf{D}^{q}A\

\displaystyle\leadsto_{0}\ \{\vdash c\leftarrowtail A\}

and letting
\Phi\leadsto\Psi
whenever,
\Phi=\Phi^{\prime}\cup\{\vdash L\}
,

\vdash L\leadsto_{0}\Psi^{\prime}
and
\Psi=\Phi^{\prime}\cup\Psi^{\prime}
.
3
3

3

The
complete definition of
\leadsto_{0}
and
\leadsto
is presented in the Appendix
A
and specifically, see respectively, Definition
A.5
and Definition
A.6
.

Lemma 3
.

The reduction
\leadsto
is strongly normalizing.

Lemma
3
is proved in a standard way by
defining a measure
\mathsf{ms}(\Phi)
over finite sets of sequents and showing
that whenever
\Phi\leadsto\Psi
,
\mathsf{ms}(\Psi)<\mathsf{ms}(\Phi)
.
For a sequent
\phi
=
\vdash L
,
let
\mathsf{ms}(\phi)
be the size of
L
, which is
\mathsf{cn}(L)
.
Then,
\mathsf{ms}(\Phi)
is defined as
\sum_{i}^{k}3^{\mathsf{ms}(\phi_{i})}
,
where
\Phi=\{\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{k}\}
.
4
4

4

For further details on the proof, see Appendix
A.2
. Specifically, the precise notions of
number of connectives and of set measure can be found as
Definition
A.9
and Definition
A.10
,
while the proof of
\leadsto
strong normalization is presented as
Lemma
3
in
A.2
.

For a finite set of sequents
\Phi=\{\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{k}\}
, let

\vDash\Phi

(resp.,
\vdash_{\textsf{{CPL}}_{0}}\Phi
) indicate that

\vDash\phi_{i}

(reps.,
\vdash_{\textsf{{CPL}}_{0}}\phi_{i}
) holds for all
i=1,\dots,k
.
The fundamental ingredients of the completeness proof
are expressed by the following three properties:

Lemma 4
.

Concerning
\textsf{{CPL}}_{0}
, the following properties hold:

i

if
\Phi
is
\leadsto
-normal, then
\vDash\Phi
if and only if

\vdash_{\textsf{{CPL}}_{0}}\Phi

ii.

if
\vDash\Phi
, there is a
\Psi
such that
\Phi\leadsto\Psi
and
\vDash\Psi

iii.

if
\vdash_{\textsf{{CPL}}_{0}}\Psi
and
\Phi\leadsto\Psi
, then
\vdash_{\textsf{{CPL}}_{0}}\Phi
.

Using the properties above, one can check that if
\vDash L

holds, then by ii.
\vDash\Phi
holds for some
\leadsto
-normal form

\Phi
of
\vdash L
; then by i.,
\vdash_{\textsf{{CPL}}_{0}}\Phi
holds and by iii. we
conclude that
\vdash_{\textsf{{CPL}}_{0}}L
.
5
5

5

The full proof of completeness is presented in Appendix
A.2
. Specifically,
i. is established as Lemma
,
ii. is proved as Lemma
25
,
and iii. corresponds to Lemma
26
.

Proposition 5

(Completeness)
.

If
\vDash L
holds, then
\vdash_{\textsf{{CPL}}_{0}}L
holds.

Remark 2
.

A remarkable consequence of the completeness theorem is that the following cut-rule comes immediately out to be derivable in
\textsf{{CPL}}_{0}
:

\vdash c\rightarrowtail\lnot A\vee B

\vdash d\rightarrowtail A

b\vDash c\land d

\mathsf{Cut}

\vdash b\rightarrowtail B

3.3
\textsf{{CPL}}_{0}
-validity is in
\textsf{P}^{\sharp\mathtt{SAT}}

As the right-hand premisses of the counting rules

\mathsf{R}_{\mathbf{C}}^{\rightarrowtail}/\mathsf{R}_{\mathbf{C}}^{\leftarrowtail}
and
\mathsf{R}_{\mathbf{D}}^{\rightarrowtail}/\mathsf{R}_{\mathbf{D}}^{\leftarrowtail}
suggest,
the inference of formulas such as
\mathbf{C}^{q}A
or

\mathbf{D}^{q}A
can be seen as obtained by invoking
an
oracle
, providing a suitable measurement.
Actually, the needed forms of measurement are expressed
as
\mu(\llbracket b\rrbracket)
,
where
b
is a Boolean formula.
As shown by Lemma
1
,
these measurements correspond to actually
counting

the number of valuations satisfying such formulas.
We can make this intuition precise by showing that validity
in
\textsf{{CPL}}_{0}
can be decided by a polytime algorithm having access to an oracle for the problem
\sharp\mathtt{SAT}
of counting the models of a Boolean formula.

Let us define a formula of
\textsf{{CPL}}_{0}
, call it
A
,

closed
if it is either of the form

\mathbf{C}^{q}B
or
\mathbf{D}^{q}B
or is a negation,
conjunction or disjunction of closed formulas.
It can be easily checked by induction on the structure
of formulas that for all closed formula, its interpretation
is either
2^{\omega}
or
\emptyset
, see Lemma
27
in Appendix
A.3
.
We define by mutual recursion two polytime algorithms

\mathtt{Bool}
and
\mathtt{Val}
such that, for each formula of
\textsf{{CPL}}_{0}
,
call it
A
,
\mathtt{Bool}(A)
is the Boolean formula

b_{A}
, and, for all closed formula
A
,

\mathtt{Val}(A)=1
if and only if
\llbracket A\rrbracket=2^{\omega}

and
\mathtt{Val}(A)=0
if and only if
\llbracket A\rrbracket=\emptyset
.
The algorithm
\mathtt{Val}(A)
will make use of a
\sharp\mathtt{SAT}
oracle.
The two algorithms are defined as follows:

\displaystyle\mathtt{Bool}(n)

\displaystyle=x_{n}

\displaystyle\mathtt{Bool}(A_{1}\land A_{2})

\displaystyle=\mathtt{Bool}(A_{1})\land\mathtt{Bool}(A_{2})

\displaystyle\mathtt{Bool}(A_{1}\lor A_{2})

\displaystyle=\mathtt{Bool}(A_{1})\lor\mathtt{Bool}(A_{2})

\displaystyle\mathtt{Bool}(\lnot A_{1})

\displaystyle=\lnot\mathtt{Bool}(A_{1})

\displaystyle\mathtt{Bool}(\mathbf{C}^{q}A_{1})

\displaystyle=\mathtt{Val}(\mathbf{C}^{q}A_{1})

\displaystyle\mathtt{Bool}(\mathbf{D}^{q}A_{1})

\displaystyle=\mathtt{Val}(\mathbf{D}^{q}A_{1})

\displaystyle\mathtt{Val}(A_{1}\land A_{2})

\displaystyle=\mathtt{Val}(A_{1})\texttt{ AND }\mathtt{Val}(A_{2})

\displaystyle\mathtt{Val}(A_{1}\lor A_{2})

\displaystyle=\mathtt{Val}(A_{1})\texttt{ OR }\mathtt{Val}(A_{2})

\displaystyle\mathtt{Val}(\lnot A_{1})

\displaystyle=\texttt{NOT }\mathtt{Val}(A_{1})

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}\mathtt{Val}(\mathbf{C}^{q}A_{1})\\
\ \\
\ \end{matrix}

\displaystyle\ \begin{matrix}=&\texttt{let }b=\mathtt{Bool}(A_{1})\ \texttt{in}\\
&\texttt{let }n=\sharp\texttt{Var}(b)\ \texttt{in }\\
&\frac{\sharp\mathtt{SAT}(b)}{2^{n}}\geq q\end{matrix}

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}\mathtt{Val}(\mathbf{D}^{q}A_{1})\\
\ \\
\ \end{matrix}

\displaystyle\ \begin{matrix}=&\texttt{let }b=\mathtt{Bool}(A_{1})\ \texttt{in}\\
&\texttt{let }n=\sharp\texttt{Var}(b)\ \texttt{in }\\
&\frac{\sharp\mathtt{SAT}(b)}{2^{n}}<q\end{matrix}

where
\sharp\texttt{Var}(b)
is the number of propositional variables in
b
.
We then immediately deduce:

Proposition 6
.

\textsf{{CPL}}_{0}
-validity is in
\textsf{P}^{\sharp\mathtt{SAT}}
.

Can we say anything about the
completeness
of
\textsf{{CPL}}_{0}
-validity?
Under Turing reductions, the latter is undoubtedly a complete problem
for
\textsf{P}^{\sharp\mathtt{SAT}}
, since an oracle for
\textsf{{CPL}}_{0}
-validity can, of course,
solve any instance of
\sharp\mathtt{SAT}
. Unfortunately, the same cannot be
said about many-one reductions.

4
The Multivariate Case

After having introduced the basic counting logic
\textsf{{CPL}}_{0}

and discussed its connections with the class
\textsf{P}^{\sharp\mathtt{SAT}}
,
let us now take into account a more expressive logic
CPL
,
that will be shown in Section
5
to yield
a characterization of the full counting hierarchy.

4.1
A Logic for Relative Counting Problems

As discussed in Section
2
, the counting problems
coming from
PL
are not restricted to those in the class
\textsf{P}^{\sharp\mathtt{SAT}}
.
Indeed, behind counting the models of a formula, one can consider
multivariate counting problems, such as
\mathtt{MajMajSAT}
, which
involves the relation between valuations of
different
groups of
variables.
In order to address this kind of problems in our logic, we need counting
quantifiers to relate to groups of variables.
To
this end we introduce a countable alphabet of
names
,

a,b,c,\dots
, so that any logical atom, such as
\mathbf{i}
,
becomes named,
\mathbf{i}_{a}
, and, equally,
counting quantifiers turn into named ones,

\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}A
or
\mathbf{D}^{q}_{a}A
.

Definition 4.1

(Grammar of
CPL
)
.

The
formulas
of
CPL
are defined by the grammar below:

A,B:=\ \mathbf{i}_{a}\;\;\mbox{\Large{$\mid$}}\;\;\lnot A\;\;\mbox{\Large{$\mid$}}\;\;A\land B\;\;\mbox{\Large{$\mid$}}\;\;A\lor B\;\;\mbox{\Large{$\mid$}}\;\;\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}A\;\;\mbox{\Large{$\mid$}}\;\;\mathbf{D}^{q}_{a}A

where
\mathbf{i}
is a natural number,
a
is a name,
and
q
is a rational
number included in the interval
[0,1]
.

The intuitive meaning of
named quantifiers
is that
they count models
relative
to the corresponding
bounded variables.
The name
a
is considered bound in the formulas

\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}A
and
\mathbf{D}^{q}_{a}A
.
Given a formula of
CPL
, call it
A
, we denote
the set of names which occur
free
in it,
\mathrm{FN}(A)
.

The semantics of these named formulas is slightly subtler
than that of
\textsf{{CPL}}_{0}
.
Since we must take names into account, the interpretation of
a formula
A
of
CPL
will no longer be defined as a
measurable set
\llbracket A\rrbracket\in\mathcal{B}(2^{\omega})
.
Instead, for all finite set of names
X\supseteq\mathrm{FN}(A)
,
we will define a measurable set
\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X}

belonging to the Borel algebra
\mathcal{B}((2^{\omega})^{X})
.
To describe the semantics of
CPL
we first need to introduce a
fundamental operation:

Definition 4.2

(
f
-projection)
.

Let
X,Y
be two disjoint finite sets of names and
f\in(2^{\omega})^{X}
. For all
\mathcal{X}\subseteq(2^{\omega})^{X\cup Y}
,
the
f
-projection of
\mathcal{X}
is the set:

\Pi_{f}(\mathcal{X})=\{g\in(2^{\omega})^{Y}\mid f+g\in\mathcal{X}\}\subseteq(2^{\omega})^{Y}

where
(f+g)(\alpha)
is
f(\alpha)
, if
\alpha\in X
and
g(\alpha)
if
\alpha\in Y
.

Suppose
X
and
Y
are disjoint sets of names,
with
\mathrm{FN}(A)\subseteq X\cup Y
.
Then, if we fix a valuation
f\in(2^{\omega})^{X}
of the
variables of
A
with names in
X
, the set

\Pi_{f}(\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X\cup Y})

describes the set of valuations of the variables of
A

with names in
Y
which extend
f
.
We can now define the set
\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X}
formally:

Definition 4.3

(Semantics of
CPL
)
.

For all formula
A
of
CPL
and finite set of names

X\supseteq\mathrm{FN}(A)
, the set
\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X}
is inductively defined as
follows:

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathbf{i}_{a}\rrbracket_{X}

\displaystyle=\{f\mid f(a)(i)=1\}

\displaystyle\llbracket\lnot A\rrbracket_{X}

\displaystyle=(2^{\omega})^{X}\ -\ \llbracket A\rrbracket

\displaystyle\llbracket A\land B\rrbracket_{X}

\displaystyle=\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X}\cap\llbracket B\rrbracket_{X}

\displaystyle\llbracket A\lor B\rrbracket_{X}

\displaystyle=\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X}\cup\llbracket B\rrbracket_{X}

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}A\rrbracket_{X}

\displaystyle=\{f\mid\mu(\Pi_{f}(\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}}))\geq q\}

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathbf{D}^{q}_{a}A\rrbracket_{X}

\displaystyle=\{f\mid\mu(\Pi_{f}(\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}}))<q\}

We will show below that
\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X}\in\mathcal{B}((2^{\omega})^{X})
, which is that the interpretation of a formula of

CPL
is always a measurable set.
To have a grasp of the semantics of named quantifiers, consider the following example:

Example 3
.

Let
A
be the following formula of
CPL
:

A=(\mathbf{0}_{a}\wedge(\neg\mathbf{0}_{b}\wedge\mathbf{1}_{b}))\vee(\neg\mathbf{0}_{a}\wedge\mathbf{0}_{b}\wedge\neg\mathbf{1}_{b})\vee((\neg\mathbf{0}_{a}\wedge\mathbf{1}_{a})\wedge\mathbf{1}_{b}).

The valuations
f\in(2^{\omega})^{\{b\}}

belonging to
\llbracket\mathbf{C}^{\frac{1}{2}}_{a}A\rrbracket_{\{b\}}

are those which can be extended to models
of
A
in at least
\frac{1}{2}
of the cases.
In such a simple situation, we can list all possible
cases:

1.

if
f(b)(0)=f(b)(1)=1
, then
A
has
\frac{1}{4}

chances of being true, since both
\neg\mathbf{0}_{a}
and

\mathbf{1}_{a}
must be true,

2.

if
f(b)(0)=1,f(b)(1)=0
, then
A
has
\frac{1}{2}
chances of being true, since
\neg\mathbf{0}_{a}
must be true

3.

if
f(b)(0)=0,f(b)(1)=1
, then
A

has
\frac{3}{4}
chances of being true, since either
\mathbf{0}_{a}
or both
\neg\mathbf{0}_{a}
and
\mathbf{1}_{a}
must be true

4.

if
f(b)(0)=f(b)(1)=0
, then
A
has 0 chances of being true.

Thus,
\llbracket\mathbf{C}^{\frac{1}{2}}_{a}A\rrbracket_{\{b\}}

only contains the valuations which agree
with cases 2. and 3.
Therefore,
\llbracket\mathbf{C}_{b}^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathbf{C}^{\frac{1}{2}}_{a}A\rrbracket_{\emptyset}=2^{\omega}
,
which is

\mathbf{C}^{\frac{1}{2}}_{b}\mathbf{C}^{\frac{1}{2}}_{a}A

is true, since half of the valuations
of
b
has at least
\frac{1}{2}
chances of being
extended to a model of
A
.

Let us now consider a slightly different case, being
A
the following formula:

A=\big(\mathbf{0}_{a}\wedge(\neg\mathbf{0}_{b}\wedge\mathbf{1}_{b})\big)\vee\big(\neg\mathbf{0}_{a}\wedge\mathbf{0}_{b}\wedge\neg\mathbf{1}_{b}\big)\vee\big((\neg\mathbf{0}_{a}\vee\mathbf{1}_{a})\wedge\mathbf{1}_{b}\big).

Cases 2. and 4. are equivalent, but 1. and 3. are not.
Indeed,

1.

if
f(b)(0)=f(b)(1)=1
, then
A
has
\frac{3}{4}

chances of being true, since either
\neg\mathbf{0}_{a}
or

\mathbf{1}_{a}
must be true.

2.

if
f(b)(0)=1,f(b)(1)=0
, then
A
has
\frac{1}{2}
chances of being true, since, as before,
\neg\mathbf{0}_{a}
must be true.

3.

if
f(b)(0)=0,f(b)(1)=1
, then
A
has 1 chances of being true, since either
\mathbf{0}_{a}
or either
\neg\mathbf{0}_{a}
or
\mathbf{1}_{a}
must be true.

4.

if
f(b)(0)=f(b)(1)=0
, then
A
has 0 chances of
being true.

In this case
\llbracket\mathbf{C}^{\frac{1}{2}}_{a}A\rrbracket_{\{b\}}

contains the valuations which agree with cases 1., 2., and 3. Thus, again,
\llbracket\mathbf{C}^{\frac{1}{2}}_{b}\mathbf{C}^{\frac{1}{2}}_{a}A\rrbracket_{\emptyset}=2^{\omega}
, but
also
\llbracket\mathbf{C}^{\frac{3}{4}}_{b}\mathbf{C}^{\frac{1}{2}}_{a}A\rrbracket_{\emptyset}=2^{\omega}
.

4.2
Characterizing the Semantics of
CPL
using Boolean Formulas

The definition of the sets
\llbracket\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}A\rrbracket_{X}
and

\llbracket\mathbf{D}^{q}_{a}A\rrbracket_{X}
is not very intuitive at first glance.
We now provide an alternative characterization of these sets
by means of
named
Boolean formulas,
and show that they are measurable.

Definition 4.4

(Named Boolean Formulas)
.

Named Boolean formulas
are defined by the following grammar:

b,c:=x_{i}^{a}\;\;\mbox{\Large{$\mid$}}\;\;\top\;\;\mbox{\Large{$\mid$}}\;\;\bot\;\;\mbox{\Large{$\mid$}}\;\;\lnot b\;\;\mbox{\Large{$\mid$}}\;\;b\land c\;\;\mbox{\Large{$\mid$}}\;\;b\vee c

The interpretation
\llbracket b\rrbracket_{X}
of a Boolean formula
b

with
\mathrm{FN}(b)\subseteq X
is defined in a straightforward way,
following Definition
4.3
.
6
6

6

For further details, see Definition
B.1
in Appendix
B.1
.

Definition 4.5

(
a
-decomposition)
.

Let
b
be a named Boolean formula with free names in

X\cup\{a\}
.
An
a
-decomposition of
b
is a Boolean
formula
c=\bigvee_{i=0}^{k-1}d_{i}\land e_{i}

such that:

•

\llbracket c\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}}=\llbracket b\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}}

•

\mathrm{FN}(d_{i})\subseteq\{a\}
and
\mathrm{FN}(e_{i})\subseteq X

•

if
i\neq j
, then
\llbracket e_{i}\rrbracket_{X}\cap\llbracket e_{j}\rrbracket_{X}=\emptyset
.

The following lemma can be proved by induction.

Lemma 7
.

Any named Boolean formula
b
with
\mathrm{FN}(b)\subseteq X\cup\{a\}
admits an
a
-decomposition in
X
.

It is worth observing that while an
a
-decomposition of
b

always exists, it needs not be feasible to find it, since this formula
can be of exponential length with respect to
b
.
Yet,
a
-decompositions can be used to show that the
interpretation of a quantified formula is a finite union of
measurable sets:

Lemma 8

(Fundamental Lemma)
.

Let
b
be a named Boolean formula with
\mathrm{FN}(b)\subseteq X\cup\{a\}
and let

c=\bigvee_{i=0}^{k-1}d_{i}\land e_{i}
be an

a
-decomposition of
b
. Then, for all
q\in[0,1]
,

\displaystyle\{f\in(2^{\omega})^{X}\mid\mu(\Pi_{f}(\llbracket b\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}}))\geq q\}

\displaystyle=\bigcup\{\llbracket e_{i}\rrbracket_{X}\mid\mu(\llbracket d_{i}\rrbracket_{\{a\}})\geq q\}

\displaystyle\{f\in(2^{\omega})^{X}\mid\mu(\Pi_{f}(\llbracket b\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}}))<q\}

\displaystyle=\bigcup\{\llbracket e_{i}\rrbracket_{X}\mid\mu(\llbracket d_{i}\rrbracket_{\{a\}})<q\}.

Proof.

We only prove the first equality, the second one being proved in a
similar way.
First note that if
r=0
, then both sets are equal to

(2^{\omega})^{X}
, so we can suppose
r>0
.

(
\subseteq
)

Suppose
\mu(\Pi_{f}(\llbracket b\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}}))\geq r
. Then
\Pi_{f}(\llbracket b\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})
is non-empty and from
b\equiv\bigvee_{i}^{k}d_{i}\land e_{i}

we deduce that there exists
i\leq k
such that

f\in\llbracket e_{i}\rrbracket_{X}
and
for all
g\in\llbracket d_{i}\rrbracket_{\{a\}}
,
f+g\in\llbracket d_{i}\land e_{i}\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}}
.
This implies then that
\llbracket d_{i}\rrbracket_{\{a\}}\subseteq\Pi_{f}(\llbracket b\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})
.
Moreover, since the sets
\llbracket e_{i}\rrbracket_{X}
are pairwise disjoint, for all
j\neq i
,
f\notin\llbracket e_{j}\rrbracket_{X}
, which implies that

\Pi_{f}(\llbracket b\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})\subseteq\llbracket d_{i}\rrbracket_{\{a\}}
.
Hence

\Pi_{f}(\llbracket b\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})=\llbracket d_{i}\rrbracket_{\{a\}}
, which implies

\mu(\llbracket d_{i}\rrbracket_{\{a\}})\geq r
.

(
\supseteq
)

If
f\in\llbracket e_{i}\rrbracket_{X}
, where

\mu(\llbracket d_{i}\rrbracket_{\{a\}})\geq r
, then since
d_{i}\land e_{i}\vDash^{X\cup\{a\}}b
,

\mu\big(\Pi_{f}(\llbracket b\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})\big)

\geq

\mu\big(\Pi_{f}(\llbracket d_{i}\land e_{i}\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})\big)=\mu(\llbracket d_{i}\rrbracket_{\{a\}})\geq r
.

∎

The Lemma
8
above can be used
to associate with any formula
A

(with
\mathrm{FN}(A)\subseteq X
) a Boolean formula

b_{A}
, such that

\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X}=\llbracket b_{A}\rrbracket_{X}
.
The crucial steps of

\llbracket\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}A\rrbracket_{X}
and

\llbracket\mathbf{D}^{q}_{a}A\rrbracket_{X}
are
handled using the fact that these sets are finite unions of measurable
sets of the form
\llbracket e_{i}\rrbracket_{X}
, where

\bigvee_{i=0}^{k-1}d_{i}\land e_{i}
is an

a
-decomposition of
b_{A}
.
This shows in particular that
all sets
\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X}
are measurable.

4.3
The Proof Theory of
CPL

Using the Fundamental Lemma, it is not difficult to devise a
complete proof system for
CPL
,
in analogy with the one defined for
\textsf{{CPL}}_{0}
.
A
named sequent
is an expression of the form

\vdash^{X}L
, where
L
is a labelled formula,

b\rightarrowtail A,b\leftarrowtail A
, such that

\mathrm{FN}(b)\cup\mathrm{FN}(A)\subseteq X
.
Most of the rules of the proof system for
CPL

are straightforward extensions of those of
\textsf{{CPL}}_{0}
.
We illustrate in
Fig.
3
the initial sequents and the counting rules.
These are the only rules which are substantially different
with respect to the corresponding
\textsf{{CPL}}_{0}
ones.
7
7

7

For the complete proof system for
CPL
, see Definition
B.5

in Appendix
B.2
.

Initial Sequents

\vDash a\in X

b\vDash^{X}x_{i}^{a}

\mathsf{Ax1}

\vdash^{X}b\rightarrowtail\mathbf{i}_{a}

\vDash a\in X

x_{i}^{a}\vDash^{X}b

\mathsf{Ax2}

\vdash^{X}b\leftarrowtail\mathbf{i}_{a}

Counting Rules

\mu(\llbracket b\rrbracket_{X})=0

\mathsf{R_{\mu}^{\rightarrowtail}}

\vdash^{X}b\rightarrowtail A

\mu(\llbracket b\rrbracket_{X})=1

\mathsf{R_{\mu}^{\leftarrowtail}}

\vdash^{X}b\leftarrowtail A

\vdash^{X\cup\{a\}}c\rightarrowtail A

b\vDash^{X}\bigvee_{i}\{e_{i}\ |\ \mu(\llbracket d_{i}\rrbracket_{\{a\}})\geq q\}

\mathsf{R}_{\mathbf{C}}^{\rightarrowtail}

\vdash^{X}b\rightarrowtail\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}A

\vdash^{X\cup\{a\}}c\leftarrowtail A

\bigvee_{i}\{e_{i}\ |\ \mu(\llbracket d_{i}\rrbracket_{\{a\}})\geq q\}\vDash^{X}b

\mathsf{R}_{\mathbf{C}}^{\leftarrowtail}

\vdash^{X}b\leftarrowtail\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}A

\vdash^{X\cup\{a\}}c\leftarrowtail A

b\vDash^{X}\bigvee_{i}\{e_{i}\ |\ \mu(\llbracket d_{i}\rrbracket_{\{a\}})<q\}

\mathsf{R}_{\mathbf{D}}^{\rightarrowtail}

\vdash^{X}b\rightarrowtail\mathbf{D}^{q}_{a}A

\vdash^{X\cup\{a\}}c\rightarrowtail A

\bigvee_{i}\{e_{i}\ |\ \mu(\llbracket d_{i}\rrbracket_{\{a\}})<q\}\vDash^{X}b

\mathsf{R}_{\mathbf{D}}^{\leftarrowtail}

\vdash^{X}b\leftarrowtail\mathbf{D}^{q}_{a}A

(where
\bigvee_{i}e_{i}\wedge d_{i}
is an
a
-decomposition of
c
)

Figure 3
:
CPL
Initial Sequents and Counting Rules.

Similarly to
\textsf{{CPL}}_{0}
, given a named Boolean formula
b
,
a formula of
CPL

A
, and a set
X
,
such that
\mathrm{FN}(b)\cup\mathrm{FN}(A)\subseteq X
,
the labelled formula
b\rightarrowtail A

(resp.
b\leftarrowtail A
) is
X
-valid
when

\llbracket b\rrbracket_{X}\subseteq\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X}
(resp.

\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X}\subseteq\llbracket b\rrbracket_{X}
).
A named sequent
\vdash^{X}L
is
valid

(denoted
\vDash^{X}L
) when
L
is
X
-valid.
We write
A\equiv_{X}B

when
\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X}=\llbracket B\rrbracket_{X}
.
The soundness of the proof system can be again
proved by standard induction.
Completeness is established with an argument very similar to that for
\textsf{{CPL}}_{0}
.
8
8

8

For further details, see Appendix
B.2
. Specifically,
soundness is proved as Theorem
29

and completeness as Theorem
36
.

Theorem 9

(Soundness and Completeness)
.

\vdash_{\textsf{{CPL}}}^{X}L
holds if and only if
\vDash^{X}L
holds.

From completeness one can deduce the admissibility of a cut-rule defined as in Remark
2
.

5
Relating to the Counting Hierarchy

In his seminal works
[
66
,
67
,
68
]
,
Wagner observed that one can define canonical complete
problems for the each level of counting hierarchy,
in the spirit of similar results for the
polynomial hierarchy
[
38
,
39
,
59
,
69
,
11
]
.
Nevertheless, there are some crucial differences.
First of all, satisfiability should be checked
against
bunches
of variables,
rather than on
one
variable.
Moreover, the verifier is allowed to require the
number of models to be higher than a certain threshold,
rather than merely being minimal or maximal.
The resulting operators on classes of languages are
called
counting quantifiers
.

In some sense,
CPL
can be seen as a way to internalize
Wagner’s construction inside a proper logical system.
Counting quantifiers, however, can be used deep inside formulas,
rather than just at top-level.
Does all this make us lose the connection to the counting hierarchy?
The answer to the question is negative and is the topic of
this section.
More specifically, formulas can be efficiently put in prenex form,
as we prove in Section
5.1
below.
Moreover, there is a way of getting rid of the
\mathbf{D}
quantifier,
which has no counterpart in Wagner’s problems,
as shown in Section
5.2
.

5.1
Prenex Normal Forms

The prenex normal form for formulas of
CPL
is defined in
a standard way:

Definition 5.1

(PNF)
.

A formula of
CPL
is an

n
-ary prenex normal form

(or simply a
prenex normal form
, PNF for short)
if it can be written as
\triangle_{1}\ldots\triangle_{n}A
where,
for every
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
,
the operator
\triangle_{i}
is either in the form
\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}
or

\mathbf{D}^{q}_{a}
(for arbitrary
a,
and
q
),
and
A
does not contain any counting operator.
In this case, the formula
A
is said
to be the
matrix
of the PNF.

To convert a formula of
CPL
into an equivalent
formula in
in prenex normal form
,
some preliminary lemmas are needed.
First, it is worth noticing that for every formula of
CPL

A
,
name
a
, and finite set
X
,
with
\mathrm{FN}(A)\subseteq X
and
a\not\in X
,
if
q
= 0, then

\llbracket\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}A\rrbracket_{X}
= (
2^{\omega}
)
X

and
\llbracket\mathbf{D}^{q}_{a}A\rrbracket_{X}
=
\emptyset^{X}
.
9
9

9

For the proof, see Lemma
37
in Appendix
C.1
.

The following lemma states that counting quantifiers occurring
inside any conjunction or disjunction can somehow be extruded from it.
10
10

10

For further details, see the corresponding Lemma
10
, in Appendix
C.1
.

Lemma 10
.

Let
a\not\in\mathrm{FN}(A)
and
q>0
.
Then, for every
X
such that

\mathrm{FN}(A)\cup\mathrm{FN}(B)\subseteq X
, and
a\not\in X
,
the followings hold:

\displaystyle A\wedge\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}B

\displaystyle\equiv_{X}\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}(A\wedge B)

\displaystyle A\vee\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}B

\displaystyle\equiv_{X}\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}(A\vee B)

\displaystyle A\wedge\mathbf{D}^{q}_{a}B

\displaystyle\equiv_{X}\mathbf{D}^{q}_{a}(\neg A\vee B)

\displaystyle A\vee\mathbf{D}^{q}_{a}B

\displaystyle\equiv_{X}\mathbf{D}_{a}^{q}(\neg A\wedge B).

Remarkably, a similar lemma does
not
hold for
\textsf{{CPL}}_{0}
.

What about negation? Equation (
3
)
can be generalized to
CPL
, in order to get rid of negations,
which lie between any occurrence of a counting quantifier
and the formula’s root.
11
11

11

For the proof, see the corresponding Lemma
10
, in Appendix
C.1
.

Lemma 11
.

For every
q\in\mathbb{Q}_{[0,1]}
, name
a
, and
X

such that
\mathrm{FN}(A)\subseteq X\cup\{a\}
and
a\not\in X
, then:

\lnot\mathbf{D}^{q}_{a}A\equiv_{X}\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}A\qquad\qquad\lnot\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}A\equiv_{X}\mathbf{D}^{q}_{a}A.

Please notice that
\mathbf{C}
and
\mathbf{D}
, contrarily to standard

\forall
and
\exists
, are
not
dual operators.

As the equivalences from Lemma
10

and Lemma
11

can be oriented, see Appendix
C.1
,
we conclude the following:

Proposition 12
.

For every formula of
CPL
,
A
, there is a PNF
B

such that, for every
X
, with
\mathrm{FN}(A)

\cup

\mathrm{FN}(B)\subseteq X
:

A\equiv_{X}B.

Moreover,
B
can be computed
in polynomial time from
A
.

5.2
Positive Prenex Normal Form

Prenex normal forms for
CPL
are already very close
to what we need,
but there is one last step to make,
namely getting rid of the
\mathbf{D}
operator, which does not
have any counterpart in Wagner’s construction.
In other words, we need prenex normal forms
of a
special kind
.

Definition 5.2

(PPNF)
.

A formula of
CPL
is said to be in
positive prenex normal form
(PPNF, for short) if it is in PNF and no
\mathbf{D}
-quantifier
occurs in it.

How could we get rid of
\mathbf{D}
? The idea is to turn any instance of

\mathbf{D}
into one of
\mathbf{C}
by applying the second equality from Lemma
11
,
and then to use the fact that
\mathbf{C}
enjoys a weak form of
self duality, as shown by the following lemma:

Lemma 13

(Epsilon Lemma)
.

For every formula of
CPL
, call it
A
,
and for every
q\in\mathbb{Q}_{[0,1]}
,
there is a
p\in\mathbb{Q}_{[0,1]}
such that,
for every
X
, with
\mathrm{FN}(A)\subseteq X
and

a\not\in X
, the following holds:

\neg\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}A\equiv_{X}\mathbf{C}^{p}_{a}\neg A.

Moreover,
p
can be computed from
q
in polynomial
time.

Proof Sketch of Lemma
13
.

To establish Lemma
13
we need a few preliminary lemmas which we will here assumed to be already established.
12
12

12

The detailed proof of Lemma
13
and of all the auxiliary lemmas can be found in Appendix
C.2
.

Let
b_{A}
be a Boolean formula satisfying
\llbracket b\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}}=\llbracket b_{A}\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}}
. Let
b_{A}
be
a
-decomposable as
\bigvee_{i}^{n}d_{i}\land e_{i}
and let
k
be maximum such that
x_{k}^{a}
occurs in
b_{A}
.
Let
[0,1]_{k}
be the set of those rational numbers
r\in[0,1]
which can be written as a finite sum of the form
\sum_{i=0}^{k}b_{i}\cdot 2^{–i}
.
For all
i=0,\dots,n
,
\mu(\llbracket d_{i}\rrbracket_{\{a\}})\in[0,1]_{k}
where
b_{i}\in\{0,1\}
(see Lemma
44
in Appendix
C.2
).
Hence, for all
f:X\to 2^{\omega}
, also
\mu\big(\Pi_{f}(\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})\big)\in[0,1]_{k}
, since
\Pi_{f}\big(\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})\big)
coincides with the unique
\llbracket d_{i}\rrbracket_{\{a\}}
such that
f\in\llbracket e_{i}\rrbracket_{X}
, by Lemma
8
.
Let now
\epsilon
be
2^{–(k+1)}
if
q\in[0,1]_{k}
and
q\neq 1
, let
\epsilon
be
–2^{–(k+1)}
if
q=1
and let
\epsilon=0
if
q\notin[0,1]_{k}
. In all cases
q+\epsilon\notin[0,1]_{k}
so, by means of some auxiliary results (specifically by Lemma
46
and Lemma
45
),
it is possible to conclude:

\llbracket\neg\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}A\rrbracket_{X}=\llbracket\mathbf{C}^{1–(q+\epsilon)}_{a}\neg A\rrbracket_{X}.

∎

Actually, the value of
p
is very close to
1-q
, the difference between
the two being easily computable from the formula
A
.
Hence, any negation occurring in the counting prefix of a
PNF can be pushed back into
the formula.
Summing up:

Proposition 14
.

For every formula of
CPL
, call it
A
, there is a PPNF
B

such that for every
X
, with
\mathrm{FN}(A)\cup\mathrm{FN}(B)\subseteq X
,

A\equiv_{X}B.

Moreover,
B
can be computed
from
A
in polynomial time.

5.3
CPL
and the Counting Hierarchy

In
[
67
]
, Wagner not only introduced counting quantifiers
(as operators on classes of languages) and hierarchy,
but he also presented complete problems for each level of
\mathsf{CH}
.
Below, we state a slightly weaker version of Wagner’s Theorem,
which however perfectly fits our needs.
For the sake of keeping our exposition self-contained,
we also introduce preliminary definitions,
despite their being standard.

Assume
\mathcal{L}
is a subset of
S^{n}
, where
S
is a set.
Now, suppose that
1\leq m<n
, and
b\in\mathbb{N}
.
We thus define
\mathbf{C}_{m}^{b}\mathcal{L}
as the following subset
of
S^{n-m}
:

\left\{(a_{n},\ldots,a_{m+1})\mid\#(\{(a_{m},\ldots,a_{1})\mid(a_{n},\ldots,a_{1})\in\mathcal{L}\})\geq b\right\}.

Now, take
PL
as a set comprising the language of propositional logic
formulas, including the propositional constants
\mathbf{T}
and
\mathbf{F}
.
For any natural number
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\mathcal{TF}^{n}
be the
subset of
\textsf{{PL}}^{n+1}
containing all tuples
in the form
(A,t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})
, where
A
is a propositional
formula in CNF with at most
n
free variables, and
t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}\in\{\mathbf{T},\mathbf{F}\}
, which
render
A
true. Finally, for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we indicate as
\mathit{W}^{k}

the language consisting of all (binary encodings of) tuples
in the form
(A,m_{1},\ldots,m_{k},b_{1},\ldots,b_{k})
and such that

A\in\mathbf{C}_{m_{1}}^{b_{1}}\cdots\mathbf{C}_{m_{k}}^{b_{k}}\mathcal{TF}^{\sum m_{i}}
.

Theorem 15

(Wagner, Th. 7
[
67
]
)
.

For every
k
, the language
W^{k}
is complete for
\mathsf{CH}_{k}
.

Please notice that the elements of
W^{k}
can be seen as
alternative representations for
CPL
’s PPNFs,
once any
m_{i}
is replaced by
\min\{1,\frac{m_{i}}{2^{b_{i}}}\}
.
As a consequence:

Corollary 1
.

The closed and valid
k
-ary PPNFs, whose matrix is in CNF,
define a complete set for
\mathsf{CH}_{k}
.

6
Type Systems from Counting Propositional Logic

6.1
Counting Proofs as Programs

As is well known, the so-called Curry-Howard correspondence relates proofs with typable programs.
When this correspondence holds, any provable sequent

\vdash A

can be replaced with a type judgment

\vdash t:A
,
where
t
is a program encoding the computational content of some proof of
\vdash A
.
Now, what is the computational content of the proofs of the system
CPL
?
A natural way to interpret such proofs is as programs depending on some
probabilistic event
: if
\vdash b\rightarrowtail A
is provable in, say,
\textsf{{CPL}}_{0}
, then a program
\vdash t:b\rightarrowtail A
should be one depending on some event
f\in 2^{\omega}
and such that, whenever
f
satisfies
b
, then
t
yields a proof of
A
.

In the literature on higher-order probabilistic type systems, one usually considers the extension of the standard
\lambda
-calculus with a probabilistic choice operator
t\oplus u
, which reduces to either
t
or
u
with probability
\frac{1}{2}
.
However, this extension comes with the undesired consequence the confluence property is lost.
This is due to the basic observation that probabilistic choice does not commute with duplication: should two copies of
t\oplus u
follow the
same
probabilistic choice or behave as
independent
choices?
A solution to this problem, proposed in
[
15
]
, is to decompose the operator
\oplus
into a
generator

\nu a.t
and a
choice operator

\oplus_{a}
, so that

t\oplus u=\nu a.t\oplus_{a}u.

In this way one obtains a confluent system, in which two copies of
t\oplus_{a}u
behave following the same choice, while two copies of

\nu a.t\oplus_{a}u
behave following independent choices.

This decomposition of the probabilistic choice operator yields a surprising bridge with counting propositional logic.
In fact, suppose
b
is true in at least half of its models (i.e.
\mu(\llbracket b\rrbracket)\geq\frac{1}{2}
). As we said, a proof of
\vdash b\rightarrowtail A
should correspond to a program depending on the choice of a valuation
f
for
b
, that we can think of as
t\oplus_{a}u
.
Then, the proof of
\vdash c\rightarrowtail\mathbf{C}^{1/2}A
, that we obtain using the rules for the counting quantifier corresponds to a program which
no more depends
on
f
, that we can think of as
\nu a.t\oplus_{a}u
. In other words, it makes perfect sense to consider the following typing rule:

\vdash t:b\rightarrowtail A

\mu(\llbracket b\rrbracket)\geq q

\vdash\nu a.t:c\rightarrowtail\mathbf{C}^{q}A

This idea leads then to look for a suitable type system for a probabilistic
\lambda
-calculus in the style of
[
15
]
, which somehow reflects the structure of counting propositional logic.
This is what we are going to present below.

6.2
The Type System
\lambda\textsf{{CPL}}

We introduce a probabilistic type system
\lambda\textsf{{CPL}}
with counting quantifiers for a
\lambda
-calculus enriched with generators and choice operators as in
[
15
]
.
The set
\Lambda_{\nu}
of
\lambda
-terms with
generators
and
choice
operators is defined by the grammar:

t,u:=x\;\;\mbox{\Large{$\mid$}}\;\;\lambda x.t\;\;\mbox{\Large{$\mid$}}\;\;tu\;\;\mbox{\Large{$\mid$}}\;\;t\oplus^{i}_{a}u\;\;\mbox{\Large{$\mid$}}\;\;\nu a.t.

Note that the choice operator
t\oplus_{a}^{i}u
is indexed by both a name and an index
i\in\mathbb{N}
, since, as for
CPL
, we take the Borel algebra
\mathcal{B}(2^{\omega})
as the underlying event space.
We let
\mathrm{FN}(t)
indicate the set of free names of
t
.
A term
t
is
name-closed
when
\mathrm{FN}(t)=\emptyset
.
If
\mathrm{FN}(t)\subseteq X
, then for all
f\in(2^{\omega})^{X}
, we let the
application of
f
to
t
be the name-closed term
\pi^{f}(t)
defined by induction as follows:

\displaystyle\pi^{f}(x)

\displaystyle=x

\displaystyle\pi^{f}(\lambda x.t)

\displaystyle=\lambda x.\pi^{f}(t)

\displaystyle\pi^{f}(tu)

\displaystyle=\pi^{f}(t)\pi^{f}(u)

\displaystyle\pi^{f}(t_{0}\oplus^{i}_{a}t_{1})

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\pi^{f}(t_{f(i)})&\text{ if }a\in X\\
\pi^{f}(t_{0})\oplus^{i}_{a}\pi^{f}(t_{1})&\text{ if }a\notin X\end{cases}

\displaystyle\pi^{f}(\nu a.t)

\displaystyle=\nu a.\pi^{f}(t).

\lambda
-terms are equipped with a reduction
\rightarrowtriangle
generated by usual
\beta
-reduction,
\to_{\beta}
, and
permutative reduction
,
\to_{\mathsf{p}}
, illustrated in Fig.
4
, where for a term
t
, a name
a
, and an index
i
,
(a,i)<(b,j)
holds when either
a\neq b
and
b
is bound within the scope of
a
, or
a=b
and
i<j
.
Let
\mathcal{HN}\subseteq\mathcal{V}
be the set of pseudo-values which are in
head normal form
, that is, which are of the form
\lambda x_{1}\dots\lambda x_{n}.yu_{1}\dots u_{n}
.

\displaystyle t\oplus_{a}^{i}t

\displaystyle\to_{\mathsf{p}}t

\displaystyle(t\oplus_{a}^{i}u)\oplus_{a}^{i}v

\displaystyle\to_{\mathsf{p}}t\oplus_{a}^{i}v

\displaystyle t\oplus_{a}^{i}(u\oplus_{a}^{i}v)

\displaystyle\to_{\mathsf{p}}t\oplus_{a}^{i}v

\displaystyle\lambda x.(t\oplus_{a}^{i}u)

\displaystyle\to_{\mathsf{p}}(\lambda x.t)\oplus_{a}^{i}(\lambda x.u)

\displaystyle(t\oplus_{a}^{i}u)v

\displaystyle\to_{\mathsf{p}}(tu)\oplus_{a}^{i}(uv)

\displaystyle t(u\oplus_{a}^{i}v)

\displaystyle\to_{\mathsf{p}}(tu)\oplus_{a}^{i}(tv)

\displaystyle(t\oplus_{a}^{i}u)\oplus_{b}^{j}v

\displaystyle\to_{\mathsf{p}}(t\oplus_{b}^{j}v)\oplus_{a}^{i}(u\oplus_{b}^{j}v)

\displaystyle((a,i)<(b,j))

\displaystyle t\oplus_{b}^{j}(u\oplus_{a}^{i}v)

\displaystyle\to_{\mathsf{p}}(t\oplus_{b}^{j}u)\oplus_{a}^{i}(t\oplus_{b}^{j}v)

\displaystyle((a,i)<(b,j))

\displaystyle\nu b.(t\oplus_{a}^{i}u)

\displaystyle\to_{\mathsf{p}}(\nu b.t)\oplus_{a}^{i}(\nu b.u)

\displaystyle(a\neq b)

\displaystyle\nu a.t

\displaystyle\to_{\mathsf{p}}t

\displaystyle(a\notin\mathrm{FN}(t))

\displaystyle\lambda x.\nu a.t

\displaystyle\to_{\mathsf{p}}\nu a.\lambda x.t

\displaystyle(\nu a.t)u

\displaystyle\to_{\mathsf{p}}\nu a.(tu)

Figure 4
:
Permutative reductions of
\Lambda_{\nu}
-terms.

The following facts can be proved as in
[
15
]
.

Proposition 16
.

The reduction relations
\to_{\beta},\to_{\mathsf{p}},
and
\rightarrowtriangle
are all confluent.
Moreover,
\to_{\mathsf{p}}
is strongly normalizing.

Remark 3
.

The
\lambda
-calculus in
[
15
]
slightly differs from the present one, since choice operators do not depend on indexes
i\in\mathbb{N}
. However, one can define a bijective translation between the two languages, so that the permutative reductions in
[
15
]
translate into those in Fig.
4
.

A consequence of Proposition
16
is that any term admits a (unique)
permutative normal form
(
{PNF}
, for short). We let
\mathcal{T}
indicate the set of
{PNF}
. As shown in detail in Appendix
D
, a name-closed term
t\in\mathcal{T}
can be of two types:

•

a
pseudo-value
, that is a variable, a
\lambda
or an application (we let
\mathcal{V}\subseteq\mathcal{T}
indicate the set of pseudo-values)

•

a term
\nu a.t
, where
t
is a tree whose leaves form a finite subset of
\mathcal{T}
(the
support
of
t
,
\mathrm{supp}(t)
) and formed by concatenating sums of the form
t\oplus_{a}^{i}u
(see Def.
D.1
).

Using this decomposition, any
{PNF}

t\in\mathcal{T}
can be associated with a
distribution
of pseudo-values
\mathcal{D}_{t}:\mathcal{V}\to[0,1]
by letting
\mathcal{D}_{t}(v)=\delta_{t}
, when
t\in\mathcal{V}
, and otherwise

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{t}(v)

\displaystyle=\sum_{t^{\prime}\in\mathrm{supp}(t)}\mathcal{D}_{t^{\prime}}(v)\cdot\mu\big(\{f\in 2^{\omega}\mid\pi^{f}(t)=t^{\prime}\}\big).

The real number
\mathcal{D}_{t}(v)\in[0,1]
can be interpreted as the probability that the term
v
is found by applying to
t
a chain of random choices.

We now introduce
simple types with counting

\sigma,\tau
by the following grammar:

\displaystyle\sigma,\tau:=o\mid\mathfrak{s}\Rightarrow\sigma\qquad\qquad\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{t}:=\mathbf{C}^{q}\sigma.

A
type context

\Gamma
is a finite set of declarations of the form
x:\mathfrak{s}
, with the
x
pairwise distinct.
A
labelled type
is an expression of the form
b\rightarrowtail\sigma
, where
b
is a Boolean formula.
A
type judgement
is of the form

\Gamma\vdash^{X,q}t:b\rightarrowtail\sigma

where the free names in both
t
and
b
are included in
X
, and
q
is a rational number in
[0,1]
.
The rules of the system
\lambda\textsf{{CPL}}
are illustrated in Fig.
5
, where for a Boolean formula
b
with
\mathrm{FN}(b)\subseteq X\cup\{a\}
(with
a\notin X
), a
weak
a
-decomposition
of
b
is any Boolean formula
\bigvee_{i}^{k}c_{i}\land d_{i}
equivalent to
b
, such that
\mathrm{FN}(c_{i})\subseteq\{a\}
,
\mathrm{FN}(d_{i})\subseteq X
, and the
c_{i}
are satisfiable.

Initial Rules

\mathrm{FV}(t)\subseteq\Gamma,\mathrm{FN}(t)\subseteq X

\mathsf{R}_{\bot}

\Gamma\vdash^{X,r}t:\bot\rightarrowtail\sigma

\mathrm{FN}(b)\subseteq X

\mathsf{R}_{\mathrm{id}}

\Gamma,x:\mathbf{C}^{q}\sigma\vdash^{X,q\cdot s}x:b\rightarrowtail\sigma

Union Rule

\Gamma\vdash^{X,r}t:c\rightarrowtail\sigma

\Gamma\vdash^{X,r}t:d\rightarrowtail\sigma

b\vDash^{X}c\vee d

\mathsf{R}_{\cup}

\Gamma\vdash^{X,r}t:b\rightarrowtail\sigma

Abstraction/Application Rules

\Gamma,x:\mathfrak{s}\vdash^{X,q}t:b\rightarrowtail\sigma

\mathsf{R}_{\lambda}

\Gamma\vdash^{X,q}\lambda x.t:b\rightarrowtail\mathfrak{s}\Rightarrow\sigma

\Gamma\vdash^{X,q}t:c\rightarrowtail\mathbf{C}^{r}\sigma\Rightarrow\tau

\Gamma\vdash^{X,r}u:d\rightarrowtail\sigma

b\vDash c\land d

\mathsf{R}_{@}

\Gamma\vdash^{X,q}tu:b\rightarrowtail\tau

Choice Rules

\Gamma\vdash^{X\cup\{a\},q}t:c\rightarrowtail\sigma

b\vDash x_{i}^{a}\land c

\mathsf{R}_{\oplus}^{\mathrm{l}}

\Gamma\vdash^{X\cup\{a\},q}t\oplus^{i}_{a}u:b\rightarrowtail\sigma

\Gamma\vdash^{X\cup\{a\},q}u:c\rightarrowtail\sigma

b\vDash\lnot x_{i}^{a}\land c

\mathsf{R}_{\oplus}^{\mathrm{r}}

\Gamma\vdash^{X\cup\{a\},q}t\oplus^{i}_{a}u:b\rightarrowtail\sigma

Generator Rule

\Gamma\vdash^{X\cup\{a\},q}t:c\rightarrowtail\sigma

\left(\mu(\llbracket c_{i}\rrbracket_{\{a\}})\geq r\right)_{i\leq k}

b\vDash\bigvee_{i}^{k}d_{i}

\mathsf{R}_{\nu}

\Gamma\vdash^{X,q\cdot r}\nu a.t:b\rightarrowtail\sigma

(where
\bigvee_{i}c_{i}\land d_{i}
is a weak
a
-decomposition of
c
)

Figure 5
:
Typing rules of
\lambda\textsf{{CPL}}
.

The following standard property is proved in detail in Appendix
D
.

Proposition 17
.

If
\Gamma\vdash^{X,r}t:b\rightarrowtail\sigma
and
t\rightarrowtriangle u
, then

\Gamma\vdash^{X,r}u:b\rightarrowtail\sigma
.

Example 4
.

The term
t=\nu a.(I\oplus_{a}^{0}\Omega)(I\oplus_{a}^{0}\Omega)
is typable “with probability
\frac{1}{2}
”, where
I=\lambda x.x
, reduces to a normal form with
\mathbf{P}\geq\frac{1}{2}
.
In fact, for all types
\sigma
and
q\in[0,1]
, we can prove
\vdash^{\{a\},q}I\oplus^{0}_{a}\Omega:x_{0}^{a}\rightarrowtail(\mathbf{C}^{q}\sigma)\Rightarrow\sigma
(Fig.
6(a)
).
Then, we can use this
to deduce
\vdash^{\frac{1}{2}}t:\top\rightarrowtail\rho
(Fig.
6(b)
, where
\rho=(\mathbf{C}^{q}o)\Rightarrow o
).
Instead, the term
t=\big(\nu a.(I\oplus_{a}^{0}\Omega)\big)\big(\nu a.(I\oplus_{a}^{0}\Omega)\big)
is typable “with probability
\frac{1}{4}
” (Fig.
6(c)
).

\pi_{\sigma,a,q}\ =\ \ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\lower 36.76811pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 17.69524pt\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 12.6389pt\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 21.42009pt\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\mathrm{FN}(\bot)\subseteq\{a\}$}\hskip 4.0pt}}\vskip-1.72035pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to106.26503pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower 2.22035pt\hbox{\hskip 3.0pt$\mathsf{R}_{\mathrm{id}}$}}\vskip-1.72035pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$x:\mathbf{C}^{q}\sigma\vdash^{\{a\},q}x:\top\rightarrowtail\sigma$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}\vskip-1.72035pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to131.54283pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower 2.22035pt\hbox{\hskip 3.0pt$\mathsf{R}_{\lambda}$}}\vskip-1.72035pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\vdash^{\{a\},q}\lambda x.x:\top\rightarrowtail(\mathbf{C}^{q}\sigma)\Rightarrow\sigma$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}\vskip-2.90982pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to166.9333pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower 2.57646pt\hbox{\hskip 3.0pt$\mathsf{R}_{\oplus}^{\mathrm{l}}$}}\vskip-2.9098pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\vdash^{\{a\},q}(\lambda x.x)\oplus^{0}_{a}\Omega:x_{0}^{a}\rightarrowtail(\mathbf{C}^{q}\sigma)\Rightarrow\sigma$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}\ignorespaces

(a)

\pi_{\rho,a,1}

\vdash^{\{a\},1}I\oplus^{0}_{a}\Omega:x_{0}^{a}\rightarrowtail(\mathbf{C}^{1}\rho)\Rightarrow\rho

\pi_{o,a,1}

\vdash^{\{a\},1}I\oplus^{0}_{a}\Omega:x_{0}^{a}\rightarrowtail\rho

\mathsf{R}_{@}

\vdash^{\{a\},1}(I\oplus^{0}_{a}\Omega)(I\oplus^{0}_{a}\Omega):x_{0}^{a}\rightarrowtail\rho

\mu(\llbracket x_{0}^{a}\rrbracket_{\{a\}})\geq 1/2

\mathsf{R}_{\nu}

\vdash^{\emptyset,1/2}\nu a.(I\oplus^{0}_{a}\Omega)(I\oplus^{0}_{a}\Omega):\top\rightarrowtail\rho

(b)

\pi_{\rho,a,1/2}

\vdash^{\{a\},1/2}I\oplus^{0}_{a}\Omega:x_{0}^{a}\rightarrowtail(\mathbf{C}^{1/2}\rho)\Rightarrow\rho

\mu(\llbracket x_{0}^{a}\rrbracket_{\{a\}})\geq 1/2

\mathsf{R}_{\nu}

\vdash^{\emptyset,1/4}\nu b.(I\oplus^{0}_{b}\Omega):\top\rightarrowtail(\mathbf{C}^{1/2}\rho)\Rightarrow\rho

\pi_{o,a,1}

\vdash^{\{a\},1}I\oplus^{0}_{a}\Omega:x_{0}^{a}\rightarrowtail\rho

\mu(\llbracket x_{0}^{a}\rrbracket_{\{a\}})\geq 1/2

\mathsf{R}_{\nu}

\vdash^{\emptyset,1/2}\nu a.(I\oplus^{0}_{a}\Omega):\top\rightarrowtail\rho

\mathsf{R}_{@}

\vdash^{\emptyset,1/4}\nu a.(I\oplus^{0}_{a}\Omega)(\nu a.(I\oplus^{0}_{a}\Omega)):\top\rightarrowtail\rho

(c)

Figure 6
:
Type derivations in
\lambda\textsf{{CPL}}
(for readability we omit some external hypotheses, as they can be guessed from the context.)

Observe that in
\lambda\textsf{{CPL}}
one can type terms which are
not
normalizing. For instance, we can prove

\vdash^{\emptyset,1}\Omega:\bot\rightarrowtail\sigma
and

\vdash^{\emptyset,1}\lambda x.x\Omega:\top\rightarrowtail\sigma
,
for any type
\sigma
,
where

\Omega=(\lambda x.xx)\lambda x.xx
.
This shows that our type system provides a rather different kind of information with respect to the one in
[
15
]
, which ensures strong normalization.
In fact, a typing judgement

\Gamma\vdash^{X,r}t:b\rightarrowtail\sigma
should be interpreted as some quantitative statement about the
probability
that
t
reaches a normal form. But how can we define this probability, precisely?

In usual randomized
\lambda
-calculi, program execution is defined so as to be inherently probabilistic: for example a term
t\oplus u
can reduce to either
t
or
u
, with probability
\frac{1}{2}
. In this way, chains of reduction can be described as
stochastic Markovian sequences

[
49
]
, leading to formalize the idea of
normalization with probability

r\in[0,1]
(see
[
9
]
).
Instead, in the language
\Lambda_{\nu}
reduction has nothing probabilistic by itself (it is even confluent), so we have to look for another approach.
Since a
{PNF}
, call it
t
, can be interpreted,
statically
, as a distribution of pseudo-values, one can ask what is the probability that a pseudo-value randomly found from
t
is in normal form. This leads to the following:

Definition 6.1
.

For any
t\in\mathcal{T}
and
r\in[0,1]
,
t
is
in normal form with
\mathbf{P}\geq r
when

\sum_{v\in\mathcal{HN}}\mathcal{D}_{t}(v)\geq r.

For example, the term
\nu a.\big(\Omega\oplus_{a}^{0}(\lambda x.x\Omega)\big)\oplus_{a}^{1}(\lambda x.x)
is in normal form with probability
\frac{3}{4}
: three of the four possible choices for
(a,0)
and
(a,1)
lead to a head normal form. Instead, the term
\nu a.\Big(\Omega\oplus_{a}^{0}\big(\nu b.\Omega\oplus_{b}^{0}(\lambda x.x)\big)\Big)
is in normal form with probability
\frac{1}{4}
: only one possible choice for
(a,0)
and one possible choice for
(b,0)
lead to a head normal form.

Our main result is that typing in
\lambda\textsf{{CPL}}
captures reduction to such probabilistic normal forms.

Theorem 18
.

If
\Gamma\vdash^{X,r}t:b\rightarrowtail\sigma
holds, then for all
f\in\llbracket b\rrbracket_{X}
,

\pi^{f}(t)
reduces to a term which is in normal form with
\mathbf{P}\geq r
.

Theorem
18
is proved by adapting the standard method of
reducibility predicates
. Just to give the reader an idea of how this method can be adapted to counting simple types, we illustrate the definition, for
\sigma
,
X
,
r\in[0,1]
, and
S\in\mathcal{B}((2^{\omega})^{X})
of the set

\mathrm{Red}_{\sigma}^{X,r}(S)\subseteq\Lambda_{\nu}
, given as follows:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Red}_{o}^{X,r}(S)

\displaystyle=\{t\mid\forall f\in S\ \pi^{f}(t)\text{ reduces to a normal form with }\mathbf{P}\geq r\}

\displaystyle\mathrm{Red}_{(\mathbf{C}^{q}\sigma)\Rightarrow\tau}^{X,r}(S)

\displaystyle=\{t\mid\forall S^{\prime}\subseteq S,\forall u\in\mathrm{Red}_{\sigma}^{X,q}(S^{\prime}),\ tu\in\mathrm{Red}_{\tau}^{X,r}(S^{\prime})\}

One can then establish that (1) whenever
t\in\mathrm{Red}_{\sigma}^{X,r}(S)
, for all
f\in S
,
\pi^{f}(t)
reduces to a term in normal formal with
\mathbf{P}\geq r
, and (2) that whenever
\Gamma\vdash^{X,r}t:b\rightarrowtail\sigma
is derivable, then
t\in\mathrm{Red}_{\sigma}^{X,r}(\llbracket b\rrbracket_{X})
. All details can be found in the Appendix
D
.

6.3
The Curry-Howard Correspondence for
\lambda\textsf{{CPL}}

In the usual correspondence between the simply typed
\lambda
-calculus and propositional logic, the typing derivations can be seen as the result of
decorating
logical proofs with
\lambda
-terms.
In a similar way, the type derivations of
\lambda\textsf{{CPL}}
can be seen as the result of decorating (a certain class of)
CPL
-proofs with terms from
\Lambda_{\nu}
.
Actually, we establish the correspondence by considering proofs
in a
multi-succedent
system for
CPL
.
This sequent calculus is equivalent to the one presented in Section
4
and is provably sound and complete with respect to the counting semantics.
Specifically, it is obtained as a straightforward multi-succedent
extension of the given, single-succedent version.
13
13

13

For example,
the single-succedent version of rule
\mathsf{Ax1}

turns into the following multi-succedent rule:

\vDash a\in X

b\vDash^{X}x_{i}^{a}

\mathsf{Ax1}

\vdash^{X}\Delta,b\rightarrowtail\mathbf{i}_{a}

Remarkably, it is equally possible to establish the correspondence between
\lambda\textsf{{CPL}}
and
CPL
,
by considering the single-succedent rule system.
Nevertheless, the embedding for the abstraction rule becomes somehow trivial.

To give the reader a glimpse of how a translation from
\lambda\textsf{{CPL}}
to
CPL
can be defined, we first associate simple types
\sigma,\tau
with formulas
\sigma^{*},\tau^{*}
as follows:

\displaystyle o^{*}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{Flip}

\displaystyle\qquad(\mathfrak{s}\Rightarrow\sigma)^{*}

\displaystyle=\lnot\mathfrak{s}^{*}\vee\sigma^{*}

\displaystyle(\mathbf{C}^{q}\sigma)^{*}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}\sigma^{*}

where
\mathsf{Flip}
is the (classically provable) closed formula
\mathbf{C}^{1}_{a}(\mathbf{0}_{a}\lor\lnot\mathbf{0}_{a})
, and where
a
is some fixed name.
The idea now is that a type derivation of
\Gamma\vdash^{X,r}t:b\rightarrowtail\sigma
yields a proof in
CPL
of the labelled sequent

\vdash^{X}b\rightarrowtail\mathbf{C}^{r}_{a}\sigma^{*},\bot\leftarrowtail\Gamma^{*}
,
where
\bot\leftarrowtail\Gamma^{*}
is

\bot\leftarrowtail(\mathfrak{s}_{1})^{*},\dots,\bot\leftarrowtail(\mathfrak{s}_{n})^{*}
,
for
\Gamma=\{\mathfrak{s}_{1}:\sigma_{1},\dots,\mathfrak{s}_{n}:\sigma_{n}\}
, and
a
is some fresh name.

One can check then that the abstraction rule translates into one of the disjunction rules of
CPL
(together with an application of Lemma
10
), and that the application rule translates into the cut-rule.
Furthermore, the generator rule of
\lambda\textsf{{CPL}}
essentially translates into the rule
\mathsf{R}_{\mathbf{C}}^{\rightarrowtail}
of
CPL
(see the Appendix
D
for all details).
More generally, all rules of
\lambda\textsf{{CPL}}
translate into the application of one or more rules of
CPL
, leading to the following:

Proposition 19
.

If
\Gamma\vdash^{X,r}t:b\rightarrowtail\sigma
