is derivable in
\lambda\textsf{{CPL}}
, then

\vdash^{X}_{\textsf{{CPL}}}b\rightarrowtail\mathbf{C}^{r}_{a}\sigma^{*},\bot\leftarrowtail\Gamma^{*}
.

Is it possible to go in the other direction, that is, from proofs to programs?
Recall that simply typed systems for the
\lambda
-calculus usually correspond to proof systems for
minimal logic
. As
CPL
was defined as a
classical
system, one can hardly hope to translate
any
proof from
CPL
into a
\lambda
-term. However, it is possible to define a minimal fragment of
CPL
in such a way that proofs in
mCPL
translate into typing derivations of
\lambda\textsf{{CPL}}
.
For the interested reader, we present this system, called
mCPL
, in the Appendix
D
.

Remark 4
.

The Curry-Howard correspondence usually includes also a correspondence between
\lambda
-term reduction and normalization procedures.
We already observed that the cut-rule is admissible in
\textsf{{CPL}}_{0}
and
CPL
. However, the investigation of cut-elimination procedures for these systems is among the many aspects of counting quantifiers that we still have to explore.

7
Arithmetic

This paper introduced a new quantified propositional logic,
capable of characterizing Wagner’s counting hierarchy and
of inspiring the design of type systems for probabilistic

\lambda
-calculi.
But this is not the end of the story.
As we will further discuss in the following paragraphs,
counting quantifiers also make sense in other settings,
particularly in the context of first-order logic,
as witnessed by investigations on the descriptive complexity
of counting classes
[
34
]
.
In this section, we want to spell out, without going into the details,
how the ideas behind counting propositional logic make sense in
arithmetic too.
Remarkably, the resulting logical system becomes much
more powerful and potentially very interesting from a computational
viewpoint.
14
14

14

For further details, see
[
2
]
.

Observe first although it is convenient and elegant to
formulate the semantics of
CPL
by referring to the
Cantor space, this might look unnecessary.
In fact, the nature of the underlying system makes it

impossible
to consider formulas with more than
a constant number of indexes.
But what if natural numbers become first-class citizens,
like in arithmetic?
In fact, one may naturally view
CPL
-quantification as a quantification
over a first-order predicate
,

\mathbf{C}_{P}^{q}A
such that occurrences of the atoms

\mathbf{i}_{a}
in
A
are replaced by the first-order
atom
P(t)
, where
t
is now an arithmetical term.
It would make perfect sense then
to write sentences as the following one:

\mathbf{C}^{q}_{P}\forall x.P(x).

For obvious reasons, the formula is valid only when
q
is
0
, since the infinite sequences in
2^{\omega}
in which

0
never occurs form a singleton, which has measure
0
. Dually,
the following formula turns out to be valid for every value of
q
:

\mathbf{C}^{q}_{P}\exists x.P(x).

More interesting formulas can also be built. For example,
let
F(x,y)
and
G(x,y)
be arithmetical formulas expressing respectively that
“
y
is strictly smaller than the length of
x
” (where
x
is seen as a code for a finite binary sequence), and
“the
y+1
-th bit of
x
is 1”. Then, the following formula

\forall x.\mathbf{C}^{1}_{P}\exists y.\forall z.F(x,z)\rightarrow\big(F(x,z)\land G(x,z)\leftrightarrow P(y+z)\big)

encodes the so-called
infinite monkey theorem
, a classical
result from probability theory that states that a monkey randomly
typing on a keyboard has probability
1
of ending up writing
the
Macbeth
(or any other fixed string) sooner or later:
let
x
be a binary encoding of the
Macbeth
,
and let the predicate
P(n)
describe the random
value typed by the monkey at time
n
;
then, with probability 1, there exists a time
y
after which

P(n)
will evolve exactly like
x
.

But could we give semantics to the resulting formulas?
The answer is affirmative and surprising.
Indeed, we can do it precisely along the same lines we
followed in Section
3
:
instead of interpreting a predicate symbol,
P
,
as some function from natural numbers to truth-values,
we may directly interpret
P
as the set of

all
functions yielding a measurable set

\llbracket P\rrbracket\in\mathcal{B}(2^{\omega})
,
precisely as in
\textsf{{CPL}}_{0}
.
Since all other connectives of first-order arithmetic translate
through the operations of a
\sigma
-algebra
(with
countable
unions and intersections playing a
fundamental role in interpreting the arithmetic quantifiers),
any arithmetic formula
A
can be interpreted
as a measurable set,
\llbracket A\rrbracket\in\mathcal{B}(2^{\omega})
.
We are no longer in the finite world of propositional logic,
so it makes no sense to
count
such sets
\llbracket A\rrbracket
;
yet, it still makes perfect sense to
measure
them.
In particular, when
A
is a formula with a unique
predicate variable
P
, we can interpret

\mathbf{C}^{q}_{P}A
as being true precisely when

\mu(\llbracket A\rrbracket)\geq q
.

In this way we can even
prove
that formulas,
such as the one formalizing the infinite monkey theorem, are valid,
and their proofs will rely on some (non-trivial) facts from
measure theory.
Moreover, along similar lines to what was shown in
Section
6
, it should be possible to capture
some operational properties of probabilistic
programs, as for example
almost sure termination
,
by means of arithmetical formulas.
In sum, with counting propositional logic we just touched the
tip of the iceberg of a seemingly new and exciting
correspondence between logic and computation theory,
that deserves to be explored much further.
15
15

15

These ideas are at the basis of the development of a first-order logic with measure quantifiers, called
\mathsf{MQPA}
, as presented in
[
2
]
, where some first results are also studied.

8
Related Works

The counting hierarchy was (independently) defined
in the 1980s by Wagner
[
66
,
67
,
68
]
and
by Pareberry and Schnitger
[
45
]
.
It was conceived as Meyer and Stockmeyer’s polynomial
hierarchy
[
38
,
39
]
,
which allows to express the complexity of
many natural problems in which counting is involved,
and which are not “represented” by
\mathsf{PH}
.
There are two main, equivalent
[
63
]
, ways
of characterizing the counting hierarchy:
the original characterization, in terms of

alternating quantifiers
,
[
67
]
,
and the oracle characterization
[
62
,
63
]
.
To the best of our knowledge,
CPL
is novel in its being an
enrichment of propositional logic by means of counting quantifiers.
However, strong similarities exist between our
\mathbf{C}
and
Wagner’s
counting quantifier

[
67
]
,
and these similarities have been exploited in Section
5
.
Furthermore, there is a worth-noticing analogy between the role of
the operator
\mathbf{C}
and usual propositional quantifiers:
remarkably, Meyer and Stockmeyer
[
39
]

not only showed that
\mathtt{TQBF}
, the quantitative analogue of
CPL
,
is
PSPACE
-complete, but also that
\Sigma^{P}_{k}
-complete
problems in the polynomial hierarchy correspond to
the satisfiability problem for quantified Boolean languages with

k
–1 quantifiers alternation, namely
\mathtt{TQBF}_{k}
.

It is worth mentioning that in the same years some
other “probabilistic” quantifiers were introduced.
For example, Papadimitriou
[
44
]
characterizes

PPSPACE
by alternating standard quantifiers with a
probabilistic
quantifier, which expresses the fact that more than the half of the strings of a
certain length satisfy the underlying predicate.
Zachos and Heller characterized
BPP
by means of a

random
quantifier
[
71
]
, and Zachos also
considered the relationship between classical and
probabilistic classes introducing other quantifiers, such as
the
overwhelming
the
and
majority
ones
[
70
]
.
Remarkably, all these works concern counting quantification on
(classes of) languages,
rather than
stricto sensu
logical operators.
An exception is represented by Kontinen’s work
[
34
]
,
in which counting quantifiers are second-order quantifiers
in an otherwise first-order logic,
much in the style of descriptive complexity.
In other words, one formula stands for a complexity
bounded algorithm, while here formulas are taken as data.

Despite the extensive literature on logical systems enabling
(in various ways and for different purposes) some forms of probabilistic
reasoning, there is not much about logics tied to computational aspects,
as
CPL
is.
Most of the recent logical formalisms have been
developed in the realm of modal logic, starting from the work by
Nilsson
[
41
,
42
]
.
In particular, in the 1990s,
Bacchus
[
7
,
4
,
5
]

defined
probability terms
by means of a modal operator
\mathsf{prob}
,
which effectively computes the probability of certain events,
and
probability formulas
,
which are equalities between probability
terms and numbers, such as
\mathsf{prob}(\alpha)=\frac{1}{2}

(this, by the way, is not too different from the formula of

\textsf{{CPL}}_{0}

\mathbf{C}^{\frac{1}{2}}A
;
noticeably, Bacchus’
\mathsf{prob}
yields terms, whereas
\mathbf{C}
yields
formulas).
In the same years also Fagin, Halpern and Megiddo
[
24
]

introduced an interesting form of probability logic (which was later
further studied in
[
23
,
30
,
31
]
).
In this logic, probability spaces are the underlying model,
and can be accessed through so-called
weight terms
.

Another class of probabilistic modal logics have been designed to model Markov chains and similar structures. Some of these logics are probabilistic extensions of
CTL
, the standard logic for model-checking (see, for example,
[
35
,
32
,
36
]
).
A notable example is
Riesz modal logic

[
25
]
, which admits a sound and complete proof system.
Differently from
CPL
, in these logics modal operators have a dynamical meaning, as they describe transitions in a MDP.

Concerning proof theory, axiomatic proof systems have been provided
for both logics
[
7
,
24
]
, in connection with well-known
modal systems such as
KD45

[
6
,
22
]
.
With the sole exception of Riesz modal logic, we are
not aware of sequent calculi for probability logic.
By the way, our one-sided, sequent calculi are inspired by labelled
calculi such as
G3K*
and
G3P*
,
as presented for example in
[
40
,
29
]
,
but, differently from them, our rules are not invertible.

Probabilistic models and randomized computation are pervasive
in many areas of computer science and programming.
From the 1950s on, the interest for probabilistic algorithms and
models started
spreading
[
18
,
17
,
12
,
50
,
53
,
54
,
57
]

and probabilistic computation has been intensely
studied by the TCS research
community
[
50
,
55
,
19
,
56
]
.
Nowadays, well-defined computational models,
such as randomized variations on
probabilistic automata[56],
(both Markovian and oracle) Turing machines
[
55
,
56
,
26
,
27
]
,
and
\lambda
-calculi, are available.

Also probabilistic programming languages have been extensively
studied from the 1970s on, and are still under
scrutiny.
Specifically, foundational work on probabilistic functional languages
is
[
52
]
, where Saheb-Djahromy’s the typed,
higher-order
\lambda
-calculus
LCF
is introduced.
Other early introduced calculi are, for example,
Ramsey and Pfeffer’s stochastic
\lambda
-calculus,
[
51
]
and Park’s
\lambda_{\gamma}
,
[
46
]
,
see also
[
33
]
,
[
48
]
,
[
47
]
.
Operational semantics and properties of higher-order
probabilistic programs have been studied in
[
16
]
.
A particularly fruitful approach to the study of higher-order probabilistic
computation consists in enriching general
\lambda
-calculus with a
probabilistic choice operator
\oplus

[
20
,
16
,
21
]
.
For these calculi, type systems ensuring various form of termination
properties have recently been
introduced
[
14
,
10
,
3
]
.
Our calculus is particularly inspired by the one introduced
in
[
15
]
.
Moreover, our probabilistic definition of normal forms is
somehow reminiscent of the operational
semantics from
[
37
]
.
In all these cases, however, no logically inspired
type system or Curry-Howard style correspondence is currently known.

9
Conclusion

The aim of the present paper is not the introduction of counting
propositional logic
per se
.
After all, the idea of switching from qualitative to quantitative notions
of quantification is not ours and has already been proved useful.
Indeed, the main contribution of this work is to define a logical
counterpart for
randomized
computation and, so, to
generalizing some standard results developed in the deterministic framework.
Indeed, in Section
5
, we have shown that counting
quantifiers can play nicely with propositional logic in characterizing
the counting hierarchy, while, in Section
6
, we have
designed type systems for the lambda-calculus in the
spirit of the Curry-Howard correspondence.
In this way, logic can somehow catch up with some old
and recent computational complexity and
programming language theory results.

Many problems and questions are still open, or could not have
been described in this paper, due to space reasons.
For example, the proof theory of
CPL
has just been briefly delineated,
but the dynamics of the introduced formal systems
certainly deserves to be investigated, despite not being
crucial for the results in this paper.
The potential interest of injecting counting quantifiers
(or, better, measure quantifiers) into the language of arithmetic has
been briefly described in Section
7
,
and implicitly suggests several intriguing problems,
which are still open.
A first-order logic endowed with measure-quantifiers,
called
MQPA
, has been presented in
[
2
]
,
where some first representation results concerning
its connection with randomized computation has been established.

Appendix

Appendix A
Proofs from Section
3

A.1
From Valuations to Measurable Sets

Lemma 20
.

For every formula of
\textsf{{CPL}}_{0}
, call it
A
,
the following holds:

\llbracket\mathbf{C}^{0}A\rrbracket=2^{\omega}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \llbracket\mathbf{D}^{0}A\rrbracket=\emptyset.

Proof.

For every
A
,
\mu
(
\llbracket A\rrbracket
)
\geq 0
holds.
Thus,

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathbf{C}^{0}A\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}2^{\omega}\ \ \ &\text{if }\mu(\llbracket A\rrbracket)\geq 0\\
\emptyset\ \ \ &\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

\displaystyle=2^{\omega}

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathbf{D}^{0}A\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}2^{\omega}\ \ \ &\text{if }\mu(\llbracket A\rrbracket)<0\\
\emptyset\ \ \ &\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

\displaystyle=\emptyset.

∎

Lemma 21
.

For every formula of
\textsf{{CPL}}_{0}
, call it
A
, and

q\in\mathbb{Q}_{[0,1]}
, the following holds:

\mathbf{C}^{q}A\equiv\lnot\mathbf{D}^{q}A\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \mathbf{D}^{q}A\equiv\lnot\mathbf{C}^{q}A.

Proof.

The proof is based on Definition
3.1
:

\displaystyle\llbracket\neg\mathbf{D}^{q}A\rrbracket

\displaystyle=2^{\omega}\ –\ \llbracket\mathbf{D}^{q}A\rrbracket

\displaystyle=2^{\omega}\ –\begin{cases}2^{\omega}\ \ &\text{ if }\mu(\llbracket A\rrbracket)<q\\
\emptyset\ \ &\text{ otherwise }\end{cases}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\emptyset\ \ &\text{ if }\mu(\llbracket A\rrbracket)<q\\
2^{\omega}\ \ &\text{ otherwise }\end{cases}

\displaystyle=\llbracket\mathbf{C}^{q}A\rrbracket

\displaystyle\llbracket\neg\mathbf{C}^{q}A\rrbracket

\displaystyle=2^{\omega}\ –\ \llbracket\mathbf{C}^{q}A\rrbracket

\displaystyle=2^{\omega}\ –\ \begin{cases}2^{\omega}\ \ &\text{ if }\mu(\llbracket A\rrbracket)\geq q\\
\emptyset\ \ &\text{ otherwise}\end{cases}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\emptyset\ \ &\text{ if }\mu(\llbracket A\rrbracket)\geq q\\
2^{\omega}\ \ &\text{ otherwise}\end{cases}

\displaystyle=\llbracket\mathbf{D}^{q}A\rrbracket.

∎

A.2
The Proof Theory of
\textsf{{CPL}}_{0}

From now on, let us denote the one-sided,
single-succedent proof system for
\textsf{{CPL}}_{0}
as
\mathbf{GsCPL_{0}}
.
Let us also call
\mu
-rules the two rules
\mathsf{R_{\mu}^{\rightarrowtail}}
and
\mathsf{R_{\mu}^{\leftarrowtail}}
.
The notion of derivation in
\mathbf{GsCPL_{0}}
is defined in the
standard way.

Definition A.1

(Derivation in
\mathbf{GsCPL_{0}}
)
.

A
derivation in
\mathbf{GsCPL_{0}}
is either an initial
sequent
–

\mathsf{Ax1}
or
\mathsf{Ax2}

–

or an instance of a
\mu
-rule
–

\mathsf{R_{\mu}^{\rightarrowtail}}
or
\mathsf{R_{\mu}^{\leftarrowtail}}

–
,
or it is obtained by applying a rule of
\mathbf{GsCPL_{0}}

to derivations concluding its premisses.

The definition of
derivation height
is completely
canonical as well.

Definition A.2

(Derivation Height)
.

The
height of a derivation in
\mathbf{GsCPL_{0}}
is
the greatest number of successive applications of rules in it,
where initial sequents and
\mu
-rules have height 0.

As anticipated, the proof of soundness is standard.
See
2

Proof.

The proof is by induction on the height of the derivation of

\vdash L
, call it
n
.

•

Base case.
If
n
= 0,
\vdash L
is either an initial sequent or is derived by a
\mu
-rule.
Both cases are trivial:

\mathsf{Ax1}.

For some
b
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
L=b\rightarrowtail\mathbf{n}
.
Then, the derivation is in the following form:

b\vDash x_{n}

\mathsf{Ax1}

\vdash b\rightarrowtail\mathbf{n}

But,
b\vDash x_{n}
means that

\llbracket b\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket x_{n}\rrbracket
,
which is
\llbracket b\rrbracket\subseteq\mathrm{Cyl}(n)
.
Furthermore, since
\llbracket\mathbf{n}\rrbracket
=
\mathrm{Cyl}(n)
,

\llbracket b\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket\mathbf{n}\rrbracket
holds.
For Definition
3.5
, this means that

\vDash b\rightarrowtail\mathbf{n}
, as desired.

\mathsf{Ax2}
.
For some
b
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

L=b\leftarrowtail\mathbf{n}
.
Then, the derivation is in the following form:

x_{n}\vDash b

\mathsf{Ax2}

\vdash b\leftarrowtail\mathbf{n}

But,
x_{n}\vDash b
means
\llbracket x_{n}\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket b\rrbracket
, which is
\mathrm{Cyl}(n)\subseteq\llbracket b\rrbracket
.
Moreover,
\llbracket\mathbf{n}\rrbracket=\mathrm{Cyl}(n)
, so

\llbracket\mathbf{n}\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket b\rrbracket
holds.
For Definition
3.5
, this means that

\vDash b\leftarrowtail\mathbf{n}
, as desired.

\mathsf{R_{\mu}^{\rightarrowtail}}
.
For some
b
and
A
,
L=b\rightarrowtail A
.
Then, the derivation is in the following form:

\mu(\llbracket b\rrbracket)=0

\mathsf{R_{\mu}^{\rightarrowtail}}

\vdash b\rightarrowtail A

Since
\mu
(
\llbracket b\rrbracket
) = 0, for basic measure theory
\llbracket b\rrbracket=\emptyset
.
Thus, in particular,
\llbracket b\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket A\rrbracket
,
and so
\vDash b\rightarrowtail A
.

\mathsf{R_{\mu}^{\leftarrowtail}}
.
For some
b
and
A
,
L
=
b\rightarrowtail A
.
Then, the derivation is in the following form:

\mu(\llbracket b\rrbracket)=1

\mathsf{R_{\mu}^{\leftarrowtail}}

\vdash b\leftarrowtail A

Since
\mu
(
\llbracket b\rrbracket
) = 1, for basic measure theory,
\llbracket b\rrbracket
=
2^{\omega}
.
Thus, in particular,

\llbracket A\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket b\rrbracket
,
and so
\vDash b\leftarrowtail A
.

•

Inductive case.

Let us assume that soundness holds for derivations of
height up to
n
,
and show that it holds for derivations of height
n
+1.

\mathsf{R}_{\cup}^{\rightarrowtail}.

If the last rule applied is an instance of
\mathsf{R}_{\cup}^{\rightarrowtail}
,
the derivation has the following form:

\vdots

\vdash c\rightarrowtail A

\vdots

\vdash d\rightarrowtail A

b\vDash c\vee d

\mathsf{R}_{\cup}^{\rightarrowtail}

\vdash b\rightarrowtail A

By IH,
\vdash c\rightarrowtail A
and

\vdash d\rightarrowtail A
are valid.
Furthermore, since
b\vDash c\vee d
,

\llbracket b\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket c\vee d\rrbracket
holds,
that is

\llbracket b\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket c\rrbracket\cup\llbracket d\rrbracket
.
For IH and Definition
3.5
,

\vDash c\rightarrowtail A
and
\vDash d\rightarrowtail A
,
which is
\llbracket c\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket A\rrbracket
and

\llbracket d\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket A\rrbracket
.
For basic set theory,
\llbracket c\rrbracket\cup\llbracket d\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket A\rrbracket

and, since

\llbracket b\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket c\rrbracket\cup\llbracket d\rrbracket
,
also
\llbracket b\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket A\rrbracket
holds.
Thus, by Definition
3.5
,
\vDash b\rightarrowtail A
.

\mathsf{R}_{\cap}^{\leftarrowtail}.
If the last rule applied is an instance of
\mathsf{R}_{\cap}^{\leftarrowtail}
, the derivation has the following form:

\vdots

\vdash c\leftarrowtail A

\vdots

\vdash d\leftarrowtail A

c\wedge d\vDash b

\mathsf{R}_{\cap}^{\leftarrowtail}

\vdash b\leftarrowtail A

By IH,
\vdash c\leftarrowtail A
and

\vdash d\leftarrowtail A
are valid.
Since
c\wedge d\vDash b
,
for Definition
3.5

\llbracket c\wedge d\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket b\rrbracket
,
which is
\llbracket c\rrbracket\cap\llbracket d\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket b\rrbracket
.
For IH and Definition
3.5
,
\vDash c\leftarrowtail A
and
\vDash d\leftarrowtail A
, which is
\llbracket A\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket c\rrbracket
and
\llbracket A\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket d\rrbracket
.
For basic set theory,
\llbracket A\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket c\rrbracket\cap\llbracket d\rrbracket
, and
so, given
\llbracket c\rrbracket\cap\llbracket d\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket b\rrbracket
, it can be concluded that
\llbracket A\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket b\rrbracket
holds.
Therefore, by Definition
3.5
,
\vDash b\leftarrowtail A
.

\mathsf{R}_{\neg}^{\rightarrowtail}.

If the last rule applied is an instance of
\mathsf{R}_{\neg}^{\rightarrowtail}
,
the derivation has the following form:

\vdots

\vdash c\leftarrowtail A

b\vDash\neg c

\mathsf{R}_{\neg}^{\rightarrowtail}

\vdash b\rightarrowtail\neg A

Given the external hypothesis
b\vDash\neg c
,

\llbracket b\rrbracket\subseteq\big(2^{\omega}\ –\ {\llbracket c\rrbracket}\big)

holds.
For IH,
\vdash c\leftarrowtail A
so,
for Definition
3.5
,

\llbracket A\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket c\rrbracket
.
By basic set theory

\big(2^{\omega}\ –\ \llbracket c\rrbracket\big)\subseteq\big(2^{\omega}\ –\ \llbracket A\rrbracket\big)
,
that is
\big(2^{\omega}\ –\ \llbracket c\rrbracket\big)\subseteq\llbracket\neg A\rrbracket
and,
since

\llbracket b\rrbracket\subseteq\big(2^{\omega}\ –\ {\llbracket c\rrbracket}\big)
,
it is possible to conclude that

\llbracket b\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket\neg A\rrbracket
holds.
But then, for Definition
3.5

\vDash b\rightarrowtail\neg A
.

\mathsf{R}_{\neg}^{\leftarrowtail}.

If the last rule applied is an instance of
\mathsf{R}_{\neg}^{\leftarrowtail},
the derivation has the following form:

\vdots

\vdash c\rightarrowtail A

\neg c\vDash b

\mathsf{R}_{\neg}^{\leftarrowtail}

\vdash b\leftarrowtail\neg A

Given the external hypothesis
\neg c\vDash b
,
then
\big(2^{\omega}\ –\ \llbracket c\rrbracket\big)\subseteq\llbracket b\rrbracket
,
and,
for IH,
\vDash c\rightarrowtail A
,
which is

\llbracket c\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket A\rrbracket
.
For basic set theory,

\big(2^{\omega}\ –\ \llbracket A\rrbracket\big)\subseteq\big(2^{\omega}\ –\ \llbracket c\rrbracket\big)
,
which is

\llbracket\neg A\rrbracket\subseteq\big(2^{\omega}\ –\ {\llbracket c\rrbracket}\big)
,
and,
since
\big(2^{\omega}\ –\ \llbracket c\rrbracket\big)\subseteq\llbracket b\rrbracket
,
also
\llbracket\neg A\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket b\rrbracket
.
So, for Definition
3.5
,
\vDash b\leftarrowtail\neg A
.

\mathsf{R1_{\vee}^{\rightarrowtail}}.

If the last rule applied is an instance of
\mathsf{R1_{\vee}^{\rightarrowtail}}
,
the derivation has the following form:

\vdots

\vdash b\rightarrowtail A

\mathsf{R1_{\vee}^{\rightarrowtail}}

\vdash b\rightarrowtail A\vee B

By IH,
\vDash b\rightarrowtail A
, which is

\llbracket b\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket A\rrbracket
.
For basic set theory, also

\llbracket b\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket A\rrbracket\cup\llbracket B\rrbracket
, which is

\llbracket b\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket A\vee B\rrbracket
.
Therefore,
\vDash b\rightarrowtail A\vee B
as desired.

\mathsf{R2_{\vee}^{\rightarrowtail}}.

If the last rule applied is an instance of
\mathsf{R2_{\vee}^{\rightarrowtail}}
,
the derivation has the following form:

\vdots

\vdash b\rightarrowtail B

\mathsf{R2_{\vee}^{\rightarrowtail}}

\vdash b\rightarrowtail A\vee B

By IH,
\vDash b\rightarrowtail B
, which is

\llbracket b\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket B\rrbracket
.
For basic set theory, also

\llbracket b\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket A\rrbracket\cup\llbracket B\rrbracket
, which is

\llbracket b\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket A\vee B\rrbracket
.
Therefore,
\vDash b\rightarrowtail A\vee B
as desired.

\mathsf{R}_{\vee}^{\leftarrowtail}.
If the last rule applied is an instance of
\mathsf{R}_{\vee}^{\leftarrowtail}
, the derivation has the following form:

\vdots

\vdash b\leftarrowtail A

\vdots

\vdash b\leftarrowtail B

\mathsf{R}_{\vee}^{\leftarrowtail}

\vdash b\leftarrowtail A\vee B

By IH,
\vdash b\leftarrowtail A
and
\vdash b\leftarrowtail B
are valid.
So, for Definition
3.5
,
\vDash b\leftarrowtail A
and
\vDash b\leftarrowtail B
, which is,

\llbracket A\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket b\rrbracket
and

\llbracket B\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket b\rrbracket
.
For basic set theory,

\llbracket A\rrbracket\cup\llbracket B\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket b\rrbracket
,
which is
\llbracket A\vee B\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket b\rrbracket
,
and so
\vDash b\leftarrowtail A\vee B
.

\mathsf{R}_{\wedge}^{\rightarrowtail}.

If the last rule applied is an instance of
\mathsf{R}_{\wedge}^{\rightarrowtail}
,
the derivation has the following form:

\vdots

\vdash b\rightarrowtail A

\vdots

\vdash b\rightarrowtail B

\mathsf{R}_{\wedge}^{\rightarrowtail}

\vdash b\rightarrowtail A\wedge B

By IH,
\vDash b\rightarrowtail A
and
\vDash b\rightarrowtail B
,
which is
\llbracket b\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket A\rrbracket
and
\llbracket b\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket B\rrbracket
.
For basic set theory,

\llbracket b\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket A\rrbracket\cap\llbracket B\rrbracket
,
which is
\llbracket b\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket A\wedge B\rrbracket
.
Therefore, by Definition
3.5
,

\vDash b\rightarrowtail A\wedge B
.

\mathsf{R1_{\wedge}^{\leftarrowtail}}.

If the last rule applied is an instance of
\mathsf{R1_{\wedge}^{\leftarrowtail}}
,
the derivation has the following form:

\vdots

\vdash b\leftarrowtail A

\mathsf{R1_{\wedge}^{\leftarrowtail}}

\vdash b\leftarrowtail A\wedge B

By IH,
\vDash b\leftarrowtail A
,
which is
\llbracket A\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket b\rrbracket
.
For basic set theory,

\llbracket A\rrbracket\cap\llbracket B\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket b\rrbracket
, which is

\llbracket A\wedge B\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket b\rrbracket
.
Therefore,
\vDash b\leftarrowtail A\wedge B
.

\mathsf{R2_{\wedge}^{\leftarrowtail}}.

If the last rule applied is an instance of
\mathsf{R2_{\wedge}^{\leftarrowtail}}
,
the derivation has the following form:

\vdots

\vdash b\leftarrowtail B

\mathsf{R2_{\wedge}^{\leftarrowtail}}

\vdash b\leftarrowtail A\wedge B

By IH,
\vDash b\leftarrowtail B
,
which is
\llbracket B\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket b\rrbracket
.
For basic set theory,

\llbracket A\rrbracket\cap\llbracket B\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket b\rrbracket
, which is

\llbracket A\wedge B\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket b\rrbracket
.
Therefore,
\vDash b\leftarrowtail A\wedge B
.

\mathsf{R}_{\mathbf{C}}^{\rightarrowtail}.

If the last rule applied is an instance of
\mathsf{R}_{\mathbf{C}}^{\rightarrowtail}
,
the derivation has the following form:

\vdots

\vdash c\rightarrowtail A

\mu(\llbracket c\rrbracket)\geq q

\mathsf{R}_{\mathbf{C}}^{\rightarrowtail}

\vdash b\rightarrowtail\mathbf{C}^{q}A

By IH,
\vDash c\rightarrowtail A
, which is
\llbracket c\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket A\rrbracket
, and
\mu(\llbracket c\rrbracket)\geq q
holds.
For basic measure theory, also
\mu
(
\llbracket A\rrbracket
)
\geq q
.
Then,
\llbracket\mathbf{C}^{q}A\rrbracket
=
2^{\omega}
and, so,
(for every
b
)

\llbracket b\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket\mathbf{C}^{q}A\rrbracket
,
which is
\vDash b\rightarrowtail\mathbf{C}^{q}A
.

\mathsf{R}_{\mathbf{C}}^{\leftarrowtail}.

If the last rule applied is an instance of
\mathsf{R}_{\mathbf{C}}^{\leftarrowtail}
, the derivation has the following form:

\vdots

\vdash c\leftarrowtail A

\mu(\llbracket c\rrbracket)<q

\mathsf{R}_{\mathbf{C}}^{\leftarrowtail}

\vdash b\leftarrowtail\mathbf{C}^{q}A

By IH,
\vDash c\leftarrowtail A
,
which is
\llbracket A\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket c\rrbracket
,
and
\mu(\llbracket c\rrbracket)<q
holds.
For basic set theory, also
\mu(\llbracket A\rrbracket)<q
.
Therefore,
\llbracket\mathbf{C}^{q}A\rrbracket
=
\emptyset
, and so

\llbracket\mathbf{C}^{q}A\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket b\rrbracket
,
which is
\vDash b\leftarrowtail\mathbf{C}^{q}A
.

\mathsf{R}_{\mathbf{D}}^{\rightarrowtail}.

If the last rule applied is an instance of
\mathsf{R}_{\mathbf{D}}^{\rightarrowtail}
, the derivation has the following form:

\vdots

\vdash c\leftarrowtail A

\mu(\llbracket c\rrbracket)<q

\mathsf{R}_{\mathbf{D}}^{\rightarrowtail}

\vdash b\rightarrowtail\mathbf{D}^{q}A

By IH,
\vDash c\leftarrowtail A
,
which is
\llbracket A\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket c\rrbracket
, and

\mu(\llbracket c\rrbracket)<q
holds.
For basic set theory,
\mu(\llbracket A\rrbracket)<q
.
But then,
\llbracket\mathbf{D}^{q}A\rrbracket
=
2^{\omega}
and so (for every
b
)
\llbracket b\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket\mathbf{D}^{q}A\rrbracket
, which is
\vDash b\rightarrowtail\mathbf{D}^{q}A
.

\mathsf{R}_{\mathbf{D}}^{\leftarrowtail}.
If the last rule applied is an instance of
\mathsf{R}_{\mathbf{D}}^{\leftarrowtail}
, the derivation has the following form:

\vdots

\vdash c\rightarrowtail A

\mu(\llbracket c\rrbracket)\geq q

\mathsf{R}_{\mathbf{D}}^{\leftarrowtail}

\vdash b\leftarrowtail\mathbf{D}^{q}A

By IH,
\vDash c\rightarrowtail A
, which is

\llbracket c\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket A\rrbracket
, and

\mu(\llbracket c\rrbracket)\geq q
holds.
For basic set theory,
\mu(\llbracket A\rrbracket)\geq q
.
Therefore,
\llbracket\mathbf{D}^{q}A\rrbracket
=
\emptyset
and, so
\llbracket\mathbf{D}^{q}A\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket b\rrbracket
, which is
\vDash b\leftarrowtail\mathbf{D}^{q}A
.

∎

In order to prove the completeness of

\mathbf{GsCPL_{0}}
with respect to the semantics of
\textsf{{CPL}}_{0}
,
some preliminary notions and lemmas have to be introduced.

Definition A.3

(Basic Formulas)
.

A
basic formula
of
\mathbf{GsCPL_{0}}
is a labelled formula,

b\rightarrowtail A
or
b\leftarrowtail A
,
such that its logical part
A
is atomic.

Definition A.4

(Regular Sequent)
.

A
regular sequent
of
\mathbf{GsCPL_{0}}
is a sequent of the form
\vdash L
, such that
L
is a basic formula.

The notion of decomposition rewriting reduction is
defined by the following decomposition rules.

Definition A.5

(Decomposition Rewriting Reduction,
\leadsto_{0}
)
.

The
decomposition rewriting reduction
,
\leadsto_{0}
, from a sequent to a set of sequents (both in the language of
\mathbf{GsCPL_{0}}
), is defined by the following decomposition rules:

\displaystyle\text{if }b\vDash\neg c,\ \vdash b\rightarrowtail\neg A\ \

\displaystyle\leadsto_{0}\ \ \{\vdash c\leftarrowtail A\}

\displaystyle\text{if }\neg c\vDash b,\ \vdash b\leftarrowtail\neg A\ \

\displaystyle\leadsto_{0}\ \ \{\vdash c\rightarrowtail A\}

\displaystyle\text{if }b\vDash c\vee d,\ \vdash b\rightarrowtail A\vee B\ \

\displaystyle\leadsto_{0}\ \ \{\vdash c\rightarrowtail A,\vdash d\rightarrowtail B\}

\displaystyle\vdash b\leftarrowtail A\vee B\ \

\displaystyle\leadsto_{0}\ \ \{\vdash b\leftarrowtail A,\vdash b\leftarrowtail B\}

\displaystyle\vdash b\rightarrowtail A\wedge B\ \

\displaystyle\leadsto_{0}\ \ \{\vdash b\rightarrowtail A,\vdash b\rightarrowtail B\}

\displaystyle\text{if }c\wedge d\vDash b,\ \vdash b\leftarrowtail A\wedge B\ \

\displaystyle\leadsto_{0}\ \ \{\vdash c\leftarrowtail A,\vdash d\leftarrowtail B\}

\displaystyle\text{if }\mu(\llbracket c\rrbracket)\geq q,\ \vdash b\rightarrowtail\mathbf{C}^{q}A\ \

\displaystyle\leadsto_{0}\ \ \{\vdash c\rightarrowtail A\}

\displaystyle\text{if }\mu(\llbracket c\rrbracket)<q,\ \vdash b\leftarrowtail\mathbf{C}^{q}A\ \

\displaystyle\leadsto_{0}\ \ \{\vdash c\leftarrowtail A\}

\displaystyle\text{if }\mu(\llbracket c\rrbracket)<q,\ \vdash b\rightarrowtail\mathbf{D}^{q}A\ \

\displaystyle\leadsto_{0}\ \ \{\vdash c\leftarrowtail A\}

\displaystyle\text{if }\mu(\llbracket c\rrbracket)\geq q,\ \vdash b\leftarrowtail\mathbf{D}^{q}A\ \

\displaystyle\leadsto_{0}\ \ \{\vdash c\rightarrowtail A\}

\displaystyle\text{if }\mu(\llbracket b\rrbracket)=0,\ \vdash b\rightarrowtail A\ \

\displaystyle\leadsto_{0}\ \ \{\}

\displaystyle\text{if }\mu(\llbracket b\rrbracket)=1,\ \vdash b\leftarrowtail A\ \

\displaystyle\leadsto_{0}\ \ \{\}

\displaystyle\text{if }\mu(\llbracket b\rrbracket)\neq 0,\ \vdash b\rightarrowtail\mathbf{D}^{0}A\ \

\displaystyle\leadsto_{0}\ \ \{\vdash\bot\}

\displaystyle\text{if }\mu(\llbracket b\rrbracket)\neq 1,\ \vdash b\leftarrowtail\mathbf{C}^{0}A\ \

\displaystyle\leadsto_{0}\ \ \{\vdash\bot\}

Remarkably, rewriting rules are so defined that each application of a decomposition reduction on an arbitrary sequent,

\vdash L
, leads to a set of sequents,

\{\vdash L_{1},\dots,\vdash L_{n}\}
,
such that for every
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
,
the number of connectives of
\vdash L_{i}
is
(strictly) smaller than that of
\vdash L
.
Basing on
\leadsto_{0}
, it is possible to define a set-decomposition reduction,

\leadsto
, from a set of sequents to a set of sequents.

Definition A.6

(Set Decomposition,
\leadsto
)
.

The
set-decomposition reduction
,
\leadsto
, from a set of sequents to another set of sequents (in
\mathbf{GsCPL_{0}}
), is defined as follows:

\vdash L_{i}\leadsto_{0}\{\vdash L_{i_{1}},\dots,\vdash L_{i_{m}}\}

\{\vdash L_{1},\dots,\vdash L_{i},\dots,\vdash L_{n}\}\leadsto\{\vdash L_{1},\dots,\vdash L_{i_{1}},\dots,\vdash L_{i_{m}},\dots,\vdash L_{n}\}

In other words,
\leadsto
is the natural lifting of
\leadsto_{0}
to a relation on sets.

Before introducing the crucial notions of
normal form for these two reductions it is
worth mentioning that each predicate concerning one sequent
can be naturally generalized to sets of sequents
by stipulating that a predicate holds for the set when
it holds for every sequent in the set.
In order to make our presentation straightforward,
let us introduce the following auxiliary notion of corresponding set.

Definition A.7

(Corresponding Set)
.

Given a sequent,
\vdash L
,
we call its
corresponding set
the set including only
this sequent as its element,
\{\vdash L\}
.

The notion of
\leadsto
-normal form is defined
as predictable: a set of sequents is in
\leadsto
-normal form
when no set-decomposition reduction,
\leadsto
, can be applied on it.
Otherwise said, the set is such that there is no sequent in it
on which
\leadsto_{0}
can be applied.

Definition A.8

(
\leadsto
-Normal Form)
.

A sequent is a
\leadsto
-normal form
if no decomposition rewriting reduction rule,
\leadsto_{0}
, can be applied on it.
A set of sequents is in
\leadsto
-normal form
if it cannot be reduced by any
\leadsto
set-rewriting rule.

The proof of completeness crucially relies on
the given rewriting reductions,

\leadsto_{0}
and
\leadsto
, and it is based on some auxiliary
steps: (i) validity is
existentially
preserved
through
\leadsto
-decomposition, which is given a valid
sequent, there has (at least) one valid
\leadsto
-normal form
(ii) each
\leadsto
-normal form is valid if and only if it is derivable,
(iii) derivability is somehow “back-preserved”, through

\leadsto
-decomposition, which is, given a (set of) sequent(s)
which is
\leadsto
-decomposed into another set of sequents,
if the
\leadsto
-decomposed set is derivable,
then it is possible to construct a derivation for the original
(set of) sequent(s).

\leadsto
is strongly normalizing.

First it is proved that
\leadsto
is strongly normalizing,
and so that each decomposition process terminates.

Definition A.9

(Number of Connectives,
\mathsf{cn}
)
.

The
number of connectives of a labelled formula

L
,

\mathsf{cn}(L)
, is the number of connectives of its logical part
(equally labelled
\mathsf{cn}(A)
) and is inductively defined as follows:

\displaystyle\mathsf{cn}(b\rightarrowtail\mathbf{n})=\mathsf{cn}(b\leftarrowtail\mathbf{n})=\mathsf{cn}(\mathbf{n})

\displaystyle=1

\displaystyle\mathsf{cn}(b\rightarrowtail\neg A)=\mathsf{cn}(b\leftarrowtail\neg A)=\mathsf{cn}(\neg A)

\displaystyle=1+\mathsf{cn}(A)

\displaystyle\mathsf{cn}(b\rightarrowtail A^{*}B)=\mathsf{cn}(b\leftarrowtail A^{*}B)=\mathsf{cn}(A^{*}B)

\displaystyle=1+\mathsf{cn}(A)+\mathsf{cn}(B)

\displaystyle\mathsf{cn}(b\rightarrowtail*A)=\mathsf{cn}(b\leftarrowtail*A)=\mathsf{cn}(*A)

\displaystyle=1+\mathsf{cn}(A)

with
{}^{*}\in\{\wedge,\vee\}
and
*\in\{\mathbf{C}^{q},\mathbf{D}^{q}\}
.
Given a sequent
\vdash L
, the
number of connectives of the sequent
,
\mathsf{cn}(\vdash L)
, is the
number of connectives of its labelled formulas:

\mathsf{cn}(\vdash L)=\mathsf{cn}(L).

Definition A.10

(Set Measure,
\mathsf{ms}
)
.

Given a set of sequents

\{\vdash L_{1},\dots,\vdash L_{n}\},
its
measure
,

\mathsf{ms}(\{\vdash L_{1},\dots,\vdash L_{n}\}
),
is defined as follows:

\displaystyle\mathsf{ms}(\{\})

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\mathsf{ms}\big(\{\vdash L_{1},\dots,\vdash L_{n}\}\big)

\displaystyle=3^{\mathsf{cn}(\vdash L_{1})}+\dots+3^{\mathsf{cn}(\vdash L_{n})}.

See
3

Proof.

That every set of sequents is
\leadsto
-strongly normalizable is proven by showing that, if
\{\vdash L_{1},\dots,\vdash L_{m}\}\leadsto\{\vdash L_{1}^{\prime},\dots,\vdash L_{m}^{\prime}\}
, then
\mathsf{ms}(\{\vdash\Delta_{1},\dots,\vdash\Delta_{m}\})>\mathsf{ms}(\{\vdash\Delta_{1}^{\prime},\dots,\vdash\Delta_{m}^{\prime}\})
.
The proof is based on the exhaustive analysis of all
possible forms of
\leadsto
-reduction applicable to the
given set, which is by dealing with all possible forms of

\leadsto_{0}
-reduction of one of the
\vdash L_{i}
, with

i\in\{1,\dots,m\}
on which
\leadsto
is based.
Thus, we will take an arbitrary
\vdash L_{i}
to be
the “active” sequent of
\leadsto
.
Let us consider all the possible forms of
\leadsto_{0}
on which

\leadsto
can be based

Let
L=b\rightarrowtail\neg A
.
Assume that
\vdash L_{i}
is the active sequent of the

\leadsto
-decomposition and that
\leadsto
is based on the following
\leadsto_{0}
:

\vdash b\rightarrowtail\neg A\ \ \ \leadsto_{0}\ \ \ \ \{\vdash c\leftarrowtail A\}

where
b\vDash\neg c
. Thus:

\displaystyle\mathsf{ms}\big(\{\vdash L_{1},\dots,\vdash c\leftarrowtail A,\dots,\vdash L_{m}\}\big)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ref{def:cn}}}{{=}}3^{\mathsf{cn}(L_{1})}+\dots+3^{\mathsf{cn}(c\leftarrowtail A)}+\dots+3^{\mathsf{cn}(L_{n})}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ref{def:ms}}}{{=}}3^{\mathsf{cn}(L_{1})}+\dots+3^{\mathsf{cn}(A)}+\dots+3^{\mathsf{cn}(L_{n})}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ref{def:ms}}}{{=}}3^{\mathsf{cn}(L_{1})}+\dots+3^{\mathsf{cn}(\neg A)–1}+\dots+3^{\mathsf{cn}(L_{n})}

\displaystyle<3^{\mathsf{cn}(L_{1})}+\dots+3^{\mathsf{cn}(b\rightarrowtail\neg A)}+\dots+3^{\mathsf{cn}(L_{n})}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ref{def:ms}}}{{=}}\mathsf{ms}\big(\{\vdash L_{1},\dots,\vdash b\rightarrowtail\neg A,\dots,\vdash L_{m}\}\big).

L_{i}=b\leftarrowtail\neg A
.
Assume that
\vdash L_{i}
is the active sequent of the

\leadsto
-decomposition and that
\leadsto
is based on the following
\leadsto_{0}
:

\vdash b\leftarrowtail\neg A\ \ \ \leadsto_{0}\ \ \ \{\vdash c\rightarrowtail A\}

where
\neg c\vDash b
. Thus,

\displaystyle\mathsf{ms}\big(\{\vdash L_{1},\dots,\vdash c\rightarrowtail A,\dots,\vdash L_{m}\}\big)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ref{def:ms}}}{{=}}3^{\mathsf{cn}(L_{1})}+\dots+3^{\mathsf{cn}(c\rightarrowtail A)}+\dots+3^{\mathsf{cn}(L_{n})}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ref{def:cn}}}{{=}}3^{\mathsf{cn}(L_{1})}+\dots+3^{\mathsf{cn}(A)}+\dots+3^{\mathsf{cn}(L_{n})}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ref{def:cn}}}{{=}}3^{\mathsf{cn}(L_{1})}+\dots+3^{\mathsf{cn}(\neg A)–1}+\dots+3^{\mathsf{cn}(L_{n})}

\displaystyle<3^{\mathsf{cn}(L_{1})}+\dots+3^{\mathsf{cn}(b\leftarrowtail\neg A)}+\dots+3^{\mathsf{cn}(L_{n})}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ref{def:ms}}}{{=}}\mathsf{ms}\big(\{\vdash L_{1},\dots,\vdash b\leftarrowtail\neg A,\dots,\vdash L_{m}\}\big).

L_{i}=b\rightarrowtail A\vee B
. Assume that

\vdash L_{i}
is the active sequent of
\leadsto
and that

\leadsto
is based on the following
\leadsto_{0}
:

\vdash b\rightarrowtail A\vee B\ \ \ \leadsto_{0}\ \ \ \{\vdash c\rightarrowtail A,\vdash d\rightarrowtail B\}

where
b\vDash c\vee d
. Then,

\displaystyle\mathsf{ms}\big(\{\vdash L_{i},\dots,\vdash c\rightarrowtail A,\vdash d\rightarrowtail B,\dots,\vdash L_{m}\}\big)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ref{def:ms}}}{{=}}3^{\mathsf{cn}(L_{1})}+\dots+3^{\mathsf{cn}(c\rightarrowtail A)}+3^{\mathsf{cn}(d\rightarrowtail B)}+\dots+3^{\mathsf{cn}(L_{m})}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ref{def:cn}}}{{=}}3^{\mathsf{cn}(L_{1})}+\dots+3^{\mathsf{cn}(A)}+3^{\mathsf{cn}(C)}+\dots+3^{\mathsf{cn}(L_{m})}

\displaystyle<3^{\mathsf{cn}(L_{1})}+\dots+3^{\mathsf{cn}(A)+\mathsf{cn}(B)+1}+\dots+3^{\mathsf{cn}(L_{m})}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ref{def:cn}}}{{=}}3^{\mathsf{cn}(L_{1})}+\dots+3^{\mathsf{cn}(b\rightarrowtail A\vee B)}+\dots+3^{\mathsf{cn}(L_{m})}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ref{def:ms}}}{{=}}\mathsf{ms}\big(\{\vdash L_{1},\dots,\vdash b\rightarrowtail A\vee B,\dots,\vdash L_{m}\}\big).

L_{i}=b\leftarrowtail A\vee B
.
Assume that
\vdash L_{i}

is the active sequent of
\leadsto
and that
\leadsto

is based on the following
\leadsto_{0}
:

\vdash b\leftarrowtail A\vee B\ \ \ \leadsto_{0}\ \ \ \ \{\vdash b\leftarrowtail A,\vdash b\leftarrowtail B\}

Then,

\displaystyle\mathsf{ms}(\{\vdash L_{1},\dots,\vdash b\leftarrowtail A,\vdash b\leftarrowtail B,\dots,\vdash L_{m}\})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ref{def:ms}}}{{=}}3^{\mathsf{cn}(L_{1})}+\dots+3^{\mathsf{cn}(b\leftarrowtail A)}+3^{\mathsf{cn}(b\leftarrowtail B)}+\dots+3^{\mathsf{cn}(L_{m})}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ref{def:cn}}}{{=}}3^{\mathsf{cn}(L_{1})}+\dots+3^{\mathsf{cn}(A)}+3^{\mathsf{cn}(B)}+\dots+3^{\mathsf{cn}(L_{m})}

\displaystyle<3^{\mathsf{cn}(L_{1})}+\dots+3^{\mathsf{cn}(A)+\mathsf{cn}(B)+1}+\dots+3^{\mathsf{cn}(L_{m})}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ref{def:cn}}}{{=}}3^{\mathsf{cn}(L_{1})}+\dots+3^{\mathsf{cn}(b\leftarrowtail A\vee B)}+\dots+3^{\mathsf{cn}(L_{m})}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ref{def:ms}}}{{=}}\mathsf{ms}\big(\{\vdash L_{1},\dots,\vdash b\leftarrowtail A\vee B,\dots,\vdash L_{m}\}\big)

L_{i}=b\rightarrowtail A\wedge B
.
The proof is equivalent to the one for
b\leftarrowtail A\vee B
.

L_{i}=b\leftarrowtail A\wedge B
.
The proof is equivalent to the one for
b\rightarrowtail A\vee B
.

L=b\rightarrowtail\mathbf{C}^{q}A
.
Assume that
\vdash L_{i}
is the active sequent of

\leadsto
decomposition and
that
\leadsto
is based on the following
\leadsto_{0}
:

\vdash b\rightarrowtail\mathbf{C}^{q}A\ \ \ \leadsto_{0}\ \ \ \{\vdash c\rightarrowtail A\}

where
\mu(\llbracket c\rrbracket)\geq q
. Then,

\displaystyle\mathsf{ms}\big(\{\vdash L_{1},\dots,\vdash c\rightarrowtail A,\dots,\vdash L_{m}\}\big)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ref{def:ms}}}{{=}}3^{\mathsf{cn}(L_{1})}+\dots+3^{\mathsf{cn}(c\rightarrowtail A)}+\dots+3^{\mathsf{cn}(L_{m})}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ref{def:cn}}}{{=}}3^{\mathsf{cn}(L_{1})}+\dots+3^{\mathsf{cn}(A)}+\dots+3^{\mathsf{cn}(L_{m})}

\displaystyle<3^{\mathsf{cn}(L_{1})}+\dots+3^{\mathsf{cn}(A)+1}+\dots+3^{\mathsf{cn}(L_{m})}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ref{def:cn}}}{{=}}3^{\mathsf{cn}(L_{1})}+\dots+3^{\mathsf{cn}(b\rightarrowtail\mathbf{C}^{q}A)+1}+\dots+3^{\mathsf{cn}(L_{m})}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ref{def:ms}}}{{=}}\mathsf{ms}\big(\{\vdash L_{1},\dots,\vdash b\rightarrowtail\mathbf{C}^{q}A,\dots,\vdash L_{m}\}\big).

L_{i}=b\leftarrowtail\mathbf{C}^{q}A
,
L_{i}=b\rightarrowtail\mathbf{D}^{q}A
,

L_{i}=b\leftarrowtail\mathbf{D}^{q}A
.
The proof is equivalent to the one for

b\rightarrowtail\mathbf{C}^{q}A
.

L_{i}=b\rightarrowtail A
.
Assume that
\vdash L_{i}
is the active sequent of
\leadsto

decomposition and that
\leadsto
is based on the following

\leadsto_{0}
:

\vdash b\rightarrowtail A\ \ \ \leadsto_{0}\ \ \ \{\}

where
\mu(\llbracket b\rrbracket)=0
.
Since for Definition
A.10
and
A.9
,

\mathsf{cn}(\{\})=0
and
\mathsf{cn}(\vdash b\leftarrowtail A)>0
,
clearly
\mathsf{cn}(\{\})<\mathsf{cn}(\vdash b\leftarrowtail A)
.

L_{i}=b\leftarrowtail A
,

L_{i}=b\rightarrowtail\mathbf{D}^{0}A
,

L_{i}=b\leftarrowtail\mathbf{C}^{0}A
.
The proof is equivalent to the other for

b\rightarrowtail A
.
∎

\leadsto
-Normal Sequents.

It is possible to show that
\leadsto
-normal sequents are
valid if and only if they are derivable in
\mathbf{GsCPL_{0}}
.
First, it is shown that sequents which are
\leadsto
-normal
are regular. Then, it is proved that regular sequents
are derivable if and only if they are valid.
Putting these two results together it is shown that

\leadsto
-normal sequents are valid only when they are derivable.

Lemma 22
.

If a (non-empty) sequent is
\leadsto
-normal, then it is regular.

Proof.

The proof is by contraposition.
Given an arbitrary
non-regular
sequent,
call it
\vdash L
, it is shown that it is not
\leadsto
-normal.
Every possible form of lebelled formula
L
is considered.

L=b\rightarrowtail\neg A
.
Clearly,
\llbracket b\rrbracket\subseteq 2^{\omega}=\llbracket\neg\bot\rrbracket
,
which is
b\vDash\neg\bot
.
Then, the following decomposition is well-defined:

\vdash b\rightarrowtail\neg A\ \ \ \leadsto_{0}\ \ \ \{\vdash\bot\leftarrowtail A\}.

L=b\leftarrowtail\neg A
.
Clearly,
\llbracket\neg\top\rrbracket=\emptyset\subseteq\llbracket b\rrbracket
,
which is
\neg\top\vdash b
.
Then, the following decomposition is well-defined:

\vdash b\leftarrowtail\neg A\ \ \ \leadsto_{0}\ \ \ \{\vdash\top\rightarrowtail A\}.

L=b\rightarrowtail A\vee B
.
For every

\llbracket b\rrbracket\subseteq 2^{\omega}=\llbracket\top\vee\top\rrbracket
,
which is
b\vDash(\top\vee\top)
.
Then, the following decomposition is well-defined:

\vdash b\rightarrowtail A\vee B\ \ \ \leadsto_{0}\ \ \ \{\vdash\top\rightarrowtail A,\vdash\top\rightarrowtail B\}.

L=b\leftarrowtail A\vee B
.
Then, the following decomposition is well-defined:

\vdash b\leftarrowtail A\vee B\ \ \ \leadsto_{0}\ \ \ \{\vdash b\leftarrowtail A,\vdash b\leftarrowtail B\}.

L=b\rightarrowtail A\wedge B
.
Then, the following decomposition is well-defined:

\vdash b\rightarrowtail A\wedge B\ \ \ \leadsto_{0}\ \ \ \{\vdash b\rightarrowtail A,\vdash b\rightarrowtail B\}.

L=b\leftarrowtail A\wedge B
.
For every

b,\llbracket\bot\wedge\bot\rrbracket=\emptyset\subseteq\llbracket b\rrbracket
,
which is
(\bot\wedge\bot)\vDash b
.
Then, the following decomposition is well-defined:

\vdash b\leftarrowtail A\wedge B\ \ \ \leadsto_{0}\ \ \ \{\vdash\bot\leftarrowtail A,\vdash\bot\leftarrowtail B\}.

L=b\rightarrowtail\mathbf{C}^{q}A
.
For every
q\in[0,1]
,

\mu(\llbracket\top\rrbracket)=\mu(2^{\omega})\geq q
.
Then, the following decomposition is well-defined:

\vdash b\rightarrowtail\mathbf{C}^{q}A\ \ \ \leadsto_{0}\ \ \ \{\vdash\top\rightarrowtail A\}.

L=b\leftarrowtail\mathbf{C}^{q}A
.
There are two possible cases, basing on the value of

q\in[0,1]
:

•

Let
q\neq 0
. Then,

\mu(\llbracket\bot\rrbracket)=\mu(\emptyset)<q
and the following
decomposition is well-defined:

\vdash b\leftarrowtail\mathbf{C}^{q}A\ \ \ \leadsto_{0}\ \ \ \{\vdash\bot\leftarrowtail A\}.

•

Let
q=0
. There are two possible sub-cases:

1.

Let
\mu(\llbracket b\rrbracket)=1
. Then, the following
decomposition is well-defined:

\vdash b\leftarrowtail\mathbf{C}^{0}A\ \ \ \leadsto_{0}\ \ \ \{\}.

2.

Let
\mu(\llbracket b\rrbracket)\neq 1
. Then, the following
decomposition is well-defined:

\vdash b\leftarrowtail\mathbf{C}^{0}A\ \ \ \leadsto_{0}\ \ \ \{\vdash\bot\}.

L=b\rightarrowtail\mathbf{D}^{q}A
. There are two possible cases,
basing on the value of
q\in[0,1]
.

•

Let
q\neq 0
. Then
\mu(\llbracket\bot\rrbracket)=\mu(\emptyset)<q

and the decomposition is well-defined:

\vdash b\rightarrowtail\mathbf{D}^{q}A\ \ \ \leadsto_{0}\ \ \ \{\vdash\bot\leftarrowtail A\}.

•

Let
q=0
. There are two possible sub-cases:

1.

Let
\mu(\llbracket b\rrbracket)=0
. Then, the following
decomposition is well-defined:

\vdash b\rightarrowtail\mathbf{D}^{0}A\ \ \ \leadsto_{0}\ \ \ \{\}.

2.

Let
\mu(\llbracket b\rrbracket)\neq 0
. Then, the following
decomposition is well-defined:

\vdash b\rightarrowtail\mathbf{D}^{0}A\ \ \ \leadsto_{0}\ \ \ \{\vdash\bot\}.

L=b\leftarrowtail\mathbf{D}^{q}A.
For every
q\in[0,1]
,

\mu(\llbracket\top\vee\top\rrbracket)=\mu(2^{\omega})\geq q
.
Then, the following decomposition is well-defined:

\vdash b\leftarrowtail\mathbf{D}^{q}A\ \ \ \leadsto_{0}\ \ \ \{\vdash\top\rightarrowtail A\}.

∎

Lemma 23
.

A regular sequent is valid if and only if it is derivable in

\mathbf{GsCPL_{0}}
.

Proof.

Let
\vdash L
be a regular sequent, which is let
L
be basic.

\Rightarrow

Assume that
\vdash L
is valid.
Since
L
is a basic formula, there are two main cases.

L=b\rightarrowtail\mathbf{n}
, for arbitrary
b
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
Since for hypothesis
\vDash b\rightarrowtail\mathbf{n}
,
which is
\llbracket b\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket\mathbf{n}\rrbracket
,
and
(\llbracket\mathbf{n}\rrbracket=)\mathrm{Cyl}(n)=x_{n}
, also

\llbracket b\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket x_{x}\rrbracket
,
which is
b\vDash x_{n}
.
Therefore,
\vdash b\rightarrowtail\mathbf{n}

is derivable by means of
\mathsf{Ax1}
:

b\vDash x_{n}

\mathsf{Ax1}

\vdash b\rightarrowtail\mathbf{n}

L=b\leftarrowtail\mathbf{n}
, for arbitrary
b
and

n\in\mathbb{N}
. Since for hypothesis
\vDash b\leftarrowtail\mathbf{n}
,
which is
\llbracket\mathbf{n}\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket b\rrbracket
, and

\llbracket x_{n}\rrbracket=\mathrm{Cyl}(n)(=\mathbf{n})
,
also
\llbracket x_{n}\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket b\rrbracket
,
which is
x_{n}\vDash b
.
Therefore,
\vdash b\leftarrowtail\mathbf{n}
is derivable by means
of
\mathsf{Ax2}
:

x_{n}\vDash b

\mathsf{Ax2}

\vdash b\leftarrowtail\mathbf{n}

Therefore, in both cases,
\vdash L
is derivable in

\mathbf{GsCPL_{0}}
.

Assume that
\vdash L
is derivable
in
\mathbf{GsCPL_{0}}
. Then, by Proposition
2
,
\vDash L
.
∎

Corollary 2
.

If a sequent is
\leadsto
-normal, then it is valid
if and only if it is derivable in
\mathbf{GsCPL_{0}}
.

Proof.

By putting Lemma
22

and
23
together.
∎

All the given results can be naturally extended from sequents
to sets, obtaining in particular that,
if a set of sequents is
\leadsto
-normal,
which is each of its sequents is
\leadsto
-normal,
then it is valid if and only if it is derivable,
namely its sequents are valid if and only if they are
derivable.

Existential Preservation of Validity.

It is possible to prove that the validity is existentially preserved
through
\leadsto
-decomposition.
It is first needed to establish the following auxiliary lemma.

Lemma 24
.

For every formula of
\textsf{{CPL}}_{0}
, call it
A
,
there is an expression
b_{A}
,
such that
\llbracket A\rrbracket=\llbracket b_{A}\rrbracket
.

Proof.

The proof is by induction on the structure of
A
:

•

Base case.

Let
A
be atomic, which is
A\equiv\mathbf{n}
,
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
By Definition
3.2
,
\llbracket\mathbf{n}\rrbracket
=
\mathrm{Cyl}(n)
,
but also
\mathrm{Cyl}(n)
=
\llbracket x_{n}\rrbracket
.
Therefore, let us define
b_{A}=x_{n}
,
so that clearly
\llbracket\mathbf{n}\rrbracket
=
\llbracket x_{n}\rrbracket
.

•

Inductive case.
There are some possible cases.

A=\neg B
.
By IH, there is a
b_{B}
such that
\llbracket B\rrbracket=\llbracket b_{B}\rrbracket
.
So, let us define
b_{A}
=
\neg b_{B}
,
which is well-defined by Definition
3.2
.
Indeed,
\llbracket b_{A}\rrbracket
=
\llbracket\neg b_{B}\rrbracket
=
2^{\omega}\ –\ \llbracket b_{B}\rrbracket

\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{IH}}}{{=}}

2^{\omega}\ –\ \llbracket B\rrbracket
=
\llbracket\neg B\rrbracket
.

A
=
B\vee C
.
By IH, there are two
b_{B}
and
b_{C}
such that
\llbracket b_{B}\rrbracket
=
\llbracket B\rrbracket
and
\llbracket b_{C}\rrbracket
=
\llbracket C\rrbracket
.
Thus, let us define
b_{A}
=
b_{B}

\vee

b_{C}
, which is well-defined by Definition
3.2
.
Indeed, as desired,
\llbracket b_{A}\rrbracket
=
\llbracket b_{B}\vee b_{C}\rrbracket
=
\llbracket b_{B}\rrbracket\cup\llbracket b_{C}\rrbracket

\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{IH}}}{{=}}

\llbracket B\rrbracket\cup\llbracket C\rrbracket
=
\llbracket B\vee C\rrbracket
.

A
=
B\wedge C
. By IH, there are two
b_{B}
and
b_{C}
such that
\llbracket b_{B}\rrbracket
=
\llbracket B\rrbracket
and
\llbracket b_{C}\rrbracket
=
\llbracket C\rrbracket
. Let us define
b_{A}
=
b_{B}

\wedge

b_{C}
, which is well-defined by Definition
3.2
.
Indeed,
\llbracket b_{A}\rrbracket
=
\llbracket b_{B}\wedge b_{C}\rrbracket
=
\llbracket b_{B}\rrbracket\cap\llbracket b_{C}\rrbracket

\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{IH}}}{{=}}

\llbracket B\rrbracket\cap\llbracket C\rrbracket
=
\llbracket B\wedge C\rrbracket
.

A
=
\mathbf{C}^{q}B
or
A
=
\mathbf{D}^{q}B
. In both cases, either
\llbracket A\rrbracket
=
2^{\omega}
or
\llbracket A\rrbracket
=
\emptyset
.
In the former case, let us define

b_{A}
=
\top
, as
\llbracket\top\rrbracket
=
2^{\omega}
=
\llbracket A\rrbracket
.
In the latter case, let us define
b_{A}
=
\bot
, since
\llbracket\bot\rrbracket
=
\emptyset
=
\llbracket A\rrbracket
.

∎

Lemma 25

(Existential Preservation of Validity)
.

Each valid sequent has a valid
\leadsto
-normal form.

Proof.

Let
\vdash L
be an arbitrary,
valid
sequent.
It is shown that
\vdash L
has a valid
\leadsto
-normal form.
The proof is by exhaustive inspection. There are two main cases.

•

Let
\vdash L
be such that no

\leadsto_{0}
-reduction can be applied on it.
Then, for Lemma
22
,
the sequent is either empty or regular,
which is
L
is a basic formula.
In both cases, the sequent is trivially
\leadsto
-normal and,
for hypothesis, valid.

•

Let
\vdash L
be
\leadsto
-reducible.
By Lemma
32
, there is no infinite
reduction sequence.
Thus, it is sufficient to prove that each
reduction step

existentially preserves validity, which is that for every possible

\leadsto
, based on every possible
\leadsto_{0}
reduction,
if
\vDash L
, then there is a
\leadsto_{0}
such that

\vdash L\leadsto_{0}\Phi
, and
\Phi
is valid.
The proof is based on the exhaustive consideration
of all possible
\leadsto_{0}
form.

L=b\rightarrowtail\neg A
.
Let us consider a Boolean formula
defined as
c=\neg b
.
Thus,
\llbracket\neg c\rrbracket=2^{\omega}

–

\llbracket c\rrbracket=2^{\omega}

–

(
2^{\omega}

–

\llbracket b\rrbracket
) =

\llbracket b\rrbracket
and so, in
particular
b\vDash\neg c
.
Let us consider the following well-defined

\leadsto_{0}
-decomposition (given
b\vDash\neg c
):

\vdash b\rightarrowtail\neg A\ \ \ \leadsto_{0}\ \ \ \{\vdash c\leftarrowtail A\}.

For hypothesis
b\rightarrowtail\neg A
, which is

\llbracket b\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket\neg A\rrbracket
.
By basic set theory,
also
\llbracket A\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket\neg b\rrbracket
.
Since for construction
c=\neg b
,

\llbracket A\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket c\rrbracket
holds, which is

\vDash c\leftarrowtail A
, as desired.
Therefore, there is a
\leadsto_{0}
-decomposition
which preserves validity.

L=b\leftarrowtail\neg A
.
Let us consider a Boolean formula defined as

c=\neg b
.
Thus,
\llbracket c\rrbracket=\llbracket\neg b\rrbracket
and, in particular,

\neg c\vDash b
.
Let us consider the following
\leadsto_{0}
-decomposition,
which is well-defined (given
\neg c\vDash b
):

\vdash b\leftarrowtail\neg A\ \ \ \leadsto_{0}\ \ \ \{\vdash c\rightarrowtail A\}.

For hypothesis
b\leftarrowtail\neg A
, which is

\llbracket\neg A\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket b\rrbracket
, so for basic set
theory
\llbracket\neg b\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket A\rrbracket
.
Since for construction
\llbracket c\rrbracket=\llbracket\neg b\rrbracket
,
also
\llbracket c\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket A\rrbracket
, which
is
\vDash c\rightarrowtail A
and
the described
\leadsto_{0}
-decomposition is as desired.

L=b\rightarrowtail A\vee B
. Let us
consider two
b_{A}
and
b_{B}
such
that
\llbracket b_{A}\rrbracket=\llbracket A\rrbracket
and

\llbracket b_{B}\rrbracket=\llbracket B\rrbracket
(which must exists
by Lemma
24
).
For hypothesis
\vDash b\rightarrowtail A\vee B
, which
is
\llbracket b\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket A\rrbracket\cup\llbracket B\rrbracket
.
Thus, for construction, also

\llbracket b\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket b_{A}\rrbracket\cup\llbracket b_{B}\rrbracket
,
which is
b\vDash b_{A}\vee b_{B}
.
Let us consider the following derivation, which
is well-defined (given
b\vDash b_{A}\vee b_{B}
):

\vdash b\rightarrowtail A\vee B\ \ \ \leadsto_{0}\ \ \ \{\vdash b_{A}\rightarrowtail A,\vdash b_{B}\rightarrowtail B\}.

For construction
\llbracket b_{A}\rrbracket=\llbracket A\rrbracket

and
\llbracket b_{B}\rrbracket=\llbracket B\rrbracket
so, in
particular,
\llbracket b_{A}\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket A\rrbracket

and
\llbracket b_{B}\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket B\rrbracket
.
Therefore,
\vDash b_{A}\rightarrowtail A
and

\vDash b_{B}\rightarrowtail B
and the given

\leadsto_{0}
-decomposition is as desired.

L=b\leftarrowtail A\vee B
.
Let us consider the following, well-defined

\leadsto_{0}
-decomposition:

\vdash b\leftarrowtail A\vee B\ \ \ \leadsto_{0}\ \ \ \{\vdash b\leftarrowtail A,\vdash b\leftarrowtail B\}.

For hypothesis
\vDash b\leftarrowtail A\vee B
, which
is
\llbracket A\rrbracket\cup\llbracket B\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket b\rrbracket
.
Then, by basic set theory, both
\llbracket A\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket b\rrbracket

and
\llbracket B\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket b\rrbracket
,
which is
\vDash b\leftarrowtail A
and
\vDash b\leftarrowtail B
,
as desired.

L=b\rightarrowtail A\wedge B
.
The proof is equivalent
to the one for
b\leftarrowtail A\vee B
.

L=b\leftarrowtail A\wedge B
.
The proof is equivalent
to the one for
b\rightarrowtail A\vee B
.

L=b\rightarrowtail\mathbf{C}^{q}A
.
There are two main cases:

1.

Let
\mu(\llbracket b\rrbracket)=0
. Then, the sequent
can be decomposed by means of the following, well-defined

\leadsto_{0}
-decomposition:

\vdash b\rightarrowtail\mathbf{C}^{q}A\ \ \ \leadsto_{0}\ \ \ \{\}.

\{\}
is vacuously valid so the given decomposition
is as desired.

2.

Let
\mu(\llbracket b\rrbracket)\neq 0

For hypothesis
\vDash b\rightarrowtail\mathbf{C}^{q}A
,
which is

\llbracket b\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket\mathbf{C}^{q}A\rrbracket
.
Since
\llbracket b\rrbracket\neq\emptyset
, also

\llbracket\mathbf{C}^{q}A\rrbracket\neq\emptyset
,
which is
\llbracket\mathbf{C}^{q}A\rrbracket=2^{\omega}
,
and so
\mu(\llbracket A\rrbracket)\geq q
.
Let us consider a Boolean formula
b_{A}
,
such that
\llbracket b_{A}\rrbracket=\llbracket A\rrbracket

(it can be constructed due to Lemma
24
).
Thus,
\mu(\llbracket b_{A}\rrbracket)\geq q
and the
following decomposition is well-defined:

\vdash b\rightarrowtail\mathbf{C}^{q}A\ \ \ \leadsto_{0}\ \ \ \{b_{A}\rightarrowtail A\}.

Since for construction

\llbracket b_{A}\rrbracket=\llbracket A\rrbracket
,
in particular also

\llbracket b_{A}\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket A\rrbracket
,
which is
\vDash b_{A}\rightarrowtail A
as
desired.

L=b\leftarrowtail\mathbf{C}^{q}A.

There are two main cases:

1.

Let
\mu(\llbracket b\rrbracket)=1
.
Then, the sequent can be decomposed by
means of the following
well-defined
\leadsto_{0}
-decomposition:

\vdash b\leftarrowtail\mathbf{C}^{q}A\ \ \ \leadsto_{0}\ \ \ \{\}.

\{\}
is vacuously valid,
so the given decomposition is as desired.

2.

Let
\mu(\llbracket b\rrbracket)\neq 1
.
For
hypothesis
\vDash b\leftarrowtail\mathbf{C}^{q}A
,
which is

\llbracket\mathbf{C}^{q}A\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket b\rrbracket
.
Since
\llbracket b\rrbracket\neq 2^{\omega}
,

\llbracket\mathbf{C}^{q}A\rrbracket=\emptyset

and so
\mu(\llbracket A\rrbracket)<q
.
Let us consider a Boolean formula

b_{A}
such that

\llbracket b_{A}\rrbracket=\llbracket A\rrbracket
(it
can be constructed due to Lemma
24
).
Thus,
\mu(\llbracket b_{A}\rrbracket)<q
,
and the following decomposition is well-defined:

\vdash b\leftarrowtail\mathbf{C}^{q}A\ \ \ \leadsto_{0}\ \ \ \{b_{A}\leftarrowtail A\}.

Since for construction

\llbracket b_{A}\rrbracket=\llbracket A\rrbracket
,
in particular also

\llbracket A\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket b\rrbracket_{A}
,
which is
\vDash b_{A}\leftarrowtail A

as desired.

L=b\rightarrowtail\mathbf{D}^{q}A

or
L=b\leftarrowtail\mathbf{D}^{q}A
.
The proof is equivalent to the ones for

b\leftarrowtail\mathbf{C}^{q}A
and

b\rightarrowtail\mathbf{C}^{q}A
.

L=b\rightarrowtail A
and
\mu(\llbracket b\rrbracket)=0
.
Then, the following decomposition is well-defined:

\vdash b\rightarrowtail A\ \ \ \leadsto_{0}\ \ \ \{\}.

\{\}
is vacuously valid so the given decomposition
is as desired.

L=b\rightarrowtail A
and

\mu(\llbracket b\rrbracket)=1
.
Then, the following decomposition
is well-defined:

\vdash b\leftarrowtail A\ \ \ \leadsto_{0}\ \ \ \{\}.

\{\}
is vacuously valid so the given
decomposition is as desired.

There is no other possible case.
(Notice that if

L=b\rightarrowtail\mathbf{D}^{0}A
, for hypothesis,

\vDash b\rightarrowtail\mathbf{D}^{0}A
,
which is

\llbracket b\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket\mathbf{D}^{0}A\rrbracket
.
But clearly
\llbracket\mathbf{D}^{0}A\rrbracket=\emptyset
,
so
\llbracket b\rrbracket=\emptyset
and

\mu(\llbracket b\rrbracket)=0
; equally when

L=b\leftarrowtail\mathbf{C}^{0}A
,

\mu(\llbracket b\rrbracket
)=1.)
∎

Derivability Preservation.

As anticipated, properties and proofs concerning sequents can be easily generalized to corresponding proofs about sets of sequents. Specifically, by considering together Lemma
22
and
Lemma
23
,
it is established a result, corresponding
to Corollary
3
,
namely that a
\leadsto_{0}
-normal set of sequents is derivable
if and only if it is valid is easily proved.

Definition A.11

(Derivable Set)
.

A set of sequents is
derivable
if and only if all of its sequents
are derivable in
\mathbf{GsCPL_{0}}
.

Lemma 26
.

Given two sets of sequent
\Phi,\Phi^{\prime}
,
if
\Phi\leadsto\Phi^{\prime}
and
\Phi^{\prime}

is derivable in
\mathbf{GsCPL_{0}}
,
then
\Phi
is derivable in
\mathbf{GsCPL_{0}}
as well.

Proof.

Assume
\Phi\leadsto\Phi^{\prime}
.
Then, there is a
\vdash L\in\Phi
such
that it is the “active” sequent on which

\leadsto
is based, which is

\leadsto
is based on the following
\leadsto_{0}
-decomposition:

\vdash L\ \ \ \leadsto_{0}\ \ \ \{\vdash L_{1},\dots,\vdash L_{n}\}.

The proof is by inspection of all possible
forms of
\leadsto_{0}
.

Let
L=b\rightarrowtail\neg A
and
its
\leadsto_{0}
-decomposition be as follows:

\vdash b\rightarrowtail\neg A\ \ \ \leadsto_{0}\ \ \ \{\vdash c\leftarrowtail A\}

where
b\vDash\neg c
.
Since
\vdash c\leftarrowtail A\in\Phi^{\prime}
,
for hypothesis
\vdash c\leftarrowtail A

is derivable by a derivation, call
it
\mathcal{D}
.
Therefore,
\vdash b\rightarrowtail\neg A

can be shown derivable in
\mathbf{GsCPL_{0}}
as follows:

\mathcal{D}

\vdash c\leftarrowtail A

b\vDash\neg c

\mathsf{R}_{\neg}^{\rightarrowtail}

\vdash b\rightarrowtail\neg A

Let
L=b\leftarrowtail\neg A
and its
\leadsto_{0}
-decomposition
be as follows:

\vdash b\leftarrowtail\neg A\ \ \ \leadsto_{0}\ \ \ \{\vdash c\rightarrowtail A\}

where
\neg c\vDash b
.
Since
\vdash c\rightarrowtail A\in\Phi^{\prime}
,
for hypothesis
\vdash c\rightarrowtail A

is derivable by a derivation, call it

\mathcal{D}
.
Therefore,
\vdash b\leftarrowtail\neg A
can
be shown derivable in
\mathbf{GsCPL_{0}}
as follows:

\mathcal{D}

\vdash c\rightarrowtail A

\neg c\vDash b

\mathsf{R}_{\neg}^{\leftarrowtail}

\vdash b\leftarrowtail\neg A

Let
L=b\rightarrowtail A\vee B
and

\leadsto_{0}
-decomposition be as follows:

\vdash b\rightarrowtail A\vee B\ \ \ \leadsto_{0}\ \ \ \{\vdash c\rightarrowtail A,\vdash d\rightarrowtail B\}

where
b\vDash c\vee d
.
Since

\vdash c\rightarrowtail A,\vdash d\rightarrowtail B\in\Phi^{\prime}
, both

\vdash c\rightarrowtail A
and

\vdash d\rightarrowtail B
are
derivable by two derivations,
call them
\mathcal{D}
and
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
.
Then,
b\rightarrowtail A\vee B
can be
shown derivable in
\mathbf{GsCPL_{0}}
as follows:

\mathcal{D}

\vdash c\rightarrowtail A

\mathsf{R1}_{\vee}^{\rightarrowtail}

\vdash c\rightarrowtail A\vee B

\mathcal{D}^{\prime}

\vdash d\rightarrowtail B

\mathsf{R2}_{\vee}^{\rightarrowtail}

\vdash d\rightarrowtail A\vee B

b\vDash c\vee d

\mathsf{R}_{\cup}^{\rightarrowtail}

\vdash b\rightarrowtail A\vee B

Let
L=b\leftarrowtail A\vee B
and

\leadsto_{0}
-decomposition be as follows:

\vdash b\leftarrowtail A\vee B\ \ \ \leadsto_{0}\ \ \ \{\vdash b\leftarrowtail A,\vdash b\leftarrowtail B\}.

Since

\vdash b\leftarrowtail A,\vdash b\leftarrowtail B\in\Phi^{\prime}
,
both
\vdash b\leftarrowtail A
and

\vdash b\leftarrowtail B

are derivable by two derivations,
call them
\mathcal{D}
and
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
.
Then
b\leftarrowtail A\vee B
can
be shown derivable in
\mathbf{GsCPL_{0}}
as follows:

\mathcal{D}

\vdash b\leftarrowtail A

\mathcal{D}^{\prime}

\vdash b\leftarrowtail B

\mathsf{R}_{\vee}^{\leftarrowtail}

\vdash b\leftarrowtail A\vee B

Let
L=b\rightarrowtail A\wedge B
and

\leadsto_{0}
-decomposition be as follows:

\vdash b\rightarrowtail A\wedge B\ \ \ \leadsto_{0}\ \ \ \{\vdash b\rightarrowtail A,\vdash b\rightarrowtail B\}.

Since
\vdash b\rightarrowtail A,\vdash b\rightarrowtail B\in\Phi^{\prime}
,
both
\vdash b\rightarrowtail A
and
\vdash b\rightarrowtail B
are
derivable by two derivations,
call them
\mathcal{D}
and
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
.
Then,
b\rightarrowtail A\wedge B
can
be shown derivable in
\mathbf{GsCPL_{0}}
as follows:

\mathcal{D}

\vdash b\rightarrowtail A

\mathcal{D}^{\prime}

\vdash b\rightarrowtail B

\mathsf{R}_{\vee}^{\leftarrowtail}

\vdash b\rightarrowtail A\wedge B

Let
L=b\leftarrowtail A\wedge B
and
\leadsto_{0}
-decomposition
be as follows:

\vdash b\leftarrowtail A\wedge B\ \ \ \leadsto_{0}\ \ \ \ \{\vdash c\leftarrowtail A,\vdash d\leftarrowtail B\}

where
c\wedge d\vDash b
.
Since
\vdash c\leftarrowtail A,\vdash d\leftarrowtail B\in\Phi^{\prime}
,
both
\vdash c\leftarrowtail A

and
\vdash d\leftarrowtail B

are derivable by two derivations, call them
\mathcal{D}

and
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
.
Then,
b\leftarrowtail A\wedge B
can be shown
derivable in
\mathbf{GsCPL_{0}}
as follows:

\mathcal{D}

\vdash c\leftarrowtail A

\mathsf{R1}_{\wedge}^{\leftarrowtail}

\vdash c\leftarrowtail A\wedge C

\mathcal{D}^{\prime}

\vdash d\leftarrowtail B

\mathsf{R2}_{\wedge}^{\leftarrowtail}

\vdash d\leftarrowtail A\wedge B

c\wedge d\vDash b

\mathsf{R}_{\cap}^{\leftarrowtail}

\vdash b\leftarrowtail A\wedge B

Let
L=b\rightarrowtail\mathbf{C}^{q}A
and

\leadsto_{0}
-decomposition be as follows:

\vdash b\rightarrowtail\mathbf{C}^{q}A\ \ \ \leadsto_{0}\ \ \ \{\vdash c\rightarrowtail A\}

where
\mu(\llbracket c\rrbracket)\geq q
.
Since
\vdash c\rightarrowtail A\in\Phi^{\prime}
,

\vdash c\rightarrowtail A
is derivable by a derivation,
call it
\mathcal{D}
.
Then,
\vdash b\rightarrowtail\mathbf{C}^{q}A
can
be shown derivable in
\mathbf{GsCPL_{0}}
as follows:

\mathcal{D}

\vdash c\rightarrowtail A

\mu(\llbracket c\rrbracket)\geq q

\mathsf{R}_{\mathbf{C}}^{\rightarrowtail}

\vdash b\rightarrowtail\mathbf{C}^{q}A

Let
L=b\leftarrowtail\mathbf{C}^{q}A
and
\leadsto_{0}
-decomposition
be as follows:

\vdash b\leftarrowtail\mathbf{C}^{q}A\ \ \ \leadsto_{0}\ \ \ \{\vdash c\leftarrowtail A\}

where
\mu(\llbracket c\rrbracket)<q
.
Since
\vdash c\leftarrowtail A\in\Phi^{\prime}
,

\vdash c\leftarrowtail A
is derivable by a derivation,
call it
\mathcal{D}
.
Then,
\vdash b\leftarrowtail\mathbf{C}^{q}A
can be
shown derivable in
\mathbf{GsCPL_{0}}
as follows:

\mathcal{D}

\vdash c\leftarrowtail A

\mu(\llbracket c\rrbracket)<q

\mathsf{R}_{\mathbf{C}}^{\leftarrowtail}

\vdash b\leftarrowtail\mathbf{C}^{q}A

Let
L=b\rightarrowtail\mathbf{D}^{q}A
and

\leadsto_{0}
-decomposition be as follows:

\vdash b\rightarrowtail\mathbf{D}^{q}A\ \ \ \leadsto_{0}\ \ \ \{\vdash c\leftarrowtail A\}

where
\mu(\llbracket c\rrbracket)<q
.
Since
\vdash c\leftarrowtail A\in\Phi^{\prime}
,

\vdash c\leftarrowtail A
is derivable by a derivation,
call it
\mathcal{D}
.
Then,
\vdash b\rightarrowtail\mathbf{D}^{q}A

can be shown derivable in
\mathbf{GsCPL_{0}}
as follows:

\mathcal{D}

\vdash c\leftarrowtail A

\mu(\llbracket c\rrbracket)<q

\mathsf{R}_{\mathbf{D}}^{\rightarrowtail}

\vdash b\rightarrowtail\mathbf{D}^{q}A

Let
L=b\leftarrowtail\mathbf{D}^{q}A
and

\leadsto_{0}
-decomposition
be as follows:

\vdash b\leftarrowtail\mathbf{D}^{q}A\ \ \ \leadsto_{0}\ \ \ \{\vdash c\rightarrowtail A\}

where
\mu(\llbracket c\rrbracket)\geq q
.
Since
\vdash c\rightarrowtail A\in\Phi^{\prime}
,

\vdash c\rightarrowtail A
is derivable by a derivation,
call it
\mathcal{D}
.
Then,
\vdash b\leftarrowtail\mathbf{D}^{q}A
can
be shown derivable in
\mathbf{GsCPL_{0}}
as follows:

\mathcal{D}

\vdash c\rightarrowtail A

\mu(\llbracket c\rrbracket)\geq q

\mathsf{R}_{\mathbf{D}}^{\leftarrowtail}

\vdash b\leftarrowtail\mathbf{D}^{q}A

Let
L=b\rightarrowtail A
and
\leadsto_{0}
-decomposition
be as follows

\vdash b\rightarrowtail A\ \ \ \leadsto_{0}\ \ \ \{\}

where
\mu(\llbracket c\rrbracket)=0
.
Then,
b\rightarrowtail A
can be shown derivable
in
\mathbf{GsCPL_{0}}

as follows:

\mu(\llbracket b\rrbracket)=0

\mathsf{R_{\mu}^{\rightarrowtail}}

\vdash b\rightarrowtail A

Let
L=b\leftarrowtail A
and
\leadsto_{0}
-decomposition
be as follows:

\vdash b\leftarrowtail A\ \ \ \leadsto_{0}\ \ \ \{\}

where
\mu(\llbracket c\rrbracket)=1
.
Then,
b\leftarrowtail A
can be shown derivable in

\mathbf{GsCPL_{0}}
as follows:

\mu(\llbracket b\rrbracket)=1

\mathsf{R_{\mu}^{\leftarrowtail}}

\vdash b\leftarrowtail A

Notice that if
\vdash b\rightarrowtail\mathbf{D}^{0}A
is
derivable, by Lemma
2

\vDash b\rightarrowtail\mathbf{D}^{0}A
, which is

\llbracket b\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket\mathbf{D}^{0}A\rrbracket
.
But
\llbracket\mathbf{D}^{0}A\rrbracket=0
,
so
\llbracket b\rrbracket\subseteq\emptyset
,
which is
\llbracket b\rrbracket=0
.
Thus, when
\vdash b\rightarrowtail\mathbf{D}^{0}A
is derivable,
it cannot be decomposed as follows:

\vdash b\rightarrowtail\mathbf{D}^{0}A\leadsto_{0}\{\vdash\bot\}
.
The same holds for
b\leftarrowtail\mathbf{C}^{0}A
.
Therefore, all the possible forms of
\leadsto_{0}
have
been considered.
∎

The Completeness of
\mathbf{GsCPL_{0}}
.

Putting these results together, it is possible to
conclude that
\mathbf{GsCPL_{0}}
is complete with respect
to the semantics of
\textsf{{CPL}}_{0}
.
See
5

Proof.

If the sequent is valid, by Lemma
25
, it has a
valid

\leadsto
-normal form.
For Lemma
, a
\leadsto
-normal form is valid if and only if it is derivable, so the given
\leadsto
-normal form must be derivable as well.
Therefore, by Lemma
26
, the given (valid) sequent must be derivable in
\mathbf{GsCPL_{0}}
.
∎

A.3
\textsf{{CPL}}_{0}
-validity is in
\textsf{P}^{\sharp\mathtt{SAT}}

Lemma 27
.

For every
closed
formula of
\textsf{{CPL}}_{0}
,
call it
A
, either
\llbracket A\rrbracket
=
2^{\omega}
or
\llbracket A\rrbracket
=
\emptyset
.

Proof.

The proof is by induction on the structure of
A
.

•

Base case.
If
A
=
\mathbf{C}^{q}B
or
A
=
\mathbf{D}^{q}B
, then for Definition
3.1
either
\llbracket A\rrbracket
=
2^{\omega}
or
\llbracket A\rrbracket
=
\emptyset
.

•

Inductive case
. There are four possible cases:

A=\neg B
.
Since
B
is a closed formula, for IH, either
\llbracket B\rrbracket
=
2^{\omega}
or
\llbracket B\rrbracket
=
\emptyset
.
In the former case,

\llbracket A\rrbracket=\big(2^{\omega}\ –\ \llbracket B\rrbracket\big)=\big(2^{\omega}\ –\ 2^{\omega}\big)=\emptyset.

In the latter case,

\llbracket A\rrbracket=\big(2^{\omega}\ –\ \llbracket B\rrbracket\big)=\big(2^{\omega}\ –\ \emptyset\big)=2^{\omega}.

A=B\vee C
.
Since
B
and
C
are closed formulas,
for IH, both
\llbracket B\rrbracket
and
\llbracket C\rrbracket

are either
2^{\omega}
or
\emptyset
.
If both
\llbracket B\rrbracket
and
\llbracket C\rrbracket
are
\emptyset
,
then

\llbracket A\rrbracket=\llbracket B\vee C\rrbracket=\llbracket B\rrbracket\cup\llbracket C\rrbracket=\emptyset.

Otherwise,

\llbracket A\rrbracket=\llbracket B\vee C\rrbracket=\llbracket B\rrbracket\cup\llbracket C\rrbracket=2^{\omega}.

A=B\wedge C
.
Since
B
and
C
are closed formulas,
for IH, both
\llbracket B\rrbracket
and
\llbracket C\rrbracket

are either
2^{\omega}
or
\emptyset
.
Thus, if both
\llbracket B\rrbracket
and
\llbracket C\rrbracket
are

2^{\omega}
, then:

\llbracket A\rrbracket=\llbracket B\wedge C\rrbracket=\llbracket B\rrbracket\cap\llbracket C\rrbracket=2^{\omega}.

Otherwise,

\llbracket A\rrbracket=\llbracket B\wedge C\rrbracket=\llbracket B\rrbracket\cap\llbracket C\rrbracket=\emptyset.

A=\mathbf{C}^{q}B
or
A=\mathbf{D}^{q}B
.
Then the proof is trivial for Definition
3.1

of
\mathbf{C}
- and
\mathbf{D}
-formulas.

∎

Appendix B
Proofs from Section
4

B.1
Characterizing the Semantics of
CPL
using Boolean Formulas

Definition B.1

(Named Boolean Formulas)
.

Named Boolean formulas
are defined by the following grammar:

b,c:=x_{i}^{a}\;\;\mbox{\Large{$\mid$}}\;\;\top\;\;\mbox{\Large{$\mid$}}\;\;\bot\;\;\mbox{\Large{$\mid$}}\;\;\lnot b\;\;\mbox{\Large{$\mid$}}\;\;b\land c\;\;\mbox{\Large{$\mid$}}\;\;b\vee c

The interpretation
\llbracket b\rrbracket_{X}
of a Boolean formula with

\mathrm{FN}(b)\subseteq X
is defined in a straightforward way following Definition
4.3
.

\displaystyle\llbracket x_{i}^{a}\rrbracket_{X}

\displaystyle:=\{f:X\rightarrow 2^{\omega}\ |\ f(a)(\mathbf{i})=1\}

\displaystyle\llbracket\top\rrbracket_{X}

\displaystyle:=(2^{\omega})^{X}

\displaystyle\llbracket\bot\rrbracket_{X}

\displaystyle:=\emptyset^{X}

\displaystyle\llbracket\neg b\rrbracket_{X}

\displaystyle:=(2^{\omega})^{X}–\ \llbracket b\rrbracket_{X}

\displaystyle\llbracket b\wedge c\rrbracket_{X}

\displaystyle:=\llbracket b\rrbracket_{X}\cap\llbracket c\rrbracket_{X}

\displaystyle\llbracket b\vee c\rrbracket_{X}

\displaystyle:=\llbracket b\rrbracket_{X}\cup\llbracket c\rrbracket_{X}.

Lemma 28
.

Any named Boolean formula
b
with
\mathrm{FN}(b)\subseteq X\cup\{a\}
admits an
a
-decomposition in
X
.

Proof.

We will actually prove a stronger statement: any named Boolean formula
b
admits an
a
-decomposition
\bigvee_{i=1}^{k}d_{i}\land e_{i}
where
\llbracket\bigvee_{i=1}^{j}e_{i}\rrbracket_{X}=\llbracket\top\rrbracket_{X}
.
We argue by induction on
b
:

•

if
b=x_{i}^{a}
or
b=\lnot x_{i}^{a}
, then
k=1
,
d_{i}=b
and
e_{i}=\top

•

if
b=x_{i}^{b}
, where
b\neq a
, then
k=2
,
d_{0}=\top,d_{1}=\bot
and
e_{0}=b,e_{1}=\lnot b

•

if
b=b_{1}\vee b_{2}
thenm by IH,

b_{1}=\bigvee_{i=0}^{k_{1}-1}d^{1}_{i}\land e^{1}_{i}
and

b_{2}=\bigvee_{j=0}^{k_{2}-1}d^{2}_{j}\land e^{2}_{j}
. Then,

\displaystyle b

\displaystyle\equiv\left(\bigvee_{i=0}^{k_{1}-1}d^{1}_{i}\land e^{1}_{i}\right)\vee\left(\bigvee_{j=0}^{k_{2}-1}d^{2}_{j}\land e^{2}_{j}\right)

\displaystyle\equiv\left(\bigvee_{i=0}^{k_{1}-1}d^{1}_{i}\land e^{1}_{i}\land\top\right)\vee\left(\bigvee_{j=0}^{k_{2}-1}d^{2}_{j}\land e^{2}_{j}\land\top\right)

\displaystyle\equiv\left(\bigvee_{i=0}^{k_{1}-1}d^{1}_{i}\land e^{1}_{i}\land\bigvee_{j}e^{2}_{j}\right)\vee\left(\bigvee_{j=0}^{k_{2}-1}d^{2}_{j}\land e^{2}_{j}\land\bigvee_{i}e^{1}_{i}\right)

\displaystyle\equiv\left(\bigvee_{i=0,j=0}^{k_{1}-1,k_{2}-1}(d^{1}_{i}\land e^{1}_{i}\land e^{2}_{j})\right)\vee\left(\bigvee_{j=0,i=0}^{k_{2}-1,k_{1}-1}d^{2}_{j}\land e^{2}_{j}\land e^{1}_{i}\right)

\displaystyle\equiv\bigvee_{i=0,j=0}^{k_{1}-1,k_{2}-1}(d^{1}_{i}\vee d^{2}_{j})\land(e^{1}_{i}\land e^{2}_{j}).

Let
k=k_{1}\cdot k_{2}
. We can identify any
l\leq k-1
with a pair
(i,j)
,
i<k_{1}
and
j<k_{2}
.
Let
d_{i,j}=d^{1}_{i}\lor d^{2}_{j}
and

e_{i,j}=e^{1}_{i}\lor e^{2}_{j}
. We have then that

b=\bigvee_{i=0,j=0}^{k_{1}-1,k_{2}-1}d_{i,j}\land e_{i,j}.

Observe that for
(i,j)\neq(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
,
e_{i,j}\land e_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}\equiv\bot
. Moreover,

\bigvee_{i,j}e_{i,j}\equiv\bigvee_{i,j}e^{1}_{i}\lor e^{2}_{j}\equiv(\bigvee_{i}e^{1}_{i})\lor(\bigvee_{j}e^{2}_{j})\equiv\top\lor\top\equiv\top
.

•

if
b=b_{1}\land b_{2}
, then, by IH,

b_{1}\equiv\bigvee_{i=0}^{k_{1}-1}d^{1}_{i}\land e^{1}_{i}
and

b_{2}\equiv\bigvee_{j=0}^{k_{2}-1}d^{2}_{j}\land e^{2}_{j}
.
Then,

\displaystyle b

\displaystyle\equiv\left(\bigvee_{i=0}^{k_{1}-1}d^{1}_{i}\land e^{1}_{i}\right)\land\left(\bigvee_{j=0}^{k_{2}-1}d^{2}_{j}\land e^{2}_{j}\right)

\displaystyle\equiv\bigvee_{i=0,j=0}^{k_{1}-1,k_{2}-1}d^{1}_{i}\land e^{1}_{i}\land d^{2}_{j}\land e^{2}_{j}

\displaystyle\equiv\bigvee_{i=0,j=0}^{k_{1}-1,k_{2}-1}(d^{1}_{i}\land d^{2}_{j})\land(e^{1}_{i}\land e^{2}_{j}).

As in the case above let
k_{1}\cdot k_{2}
. We can identify any
l\leq k-1
with a pair
(i,j)
,
i<k_{1}
and
j<k_{2}
.
Let
d_{i,j}=d^{1}_{i}\land d^{2}_{j}
and

e_{i,j}=e^{1}_{i}\land e^{2}_{j}
. We have then that

b\equiv\bigvee_{i=0,j=0}^{k_{1}-1,k_{2}-1}d_{i,j}\land e_{i,j}.

As in the previous case we have that for
(i,j)\neq(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
,
e_{i,j}\land e_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}\equiv\bot
and

\bigvee_{i,j}e_{i,j}\equiv\bigvee_{i,j}e^{1}_{i}\land e^{2}_{j}\equiv(\bigvee_{i}e^{1}_{i})\land(\bigvee_{j}e^{2}_{j})\equiv\top\land\top\equiv\top
.

∎

See
8

B.2
The Proof Theory of
CPL

Definition B.2

(Named External Hypothesis)
.

A
named external hypothesis
is an expression of one of the following forms:

•

\vDash\{a\}\in X

•

\mu\big(\llbracket b\rrbracket_{X}\big)=0
or
\mu\big(\llbracket b\rrbracket_{X}\big)=1

•

b\vDash^{X}c

•

b\vDash^{X}\bigvee\{c_{i}\ |\ \mu\big(\llbracket d_{i}\rrbracket_{Y}\big)

\triangleright

q\}
,
\bigvee\{c_{i}

|

\mu\big(\llbracket d_{i}\rrbracket_{Y}\big)\triangleright q\}\vDash^{X}b
, with
\triangleright\in\{\geq,\leq,>,<,=\}

where
i\in\mathbb{N},

X
and
Y
are name sets,
b,c_{i},d_{i}
are named Boolean formulas, and
q\in\mathbb{Q}_{[0,1]}
.

Definition B.3

(Named Labelled Formulas)
.

A
named labelled formula
is a formula of the form
b\rightarrowtail A
or
b\leftarrowtail A
, such that
A
is a formula of
CPL
and
b
is a named Boolean formula.

For the sake of simplicity, given a labelled formula
L
=
b\rightarrowtail A
or
L
=
b\leftarrowtail A
, let us introduce the notion of
\mathrm{FN}(L)
=
\mathrm{FN}(b)\cup\mathrm{FN}(A)
.

Definition B.4

(Named Sequent)
.

A
named sequent
of
\mathbf{GsCPL}
is an expression of the form
\vdash^{X}L
, where
L
is a named labelled formula, and
\mathrm{FN}(L)\subseteq X
.

Let us now define a one-sided, single-succedent, and
labelled sequent calculus, call it
\mathbf{GsCPL}
.

Definition B.5

(The Proof System
\mathbf{GsCPL}
)
.

\mathbf{GsCPL}
is defined by the set of rules displayed in Fig.
7
.

Initial Sequents

\vDash\{a\}\in X

b\vDash^{X}x_{i}^{a}

\mathsf{Ax1}

\vdash^{X}b\rightarrowtail\mathbf{i}_{a}

\vDash\{a\}\in X

x_{i}^{a}\vDash^{X}b

\mathsf{Ax2}

\vdash^{X}\Delta,b\leftarrowtail\mathbf{i}_{a}

Union and Intersection Rules

\vdash^{X}c\rightarrowtail A

\vdash^{X}d\rightarrowtail A

b\vDash^{X}c\lor d

\mathsf{R}_{\cup}^{\rightarrowtail}

\vdash^{X}b\rightarrowtail A

\vdash^{X}c\leftarrowtail A

\vdash^{X}d\leftarrowtail A

c\land d\vDash^{X}b

\mathsf{R}_{\cap}^{\leftarrowtail}

\vdash^{X}b\leftarrowtail A

Logical Rules

\vdash^{X}c\leftarrowtail A

b\vDash^{X}\lnot c

\mathsf{R}_{\neg}^{\rightarrowtail}

\vdash^{X}b\rightarrowtail\neg A

\vdash^{X}c\rightarrowtail A

\lnot{c}\vDash^{X}b

\mathsf{R}_{\neg}^{\leftarrowtail}

\vdash^{X}b\leftarrowtail\neg A

\vdash^{X}b\rightarrowtail A

\mathsf{R1}_{\vee}^{\rightarrowtail}

\vdash^{X}b\rightarrowtail A\vee B

\vdash^{X}b\rightarrowtail B

\mathsf{R2}_{\vee}^{\rightarrowtail}

\vdash^{X}b\rightarrowtail A\vee B

\vdash^{X}b\leftarrowtail A

\vdash^{X}b\leftarrowtail B

\mathsf{R}_{\vee}^{\leftarrowtail}

\vdash^{X}b\leftarrowtail A\vee B

\vdash^{X}b\rightarrowtail A
