\vdash^{X}b\rightarrowtail B

\mathsf{R}_{\wedge}^{\rightarrowtail}

\vdash^{X}b\rightarrowtail A\wedge B

\vdash^{X}b\leftarrowtail A

\mathsf{R1^{\leftarrowtail}_{\wedge}}

\vdash^{X}b\leftarrowtail A\wedge B

\vdash^{X}b\leftarrowtail B

\mathsf{R2^{\leftarrowtail}_{\wedge}}

\vdash_{X}b\leftarrowtail A\wedge B

Counting Rules

\mu(\llbracket b\rrbracket_{X})=0

\mathsf{R_{\mu}^{\rightarrowtail}}

\vdash^{X}b\rightarrowtail A

\mu(\llbracket b\rrbracket_{X})=1

\mathsf{R_{\mu}^{\leftarrowtail}}

\vdash^{X}b\leftarrowtail A

\vdash^{X\cup\{a\}}c\rightarrowtail A

b\vDash^{X}\bigvee_{i}\{e_{i}\ |\ \mu(\llbracket d_{i}\rrbracket_{\{a\}})\geq q\}

\mathsf{R}_{\mathbf{C}}^{\rightarrowtail}

\vdash^{X}b\rightarrowtail\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}A

\vdash^{X\cup\{a\}}c\leftarrowtail A

\bigvee_{i}\{e_{i}\ |\ \mu(\llbracket d_{i}\rrbracket_{\{a\}})\geq q\}\vDash^{X}b

\mathsf{R}_{\mathbf{C}}^{\leftarrowtail}

\vdash^{X}b\leftarrowtail\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}A

\vdash^{X\cup\{a\}}c\leftarrowtail A

b\vDash^{X}\bigvee_{i}\{e_{i}\ |\ \mu(\llbracket d_{i}\rrbracket_{\{a\}})<q\}

\mathsf{R}_{\mathbf{D}}^{\rightarrowtail}

\vdash^{X}b\rightarrowtail\mathbf{D}^{q}_{a}A

\vdash^{X\cup\{a\}}c\rightarrowtail A

\bigvee_{i}\{e_{i}\ |\ \mu(\llbracket d_{i}\rrbracket_{\{a\}})<q\}\vDash^{X}b

\mathsf{R}_{\mathbf{D}}^{\leftarrowtail}

\vdash^{X}b\leftarrowtail\mathbf{D}^{q}_{a}A

where
\bigvee_{i}e_{i}\wedge d_{i}
is an
a
-decomposition of
c
.

Figure 7
:
Proof system for
CPL
.

For the sake of simplicity, let
\mathsf{R_{\mu}^{\rightarrowtail}}
and
\mathsf{R_{\mu}^{\leftarrowtail}}
be called

\mu
-rules.
The notions of derivation and of derivation height for
\mathbf{GsCPL}

are defined in a standard way.

Definition B.6

(Derivations in
\mathbf{GsCPL}
)
.

A
derivation in
\mathbf{GsCPL}
is either an initial sequent or a
\mu
-rule, or it is obtained by applying one of the remaining rules to derivations concluding its premisses.

Definition B.7

(Derivation Height in
\mathbf{GsCPL}
)
.

The
height of a derivation in
\mathbf{GsCPL}
is the greatest number of successive applications of rules in it, where initial sequents and
\mu
-rules have height 0.

\mathbf{GsCPL}
si sound and complete with respect to
the semantics of
CPL
.
Let us start by presenting the notions of validity for labelled formulas
and sequents of
\mathbf{GsCPL}
.
Similarly to
\textsf{{CPL}}_{0}
, given a named Boolean formula
b
,
a formula of
CPL
, call it
A
,
and an
X
, with
\mathrm{FN}(b)\cup\mathrm{FN}(A)\subseteq X
,
the labelled formula
b\rightarrowtail A
(resp.
b\leftarrowtail A
)
is
X-valid
, when
\llbracket b\rrbracket_{X}\subseteq\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X}
(resp.
\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X}\subseteq\llbracket b\rrbracket_{X}
).
Formally,

Definition B.8

(Validity of Labelled Formulas)
.

The validity of a labelled formula is defined as follows:

•

b\rightarrowtail A
is
X
-
valid
iff
\llbracket b\rrbracket_{X}\subseteq\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X}

•

b\leftarrowtail A
is
X
-
valid
iff
\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X}\subseteq\llbracket b\rrbracket_{X}
.

An external hypothesis containing
b_{1},\dots,b_{n}

named Boolean variables is valid
if and only if,
for every
X
such that

\bigcup_{i=1}^{m}\mathrm{FN}(b_{i})\subseteq X
the hypothesis is valid.
The notion of
X
-validity for external hypothesis is as predictable.
For example,
b\vDash^{X}x_{\mathbf{i}}^{a}
is valid if and only if
\mathrm{FN}(b)\cup\mathrm{FN}(x_{\mathbf{i}}^{a})\subseteq X
and
\llbracket b\rrbracket_{X}\subseteq\llbracket x_{\mathbf{i}}^{a}\rrbracket_{X}
and
x_{\mathbf{i}}^{a}\vDash^{X}b
is valid if and only if
\mathrm{FN}(b)\cup\mathrm{FN}(x_{\mathbf{i}}^{a})\subseteq X
and

\llbracket x_{\mathbf{i}}^{a}\rrbracket_{X}\subseteq\llbracket b\rrbracket_{X}
.
A named sequent
\vdash^{X}L
is valid,
denoted
\vDash^{X}L
,
if
L
is
X
-valid.

Definition B.9

(Validity of Sequents)
.

A sequent of
\mathbf{GsCPL}
,
\vdash^{X}L
, is
valid
if and only if
\vDash^{X}L
.
A set of such sequents,
\{\vdash^{X_{1}}L_{1},\dots,\vdash^{X_{m}}L_{m}\}
is
valid
if and only if all its sequents are valid.

Notation 1
.

We write
A\equiv_{X}B
when
\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X}=\llbracket B\rrbracket_{X}
.

Theorem 29

(Soundness of
\mathbf{GsCPL}
)
.

If a sequent is derivable in
\mathbf{GsCPL}
, then it is valid.

Proof.

The proof is by induction on the height of the derivation
of
\vdash^{X}L
, call it
n
.

•

Base case.

The sequent is either an initial sequent or it is derived by a
\mu
-rule. Let us consider the case of
\mathsf{Ax1}
only. The other cases are analogously proved.

\vDash\{a\}\in X

b\vDash^{X}x_{\mathbf{i}}^{a}

\mathsf{Ax1}

\vdash^{X}b\rightarrowtail\mathbf{i}_{a}

As seen, if
b\vDash^{X}x_{\mathbf{i}}^{a}
, then
\mathrm{FN}(b)\cup\mathrm{FN}(x_{\mathbf{i}}^{a})\subseteq X
, and
\llbracket b\rrbracket_{X}\subseteq\llbracket x_{\mathbf{i}}^{a}\rrbracket_{X}
, which is
\llbracket b\rrbracket_{X}\subseteq\{f\in(2^{\omega})^{X}

|

f
(
a
)(
i
) = 1
\}
.
But then,
for Definition
4.3
,

\llbracket\mathbf{i}_{a}\rrbracket_{X}
=
\{f\in(2^{\omega})^{X}

|

f
(
a
)(
i
) = 1
\}
, so
\llbracket b\rrbracket_{X}\subseteq\llbracket\mathbf{i}_{a}\rrbracket_{X}
.
By Definition
B.8
,

b\rightarrowtail\mathbf{i}_{a}
is
X
-valid, so
\vdash^{X}b\rightarrowtail\mathbf{i}_{a}
must be valid as well.

The proof for
\mathsf{Ax2}
proof is equivalent to the one for
\mathsf{Ax1}
,
whereas
\mu
-cases are treated similarly to the corresponding proofs in
\mathbf{GsCPL_{0}}
.

•

Inductive case.
Let us assume soundness to hold for derivations of height up to
n
and show it holds for derivations of height
n
+1.
The proof is equivalent to that for
\mathbf{GsCPL_{0}}
, so let us just consider a few examples.
All the other cases are similar.

\mathsf{R}_{\vee}^{\leftarrowtail}
.
The derivation has the following form:

\vdots

\vdash^{X}b\leftarrowtail A

\vdots

\vdash^{X}b\leftarrowtail B

\mathsf{R}_{\vee}^{\leftarrowtail}

\vdash^{X}b\leftarrowtail A\vee B

For IH, the premisses are valid, so
\vDash^{X}b\leftarrowtail A
and
\vDash^{X}b\leftarrowtail B
, which is
\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X}\subseteq\llbracket b\rrbracket_{X}
and
\llbracket B\rrbracket_{X}\subseteq\llbracket b\rrbracket_{X}
. But then also
\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X}\cup\llbracket B\rrbracket_{X}\subseteq\llbracket b\rrbracket_{X}
, which is
\llbracket A\vee B\rrbracket_{X}\subseteq\llbracket b\rrbracket_{X}
and, thus,
\vdash^{X}b\leftarrowtail A\vee B
is valid.

\mathsf{R}_{\mathbf{C}}^{\rightarrowtail}.

Let
\bigvee_{i}e_{i}\wedge d_{i}
be an
a
-decomposition of
c
.
Then, the derivation has the following form:

\vdash^{X\cup\{a\}}c\rightarrowtail A

b\vDash^{X}\bigvee\{e_{i}\ |\ \mu(\llbracket d_{i}\rrbracket_{\{a\}})\geq q\}

\mathsf{R}_{\mathbf{C}}^{\rightarrowtail}

\vdash^{X}b\rightarrowtail\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}A

For IH,
c\rightarrowtail A
is
X\cup\{a\}
-valid.
Furthermore, since
b\vDash^{X}\bigvee\{e_{i}\ |\ \mu(\llbracket d_{i}\rrbracket_{\{a\}})\geq q\}
,
\llbracket b\rrbracket_{X}\subseteq\bigcup_{i}\{\llbracket e_{i}\rrbracket_{X}

|

\mu
(
\llbracket d_{i}\rrbracket_{\{a\}})\geq q\}
,
which is by Lemma
8

and given that
\bigvee_{i}e_{i}\wedge d_{i}
be an
a
-decomposition of
c
,
\llbracket b\rrbracket_{X}\subseteq\{f\in(2^{\omega})^{X}\ |\ \mu(\Pi_{f}(\llbracket c\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}}
))
\geq q\}
.
But then, since for IH,
\llbracket c\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}}\subseteq\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}}
, also
\llbracket b\rrbracket_{X}\subseteq\{f\in(2^{\omega})^{X}\ |\ \mu(\Pi_{f}(\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}}))\geq q\}
(monotonicity).
Thus,
\{f\in(2^{\omega})^{X}\ |\ \mu(\Pi_{f}(\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}}
))
\geq q\}
=
\llbracket\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}A\rrbracket_{X}
.
Therefore,
\llbracket b\rrbracket_{X}\subseteq\llbracket\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}A\rrbracket_{X}
, which is
\vdash^{X}b\rightarrowtail\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}A
is valid.

\mathsf{R}_{\mathbf{C}}^{\leftarrowtail}.
Let
\bigvee_{i}e_{i}\wedge d_{i}
be an
a
-decomposition of
c
. Then, the derivation has the following form:

\vdots

\vdash^{X\cup\{a\}}c\leftarrowtail A

\bigvee\{e_{i}\ |\ \mu(\llbracket d_{i}\rrbracket_{\{a\}})\geq q\}\vDash^{X}b

\mathsf{R}_{\mathbf{C}}^{\leftarrowtail}

\vdash^{X}b\leftarrowtail\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}A

For IH,
c\leftarrowtail A
is
X\cup\{a\}
-valid. Furthermore,
\bigcup_{i}\{\llbracket e_{i}\rrbracket_{X}\ |\ \mu(\llbracket d_{i}\rrbracket_{\{a\}})\geq q\}\subseteq\llbracket b\rrbracket_{X}
, that, given
a
-decomposition for
c
and Lemma
8
,

\{f\in(2^{\omega})^{X}\ |\ \mu(\Pi_{f}(\llbracket c\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}}))\geq q\}\subseteq\llbracket b\rrbracket_{X}
.
For IH,
\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X}

\subseteq

\llbracket c\rrbracket_{X}
, so
\{f\in(2^{\omega})^{X}

|

\mu(\Pi_{f}(\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}}))\geq q\}\subseteq\llbracket b\rrbracket_{X}.
Thus,

\llbracket\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}A\rrbracket_{X}\subseteq\llbracket b\rrbracket_{X}
, which is
b\leftarrowtail\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}A
is
X
-valid and, thus,
\vdash^{X}b\leftarrowtail\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}A
is valid.

\mathsf{R}_{\mathbf{D}}^{\rightarrowtail}.
Let
\bigvee_{i}e_{i}\wedge d_{i}
be an
a
-decomposition of
c
. Then, the derivation has the following form:

\vdots

\vdash^{X\cup\{a\}}c\leftarrowtail A

\vdots

b\vDash^{X}\bigvee\{e_{i}\ |\ \mu(\llbracket d_{i}\rrbracket_{\{a\}})<q\}

\mathsf{R}_{\mathbf{D}}^{\rightarrowtail}

\vdash^{X}b\rightarrowtail\mathbf{D}^{q}_{a}A

So,
b\leftarrowtail A
is
X\cup\{a\}
-valid.
Furthermore,
\llbracket b\rrbracket_{X}\subseteq\bigcup_{i}\{\llbracket e_{i}\rrbracket_{X}\ |

\mu(\llbracket d_{i}\rrbracket_{\{a\}})

<q\}
, which, given
a
-decomposition of
c
and Lemma
8
, is
\llbracket b\rrbracket_{X}\subseteq\{f\in(2^{\omega})^{X}\ |

\mu(\Pi_{f}(\llbracket c\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}}))<q\}
.
For IH,
\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}}\subseteq\llbracket c\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}}
, so also
\llbracket b\rrbracket_{X}\subseteq\{f\in(2^{\omega})^{X}\ |\ \mu(\Pi_{f}(\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}}))<q\}
. Thus,

\llbracket b\rrbracket_{X}\subseteq\llbracket\mathbf{D}^{q}_{a}A\rrbracket_{X}
. Therefore,
b\rightarrowtail\mathbf{D}^{q}_{a}A
is
X
-valid, which is

\vdash^{X}b\rightarrowtail\mathbf{D}^{q}_{a}A
is valid.

\mathsf{R}_{\mathbf{D}}^{\leftarrowtail}.

Let
\bigvee_{i}e_{i}\wedge d_{i}
be an
a
-decomposition of
c
. The derivation has the following form:

\vdots

\vdash^{X\cup\{a\}}c\rightarrowtail A

\vdots

\bigvee\{e_{i}\ |\ \mu(\llbracket d_{i}\rrbracket_{\{a\}})<q\}\vDash^{X}b

\mathsf{R}_{\mathbf{D}}^{\leftarrowtail}

\vdash^{X}b\leftarrowtail\mathbf{D}^{q}_{a}A

So,
c\rightarrowtail A
is
X\cup\{a\}
-valid.
Furthermore,

\bigcup_{i}\{\llbracket e_{i}\rrbracket_{X}\ |\ \mu(\llbracket d_{i}\rrbracket_{\{a\}}<q\}\subseteq\llbracket b\rrbracket_{X}

Given the
a

and

,
\{f\in(2^{\omega})^{X}

|

.
Since, for IH,
\llbracket c\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}}\subseteq\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}}
, also

|

. Therefore,

,
which is

∎

The proof of the completeness of
\mathbf{GsCPL}
is obtained similarly to the one of
\mathbf{GsCPL_{0}}
.
Let us start by defining the decomposition relation
\leadsto
between sets of sequents, which is based on
\leadsto_{0}
.

Definition B.10

(Decomposition Rewriting Reduction,
\leadsto_{0}
)
.

The decomposition rewriting reduction,
\leadsto_{0}
, from a sequent to a set of sequents
(both in the language of
\mathbf{GsCPL}
), is defined by the following decomposition rewriting rules:

\displaystyle\text{if }b\vDash^{X}\neg c,\ \vdash^{X}b\rightarrowtail\neg A\ \

\displaystyle\leadsto_{0}\ \ \{\vdash^{X}c\leftarrowtail A\}

\displaystyle\text{if }\neg c\vDash^{X}b,\ \vdash^{X}b\leftarrowtail\neg A\ \

\displaystyle\leadsto_{0}\ \ \{\vdash^{X}c\rightarrowtail A\}

\displaystyle\text{if }b\vDash^{X}c\vee d,\ \vdash^{X}b\rightarrowtail A\vee B\ \

\displaystyle\leadsto_{0}\ \ \{\vdash^{X}c\rightarrowtail A,\ \vdash^{X}d\rightarrowtail B\}

\displaystyle\vdash^{X}b\leftarrowtail A\vee B\ \

\displaystyle\leadsto_{0}\ \ \{\vdash^{X}b\leftarrowtail A,\ \vdash^{X}b\leftarrowtail B\}

\displaystyle\vdash^{X}b\rightarrowtail A\wedge B\ \

\displaystyle\leadsto_{0}\ \ \{\vdash^{X}b\rightarrowtail A,\ \vdash^{X}b\rightarrowtail B\}

\displaystyle\text{if }c\wedge d\vDash^{X}b,\ \vdash^{X}b\leftarrowtail A\wedge B\ \

\displaystyle\leadsto_{0}\ \ \{\vdash^{X}c\leftarrowtail A,\ \vdash^{X}d\leftarrowtail B\}

\displaystyle\text{if }c=\bigvee e_{i}\wedge d_{i}\ a\text{-decomposition of }c

\displaystyle\text{and }b\vDash^{X}\bigvee\{e_{i}\ |\ \mu(\llbracket d_{i}\rrbracket_{\{a\}})\geq q\},\ \vdash^{X}b\rightarrowtail\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}A\ \

\displaystyle\leadsto_{0}\{\vdash^{X\cup\{a\}}c\rightarrowtail A\}

\displaystyle\text{if }c=e_{i}\wedge d_{i}\ a\text{-decomposition of }c

\displaystyle\text{and }\bigvee\{e_{i}\ |\ \mu(\llbracket d_{i}\rrbracket_{\{a\}})\geq q\}\vDash^{X}b,\ \vdash^{X}b\leftarrowtail\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}A\ \

\displaystyle\leadsto_{0}\{\vdash^{X\cup\{a\}}c\leftarrowtail A\}

\displaystyle\text{if }c=\bigvee e_{i}\wedge d_{i}\ a\text{-decomposition of }c

\displaystyle\text{and }b\vDash^{X}\bigvee\{e_{i}\ |\ \mu(\llbracket d_{i}\rrbracket_{\{a\}})<q\},\ \vdash^{X}b\rightarrowtail\mathbf{D}^{q}_{a}A\ \

\displaystyle\leadsto_{0}\{\vdash^{X\cup\{a\}}c\leftarrowtail A\}

\displaystyle\text{if }c=\bigvee e_{i}\wedge d_{i}\ a\text{-decomposition of }c

\displaystyle\text{and }\bigvee\{e{i}\ |\ \mu(\llbracket d_{i}\rrbracket_{\{a\}})<q\}\vDash^{X}b,\ \vdash^{X}b\leftarrowtail\mathbf{D}^{q}_{a}A\ \

\displaystyle\leadsto_{0}\{\vdash^{X\cup\{a\}}c\rightarrowtail A\}

As anticipated, we define set-decomposition reduction,
\leadsto
, from a set of sequents to a set of sequents, basing on
\leadsto_{0}
.

Definition B.11

(Set Decomposition,
\leadsto
)
.

The
set-decomposition reduction
,
\leadsto
, from a set of
sequents to another set of sequents, is defined as follows:

\vdash^{X}L_{i}\leadsto_{0}\{\vdash^{X^{\prime}_{1}}L_{i_{1}},\dots,\vdash^{X^{\prime}_{m}}L_{i_{m}}\}

\{\vdash^{X_{1}}L_{1},\dots,\vdash^{X_{i}}L_{i},\dots,\vdash^{X_{n}}L_{n}\}\leadsto\{\vdash^{X_{1}}L_{1},\dots,\vdash^{X^{\prime}_{1}}L_{i_{1}},\dots,\vdash^{X^{\prime}_{m}}L_{i_{m}},\dots,\vdash^{X_{n}}L_{n}\}

As for
\textsf{{CPL}}_{0}
,
\leadsto
is the natural lifting of
\leadsto_{0}
to a relation between sets of sequents. Again, predicate about sequents can be generalized to ones on sets.
Also the definitions of corresponding sets,
\leadsto
-normal form, and normalizations are the equivalent to the ones given for
\textsf{{CPL}}_{0}
. Also the notions of basic and regular sequents are very close to the ones
defined for
\mathbf{GsCPL_{0}}
:

Definition B.12

(Regular Sequent)
.

A
basic formula
is a named labelled formula
b\rightarrowtail A,b\leftarrowtail A,
where
A
is atomic.
A
regular sequent
of
\mathbf{GsCPL}
is a sequent of the form
\vdash^{X}L
, such that,
L
is a (named) basic formula.

All the following lemmas and their respective proofs are analogous
to those for proving the completeness of

\mathbf{GsCPL_{0}}
.

Lemma 30
.

A regular sequent is valid if and only if it is derivable in
\mathbf{GsCPL}
.

Proof.

Let
\vdash^{X}L
be an arbitrary,
regular

sequent, which is let
L
be basic.

\Rightarrow

Assume that
L
is an
X
-valid formula. There are two possible cases.

Let
L=b\rightarrowtail\mathbf{i}_{a}
for some
i\in\mathbb{N}
.
Then,
\llbracket b\rrbracket_{X}\subseteq\llbracket\mathbf{i}_{a}\rrbracket_{X}
,
which is
\llbracket b\rrbracket_{X}\subseteq\{f\in(2^{\omega})^{X}

|

f(a)(i)=1\}=\llbracket x_{i}^{a}\rrbracket_{X}
, with

\mathrm{FN}(\mathbf{i}_{a})\subseteq X
.
Thus,
\vdash^{X}b\rightarrowtail\mathbf{i}_{a}
can be
derived by means of
\mathsf{Ax1}
as follows:

a\in X

b\vDash^{X}x_{i}^{a}

\mathsf{Ax1}

\vdash^{X}b\rightarrowtail\mathbf{i}_{a}

Let
L=b\leftarrowtail\mathbf{i}_{a}
for some
i\in\mathbb{N}
.
Then,
\llbracket\mathbf{i}_{a}\rrbracket_{X}\subseteq\llbracket b\rrbracket_{X}
,
which is
\llbracket x_{i}^{a}\rrbracket_{X}=\{f\in(2^{\omega})^{X}

|

f(a)(i)=1\}

\subseteq

\llbracket b\rrbracket_{X}
,
with
\mathrm{FN}(\mathbf{i}_{a}\subseteq X
.
Thus,
\vdash^{X}b\leftarrowtail\mathbf{i}_{a}
is derivable by
means of
\mathsf{Ax2}
as follows:

a\in X

x_{i}^{a}\vDash^{X}b

\mathsf{Ax1}

\vdash^{X}b\leftarrowtail\mathbf{i}_{a}

\Leftarrow

By Theorem
29
.

∎

The following auxiliary result is established by exhaustive inspection. Since the proof idea is substantially the same as the
one used for
\textsf{{CPL}}_{0}
, just a few examples will be explicitly taken into account.

Lemma 31
.

If a (non-empty) sequent is
\leadsto_{0}
-normal, then it is regular.

Proof Sketch.

The lemma is proved by contraposition.
Assume that
\vdash^{X}L
is an arbitrary, non-regular
sequent, which is that
L
is non-basic.
Let us prove that the sequent is not
\leadsto
-normal.
The proof is by inspection. Let us consider just a few cases.

L=b\rightarrowtail\neg A
.
Then since for every
b
,

\bot\vDash^{X}b
, the following
\leadsto_{0}
-decomposition
is well-defined:

\vdash^{X}b\rightarrowtail\neg A\ \ \ \leadsto_{0}\ \ \ \{\vdash^{X}\top\leftarrowtail A\}.

L=b\rightarrowtail A\vee B
.
Then, since for every

b
,
b\vDash^{X}\top\vee\top
, the following

\leadsto_{0}
-decomposition is well-defined:

\vdash^{X}b\rightarrowtail A\vee B\ \ \leadsto_{0}\ \ \{\vdash^{X}\top\rightarrowtail A,\ \vdash^{X}\top\rightarrowtail B\}.

L=b\leftarrowtail A\vee B
.
Then, the following

\leadsto_{0}
-decomposition is well-defined:

\vdash^{X}b\leftarrowtail A\vee B\ \ \leadsto_{0}\ \ \{\vdash^{X}b\leftarrowtail A,\vdash^{X}b\leftarrowtail B\}.

L=b\rightarrowtail\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}A
.
Let
\top=\bigvee\top\wedge\top
be the
a
-decomposition of

\top
.
Then, for every
b
and
q\in[0,1]
,

b\vDash^{X}\bigvee\{\top

|

\mu(\llbracket\top\rrbracket)\geq q\}
.
Thus, the following
\leadsto_{0}
-decomposition is well-defined:

\vdash^{X}b\rightarrowtail\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}A\ \ \leadsto_{0}\ \ \{\vdash^{X\cup\{a\}}\top\rightarrowtail A\}.

∎

Corollary 3
.

If a sequent is
\leadsto_{0}
-normal, then it is valid if and only if it is derivable in
\mathbf{GsCPL}
.

Proof.

It is a straightforward consequence of Lemma
30
and Lemma
31
.
∎

Lemma 32
.

Reduction
\leadsto
is strongly normalizing.

Proof Sketch.

The proof is again based on the notion of sequent measure.
It is shown that if
\{\vdash^{X_{1}}L_{1},\dots,\vdash^{X_{m}}L_{m}\}

\leadsto

\{\vdash^{X_{1}^{\prime}}\vdash L_{1}^{\prime},\dots,\vdash^{X_{m}^{\prime}}L_{m}^{\prime}\}
,
then
\mathsf{ms}\big(\{\vdash^{X_{1}}L_{1},\dots,\vdash^{X_{m}}L_{m}\}\big)>\mathsf{ms}\big(\{\vdash^{X_{1}^{\prime}}L_{1}^{\prime},\dots,\vdash^{X_{m}^{\prime}}L_{m}^{\prime}\}\big)
.
This property is established by the exhaustive analysis of all
possible forms of
\leadsto_{0}
-reduction applicable
to the set,
which is by dealing with all possible forms of
\leadsto_{0}
-reduction
of
\vdash^{X_{i}}L_{i}
, with
i\in\{1,\dots,m\}
, where

\vdash^{X_{i}}L_{i}
is the active sequent on which the

\leadsto
-reduction is based.
These cases, are very closed to the corresponding ones
in
\textsf{{CPL}}_{0}
proof, so just a few examples are considered.

L_{i}=b\leftarrowtail\neg A
.
Assume that
\vdash^{X}b\leftarrowtail\neg A

is the sequent on which the considered
\leadsto
-reduction
of
\{\vdash^{X_{1}}L_{1},\dots,\vdash^{X_{m}}L_{m}\}

is based, and specifically that the sequent is

\leadsto_{0}
-reduced as follows:

\vdash^{X}b\leftarrowtail\neg A\ \ \leadsto_{0}\ \ \{\vdash^{X}c\rightarrowtail A\}

for some
c
such that
\neg c\vDash_{X}b
.
By Definition
A.9
,
\mathsf{cn}(b\leftarrowtail\neg A)=\mathsf{cn}(c\rightarrowtail A)+1
.
Thus, since the considered
\leadsto_{0}
-step reduces the active
sequent
\vdash^{X}b\leftarrowtail\neg A
only,
for Definition
A.10

\mathsf{ms}\big(\{\vdash^{X_{1}}L_{1},\dots,\vdash^{X}b\leftarrowtail\neg A,\dots,\vdash^{X_{m}}L_{m}\}\big)>\mathsf{ms}\big(\{\vdash^{X_{1}}L_{1},\dots,\vdash^{X}c\rightarrowtail A,\dots,\vdash^{X_{m}}L_{m}\}\big).

L_{i}=b\rightarrowtail A\vee B
.
Assume that
\vdash^{X}A\vee B
is the sequent
on which the considered
\leadsto_{0}
-reduction of

\{\vdash^{X_{1}}L_{1},\dots,\vdash^{X_{m}}L_{m}\}

is based, and specifically that the sequent is

\leadsto_{0}
-reduced as follows:

\vdash^{X}b\rightarrowtail A\vee B\ \ \leadsto_{0}\ \ \{c\rightarrowtail A,\vdash^{X}d\rightarrowtail B\}

for some
c
and
d
such that
b\vDash^{X}c\vee d
.
Thus, by Definition
A.9
and
A.10
,

\displaystyle\mathsf{ms}\big(\{\vdash^{X_{1}}L_{1},\dots,\vdash^{X}c\rightarrowtail A,\vdash^{X}d\rightarrowtail B,\dots,\vdash^{X_{m}}L_{m}\}\big)

\displaystyle=3^{\mathsf{cn}(L_{1})}+\dots+3^{\mathsf{cn}(c\rightarrowtail A)}+3^{\mathsf{cn}(d\rightarrowtail B)}+\dots+3^{\mathsf{cn}(L_{m})}

\displaystyle=3^{\mathsf{cn}(L_{1})}+\dots+3^{\mathsf{cn}(A)}+3^{\mathsf{cn}(B)}+\dots+3^{\mathsf{cn}(L_{m})}

\displaystyle<3^{\mathsf{cn}(L_{1})}+\dots+3^{\mathsf{cn}(A)+\mathsf{cn}(B)+1}+\dots+3^{\mathsf{cn}(L_{m})}

\displaystyle=3^{\mathsf{cn}(L_{1})}+\dots+3^{\mathsf{cn}(b\rightarrowtail A\vee B)}+\dots+3^{\mathsf{cn}(L_{m})}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{ms}\big(\{\vdash^{X_{1}}L_{1},\dots,\vdash^{X}b\rightarrowtail A\vee B,\dots,\vdash^{X_{m}}L_{m}\}\big).

L_{i}=b\leftarrowtail\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}A
.
Assume that
\vdash^{X}b\leftarrowtail\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}A
is the
sequent on which the considered
\leadsto_{0}
-reduction of

\{\vdash^{X_{1}}L_{1},\dots,\vdash^{X_{m}}L_{m}\}

is based, and specifically that the sequent is
\leadsto_{0}
-reduced
as follows:

\vdash^{X}b\leftarrowtail\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}A\ \ \leadsto_{0}\ \ \{\vdash^{X\cup\{a\}}c\leftarrowtail A\}

for some
c
such that

c=\bigvee e_{i}\wedge d_{d}

is the
a
-decomposition of
c
and

\bigvee\{e_{i}

|

\mu(\llbracket d_{i}\rrbracket_{\{a\}})\geq q\}

\vDash^{X}

b
.
By Definition
A.9
,
\mathsf{cn}(b\leftarrowtail\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}A)=\mathsf{cn}(c\leftarrowtail A)+1
.
Thus, since the considered
\leadsto_{0}
-step reduces the active
sequent
\vdash b\leftarrowtail\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}A
only, for
Definition
A.10
,

\mathsf{ms}\big(\{\vdash^{X_{1}}L_{1},\dots,\vdash^{X}b\leftarrowtail\neg A,\dots,\vdash^{X_{m}}L_{m}\}\big)>\mathsf{ms}\big(\{\vdash^{X_{1}}L_{1},\dots,\vdash^{X}c\rightarrowtail A,\dots,\vdash^{X_{m}}L_{m}\}\big).

∎

In order to prove that validity is existentially preserved through

\leadsto
-decomposition, it is required to introduce the following
auxiliary lemma.

Lemma 33
.

For every formula of
CPL
, call it
A
, there is a named Boolean formula
b_{A}
, such that, for every
X
, with
\mathrm{FN}(A)\cup\mathrm{FN}(B)\subseteq X
:

\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X}=\llbracket b_{A}\rrbracket_{X}.

Proof.

The proof is by induction on the structure of
b
.

•

Base case.

A=\mathbf{i}_{a}
. Let
\mathrm{FN}(A)\subseteq X
. For Definition
4.3
,

\llbracket\mathbf{i}_{a}\rrbracket_{X}=\{f\in(2^{\omega})^{X}\ |\ f(a)(i)=1\}=\llbracket x_{\mathbf{i}}^{a}\rrbracket_{X}
. Thus,
b_{A}
=
x^{a}_{i}
.

•

Inductive case.
There are four possible cases.

A=\neg B.

By IH, there is a
b_{B}
such that, for every
X
(
\mathrm{FN}(b)\cup\mathrm{FN}(b_{B})\subseteq X
),
\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X}=\llbracket b_{A}\rrbracket
. Thus, let
b_{A}
=
\neg b_{B}
. Then,

\llbracket\neg b_{B}\rrbracket_{X}=(2^{\omega})^{X}\ –\ \llbracket b_{B}\rrbracket_{X}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle IH}}{{=}}(2^{\omega})^{X}\ –\ \llbracket B\rrbracket_{X}=\llbracket\neg B\rrbracket_{X}=\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X}.

A=B\vee C
.
By IH, there are
b_{B}
and
b_{C}
such that, for every
X
(
\mathrm{FN}(B)\cup\mathrm{FN}(C)\subseteq X
),
\llbracket b_{B}\rrbracket_{X}=\llbracket B\rrbracket_{X}
and
\llbracket b_{C}\rrbracket_{X}=\llbracket C\rrbracket_{X}
.
Thus, let
b_{A}
=
b_{B}\vee b_{C}
. Then,

\llbracket b_{B}\vee b_{C}\rrbracket_{X}=\llbracket b_{B}\rrbracket_{X}\cup\llbracket b_{C}\rrbracket_{X}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle IH}}{{=}}\llbracket B\rrbracket_{X}\cup\llbracket C\rrbracket_{X}=\llbracket B\vee C\rrbracket_{X}=\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X}.

A=B\wedge C
. Equivalent to the previous case.

A=\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}B
and
A=\mathbf{D}^{q}_{a}B
. By Lemma
8
.

∎

Lemma 34
.

For each sequent of
\mathbf{GsCPL}
, if it is valid, then it has a valid

\leadsto
-normal form.

Proof.

The proof is by analytic inspection of all possible cases.
Given an arbitrary single-succedent sequent

\phi=\ \vdash^{X}L
, there are two main cases
to be taken into account:

•

Let
\phi
be such that no
\leadsto_{0}
-decomposition
reduction can be applied on it. Thus, since the sequent is
\leadsto_{0}
-normal, it is either empty or regular, by Lemma
31
.
Thus, it is already a
\leadsto
-normal form, and for hypothesis it is valid.

•

Let
\phi
be
\leadsto_{0}
-reducible.
By Theorem
32
,
\leadsto

is strongly normalizing,
so no infinite reduction sequence is possible.
Consequently, it is enough to prove that each reduction
step existentially preserves validity, which is that
for each possible
\leadsto
based on
\leadsto_{0}
-reduction,
if
\phi
is valid, then there is a set of sequents

\{\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{m}\}
, such that

\phi\leadsto_{0}\{\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{m}\}

and
\{\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{m}\}
is valid.
The proof consists in taking into account all possible forms
of
\leadsto_{0}
, on which the reduction step can be based.
Since these cases are very similar to the corresponding
one considered for
\textsf{{CPL}}_{0}
, just a few examples will be
taken into account.

L=b\leftarrowtail\neg A
. Assume

\phi=\vdash^{X}b\leftarrowtail\neg A
.
Let us show that there is a well-defined
\leadsto_{0}
-reduction
of
\phi
of the following form such that the reduced set
is valid.
For hypothesis
\vdash^{X}b\leftarrowtail\neg A
is valid,
which is
b\leftarrowtail\neg A
is
X
-valid.
Let us consider a Boolean expression
c=\neg b
.
Then,

\llbracket\neg c\rrbracket=(2^{\omega})^{X}\ –\ \llbracket c\rrbracket_{X}=(2^{\omega})^{X}\ –\ \big((2^{\omega})^{X}–\llbracket b\rrbracket_{X}\big)=\llbracket b\rrbracket_{X}

and in particular

\llbracket\neg c\rrbracket_{X}\subseteq\llbracket b\rrbracket_{X}
,
which is
\neg c\vDash^{X}b
.
Consequently, the following
\leadsto_{0}
-reduction is well-defined,

\vdash^{X}b\leftarrowtail\neg A\ \ \leadsto_{0}\ \ \{\vdash^{X}c\rightarrowtail A\}.

Since
b\leftarrowtail\neg A
is
X
-valid, also

\llbracket\neg A\rrbracket_{X}\subseteq\llbracket b\rrbracket_{X}
.
Thus, for basic set theory,

\llbracket\neg b\rrbracket_{X}\subseteq\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X}
.
But for construction
c=\neg b
, so also

\llbracket c\rrbracket_{X}\subseteq\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X}
,
which is
c\rightarrowtail A
is
X
-valid.
Consequently,
\vdash^{X}b\rightarrowtail A
is
valid as well.

L=b\rightarrowtail\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}A
.
Assume

\phi=\ \vdash^{X}b\rightarrowtail\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}A
.
Let us show that there is a well-defined
\leadsto_{0}
-reduction
of
\phi
of the following form such that
the reduced set is valid.
For hypothesis,

\vdash^{X}b\rightarrowtail\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}A
is valid,
which is
b\rightarrowtail\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}A
is

X
-valid.
For Lemma
33
,

\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}}=b_{A}
and,
by Lemma
8
,
there is an
a
-decomposition of

b_{A}:c=\bigvee d_{i}\wedge e_{i}
.
Furthermore,

\llbracket\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}A\rrbracket_{X}
=

\{f\in(2^{\omega}){X}

|

\mu\big(\Pi_{f}(\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})\big)\geq q\}

and
\{f\in(2^{\omega})^{X}

|

\mu\big(\Pi_{f}(\llbracket b_{A}\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})\big)\geq q\}=\bigcup_{i}\{\llbracket e_{i}\rrbracket_{X}

|

\mu(\llbracket d\rrbracket_{\{a\}})\geq q\}
.
Therefore,
\llbracket\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}A\rrbracket_{X}
=

\bigcup_{i}\{\llbracket e_{i}\rrbracket_{X}

|

\mu(\llbracket d_{i}\rrbracket_{\{a\}})\geq q\}
,
the following
\leadsto_{0}
-decomposition
is well-defined:

\vdash^{X}b\rightarrowtail\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}A\ \ \leadsto_{0}\ \ \{\vdash^{X\cup\{a\}}c\rightarrowtail A\}.

For construction
c
is an
a
-decomposition
of
b_{A}
so
\llbracket b_{A}\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}}

=
\llbracket c\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}}
.
In particular,
\llbracket c\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}}\subseteq\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}}
,
which is
c\rightarrowtail A
is
X\cup\{a\}
-valid.
Then,
\{\vdash^{X\cup\{a\}}c\rightarrowtail A\}
is valid
as well.

∎

Finally, it is proved that derivability in
\mathbf{GsCPL}
is
preserved in the following sense.

Lemma 35
.

Given the set of sequents
\Phi,\Phi^{\prime}
,
if
\Phi\leadsto\Phi^{\prime}
and
\Phi^{\prime}
is derivable in
\mathbf{GsCPL}
,
then
\Phi
is derivable in
\mathbf{GsCPL}
as well.

Proof.

For hypothesis
\Phi\leadsto\Phi^{\prime}
, which is, for some

\phi\in\Phi
, there is a
\leadsto_{0}
-decomposition

\phi\ \ \leadsto_{0}\ \ \{\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{m}\}

on which the considered
\leadsto_{0}
is based, so that

\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{m}\in\Phi^{\prime}
.
Since the number of possible
\leadsto_{0}
is finite, the proof
is obtained by considering all forms of reduction
applicable to the set.

L=b\rightarrowtail\neg A
.

and that the considered
\leadsto_{0}

\leadsto_{0}

c

.

(

\mathsf{R}_{\neg}^{\rightarrowtail}

Theorem 36

(Completeness of
\mathbf{GsCPL}
)
.

If a sequent of
\mathbf{GsCPL}
is valid, then it is derivable
in
\mathbf{GsCPL}
.

Proof.

By combining the given Lemma
34
,
Corollary
3
, and
Lemma
35
.
∎

Appendix C
Proofs from Section
5

C.1
Prenex Normal Forms

Lemma 37
.

For every
X
such that
\mathrm{FN}(A)\subseteq X
and
a\in X
,
it holds that:

\llbracket\mathbf{C}^{0}_{a}A\rrbracket_{X}=(2^{\omega})^{X}\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\llbracket\mathbf{D}^{0}_{a}A\rrbracket_{X}=\emptyset^{X}.

Proof.

Let us consider
\llbracket\mathbf{C}^{0}_{a}A\rrbracket_{X}=(2^{\omega})^{X}.

Since for every
\Pi_{f}(\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})
, also
\mu\big(\Pi_{f}(\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})\big)\geq 0
holds.
So, trivially,

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathbf{C}^{0}_{a}A\rrbracket_{X}

\displaystyle=\{f\in(2^{\omega})^{X}\ |\ \mu\big(\Pi_{f}(\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})\big)\geq 0\}

\displaystyle=(2^{\omega})^{X}.

Let us now consider

\llbracket\mathbf{D}^{0}_{a}A\rrbracket_{X}=\emptyset^{X}
.
Since for not
\Pi_{f}(\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})
,
also
\mu\big(\Pi_{f}(\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})\big)<0
holds.
So, trivially,

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathbf{D}^{0}_{a}A\rrbracket_{X}

\displaystyle=\{f\in(2^{\omega})^{X}\ |\ \mu\big(\Pi_{f}(\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})\big)<0\}

\displaystyle=\emptyset^{X}.

∎

Without loss of generality, let us assume
a\not\in\mathrm{FN}(A)
.

Corollary 4
.

For every
X
such that
\mathrm{FN}(A)\cup\mathrm{FN}(B)\subseteq X
and
a\in X
, it holds that:

\displaystyle A\wedge\mathbf{C}^{0}_{a}B

\displaystyle\equiv_{X}A

\displaystyle A\vee\mathbf{C}^{0}_{a}B

\displaystyle\equiv_{X}(2^{\omega})^{X}

\displaystyle A\wedge\mathbf{D}^{0}_{a}B

\displaystyle\equiv_{X}\emptyset^{X}

\displaystyle A\vee\mathbf{D}^{0}_{a}B

\displaystyle\equiv_{X}A.

Proof.

Let us consider

A\wedge\mathbf{C}^{0}_{a}B\equiv_{X}A
.
Thus,

\llbracket A\wedge\mathbf{C}^{0}_{a}B\rrbracket_{X}=\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X}.

By Definition
4.3
,

\displaystyle\llbracket A\wedge\mathbf{C}^{0}_{a}B\rrbracket_{X}=\ \ \

\displaystyle\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X}\cap\llbracket\mathbf{C}^{0}_{a}B\rrbracket_{X}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{L}\penalty\ \ref{Lq0}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X}\cap(2^{\omega})^{X}

\displaystyle=\ \ \

\displaystyle\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X}.

Let us now consider
A\vee\mathbf{C}^{0}_{a}B\equiv_{X}(2^{\omega})^{X}
.
Thus,

\llbracket A\vee\mathbf{C}^{0}_{a}B\rrbracket_{X}=(2^{\omega})^{X}.

Again for Definition
4.3
,
16
16

16

Well-definition is ensured by the fact that
a\not\in X
.

\displaystyle\llbracket A\vee\mathbf{C}^{0}_{a}B\rrbracket_{X}=\ \ \

\displaystyle\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X}\cup\llbracket\mathbf{C}^{0}_{a}B\rrbracket_{X}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{L}\penalty\ \ref{Lq0}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X}\cup(2^{\omega})^{X}

\displaystyle=\ \ \

\displaystyle(2^{\omega})^{X}.

Let us consider

A\wedge\mathbf{D}^{0}_{a}B\equiv_{X}\emptyset^{X}
.
Thus,

\llbracket A\wedge\mathbf{D}^{0}_{a}B\rrbracket_{X}=\emptyset^{X}.

For Definition
4.3
,

\displaystyle\llbracket A\wedge\mathbf{D}^{0}_{a}B\rrbracket_{X}=\ \ \

\displaystyle\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X}\cap\llbracket\mathbf{D}^{0}_{a}B\rrbracket_{X}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{L}\penalty\ \ref{Lq0}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X}\cap\emptyset^{X}

\displaystyle=\ \ \

\displaystyle\emptyset^{X}.

Let us finally consider

A\vee\mathbf{D}^{0}_{a}B\equiv_{X}A
.
Thus,

\llbracket A\vee\mathbf{D}^{0}_{a}B\rrbracket_{X}=\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X}.

For Definition
4.3
,

\displaystyle\llbracket A\vee\mathbf{D}^{0}_{a}B\rrbracket_{X}=\ \ \

\displaystyle\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X}\cup\llbracket\mathbf{D}^{0}_{a}B\rrbracket_{X}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{L}\penalty\ \ref{Lq0}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X}\cup\emptyset^{X}

\displaystyle=\ \ \

\displaystyle\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X}.

∎

In order to prove Lemma
10
, some auxiliary lemmas has to be preliminarily considered.

Lemma 38
.

Given
\mathcal{Z}\subseteq(2^{\omega})^{X\cup Y}
and
f\in\mathcal{X}\subseteq(2^{\omega})^{X}
, then
\Pi_{f}(\mathcal{Z})=\Pi_{f}(\mathcal{Z}\cap(X)^{\Uparrow Y})
.

Proof.

\subseteq

If
g\in\Pi_{f}
(
\mathcal{Z}
), then
f+g\in\mathcal{Z}
. Since
f\in\mathcal{X},
by
(X)^{\Uparrow Y}
definition,
f+g\in(X)^{\Uparrow Y}
. As a consequence,
f+g\in\mathcal{Z}\cap(X)^{\Uparrow Y}
and
g\in\Pi_{f}
(
\mathcal{Z}\cap(X)^{\Uparrow Y}
)
.

\supseteq

Trivial, since the projection operator is monotone.

∎

Lemma 39
.

Let
f\in(2^{\omega})^{X}
,

\mathcal{Z}\subseteq(2^{\omega})^{X\cup Y}
, and

\mathcal{X}\subseteq(2^{\omega})^{X}
,
with
X\cap Y=\emptyset
.
If
\mu\big(\Pi_{f}(\mathcal{Z}\cap(\mathcal{X})^{\Uparrow Y})\big)>0
, then
f\in\mathcal{X}
.

Proof.

By contraposition. Assume
f\not\in\mathcal{X}
. There are two possible cases.

•

If
\mathcal{Z}\cap(X)^{\Uparrow Y}
=
\emptyset
, then

\displaystyle\mu(\Pi_{f}\big(\mathcal{Z}\cap(X)^{\Uparrow Y})\big)

\displaystyle=\mu\big(\Pi_{f}(\emptyset)\big)

\displaystyle=\mu(\emptyset)

\displaystyle=0.

•

Otherwise,

\displaystyle\mu\big(\Pi_{f}(\mathcal{Z}\cap(X)^{\Uparrow Y})\big)

\displaystyle\ =\mu\big(\{g\in(2^{\omega})^{Y}\ |\ f+g\in(\mathcal{Z}\cap(X)^{\Uparrow Y})\}\big)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f\not\in\mathcal{X}}}{{=}}\mu(\emptyset)

\displaystyle\ =0.

Indeed by definition,
(X)^{\Uparrow Y}
=
\{h+y\in(2^{\omega})^{X\cup Y}

|

h\in\mathcal{X}\}
. So (since
X\cap Y
=
\emptyset
), if
f\not\in\mathcal{X}
,
\{g\in
(
2^{\omega}
)
Y

|

f+g\in(X)^{\Uparrow Y}\}
=
\emptyset
. Trivially, also
\{g\in
(
2^{\omega}
)
Y

|

f+g\in\mathcal{Z}\cap(X)^{\Uparrow Y}\}
=
\emptyset
.

∎

It is possible to prove that, for
a\not\in X,\mathrm{FN}(A)\subseteq X,
and
f\in
(
2^{\omega}
)
X
, if
f\in\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X},

(
\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}}
) = (
2^{\omega}
)
{a}
. Intuitively, since
a\not\in\mathrm{FN}(A),\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}}
is a set of
f\in
(
2^{\omega}
)
X∪{a}
, the characterization of which
does not

\{a\}
. So, for each
\alpha\in Y
, either
f
(
\alpha
)
\in\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X}

, or
f
(
\alpha

, and

. Therefore if
f\in\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X},\Pi_{f}(\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}}
) = (
2^{\omega}
)
{a}

Lemma 40
.

Let
a\not\in X,\mathrm{FN}(A)\subseteq X,
and
f\in(2^{\omega})^{X}
:

•

if
f\in\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X},
then
\Pi_{f}(\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})=(2^{\omega})^{\{a\}}

•

if
f\not\in\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X},
then
\Pi_{f}(\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})=\emptyset^{\{a\}}

(
or equivalently,
\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}}=\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X}^{\Uparrow\{a\}}
)
.

Proof.

Since
a\not\in\mathrm{FN}(A)
for every
a\in Y
, there are two possible cases:

•

if
f
(
\alpha
)
\in\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X}
, then
f+g\in\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}}

•

if
f
(
\alpha
)
\not\in\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X}
, then
f+g\not\in\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}}
.

Thus, Let
f\in\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X}
. Then,

\displaystyle\Pi_{f}(\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})

\displaystyle=\{g\in(2^{\omega})^{\{a\}}\ |\ f+g\in\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}}\}

\displaystyle=\{g\in(2^{\omega})^{\{a\}}\ |\ f\in\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X}\}

\displaystyle=(2^{\omega})^{\{a\}}.

Let
f\not\in\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X}
. Then,

\displaystyle\Pi_{f}(\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})

\displaystyle=\{g\in(2^{\omega})^{\{a\}}\ |\ f+g\in\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}}\}

\displaystyle=\{g\in(2^{\omega})^{\{a\}}\ |\ f\in\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X}\}

\displaystyle=\emptyset^{\{a\}}.

∎

Corollary 5
.

Let
a\not\in X,\mathrm{FN}(A)\subseteq X,f\in(2^{\omega})^{X},
and
q>0
. Then:

\displaystyle\mu\big(\Pi_{f}(\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})\big)\geq q

\displaystyle\text{ iff }f\in\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X}

\displaystyle\mu\big(\Pi_{f}(\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})\cap\Pi_{f}(\llbracket B\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})\big)\geq q\wedge\mu\big(\Pi_{f}(\llbracket B\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})\big)\geq q

\displaystyle\text{ iff }f\in\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X}

\displaystyle\mu\big(\Pi_{f}(\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})\cup\Pi_{f}(\llbracket B\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})\big)\geq q\vee\mu\big(\Pi_{f}(\llbracket B\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})\big)\geq q

\displaystyle\text{ iff }f\in\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X}

\displaystyle\mu\big(\Pi_{f}(\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})\cup\Pi_{f}(\llbracket B\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})\big)<q\wedge\mu\big(\Pi_{f}(\llbracket B\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})\big)<q

\displaystyle\text{ iff }f\not\in\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X}

Proof.

First, let us consider:

\mu\big(\Pi_{f}(\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})\big)\geq q\text{ iff }f\in\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X}

For every
\alpha^{\prime}\in X
, there are two possible cases:

•

f
(
\alpha^{\prime}
)
\in\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X}
. Then
\Pi_{f}
(
\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}}
) =
\{g\in
(
2^{\omega}
)
{a}

|

f
(
\alpha^{\prime}
)
\in\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X}\}
= (
2^{\omega}
)
{a}
. So, for every
q
,
\Pi_{f}
(
\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}}
)
\geq q

•

f
(
\alpha^{\prime}
)
\not\in\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X}
. Then
\Pi_{f}
(
\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}}
) =
\{g\in
(
2^{\omega}
)
{a}

|

f
(
\alpha^{\prime}
)
\in\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X}\}
=
\emptyset^{\{a\}}
. So, for every
q
(
q\neq
0),
\Pi_{f}
(
\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}}
)
<q
.

Let us now consider:

\mu\big(\Pi_{f}(\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})\cap\Pi_{f}(\llbracket B\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})\big)\geq q\wedge\mu\big(\Pi_{f}(\llbracket B\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})\big)\geq q\text{ iff }f\in\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X}.

There are two cases to be taken into account:

•

f\in\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X}
.
For Lemma
40
(
a\not\in X
),
\Pi_{f}(\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})

=
(2^{\omega})^{\{a\}}
, so

\displaystyle\mu\big(\Pi_{f}(\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})\cap\Pi_{f}(\llbracket B\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})\big)

\displaystyle=\mu\big((2^{\omega})^{\{a\}}\cap\Pi_{f}(\llbracket B\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})\big)

\displaystyle=\mu\big(\Pi_{f}(\llbracket B\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})\big).

•

f\not\in\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X}
.
For Lemma
40
,
\Pi_{f}
(
\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}}
) =
\emptyset^{X}
, so

\displaystyle\mu\big(\Pi_{f}(\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})\cap\Pi_{f}(\llbracket B\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})\big)

\displaystyle=\mu\big(\emptyset^{\{a\}}\cap\Pi_{f}(\llbracket B\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})\big)

\displaystyle=\mu\big(\emptyset^{\{a\}}\big)

\displaystyle=0.

Therefore,

\Leftarrow

If
f\in\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X}
and
\mu\big(\Pi_{f}(\llbracket B\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})\big)\geq q
,
then for the first clause

\mu\big(\Pi_{f}(\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})\cap\Pi_{f}(\llbracket B\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})\big)
=

\mu\big(\Pi_{f}(\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}}))
.
Therefore,
\mu\big(\Pi_{f}(\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})\cap\Pi_{f}(\llbracket B\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})\big)\geq q
.

\Rightarrow

If
f\not\in\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X},
then for the second clause

\mu\big(\Pi_{f}(\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})\cap\Pi_{f}(\llbracket B\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})\big)=0
.
Therefore,
\mu\big(\Pi_{f}(\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})\cap\Pi_{f}(\llbracket B\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})\big)<q
.

If
f\in\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X}
and

\mu\big(\Pi_{f}(\llbracket B\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})\big)<q
,
for the first clause
\mu\big(\Pi_{f}(\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}}\cap\Pi_{f}(\llbracket B\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})\big)
=

\mu\big(\Pi_{f}(\llbracket B\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})\big)
.
So,
\mu\big(\Pi_{f}(\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})\cap\Pi_{f}(\llbracket B\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})\big)<q
.

It is now possible to take into account:

\mu\big(\Pi_{f}(\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})\cup\Pi_{f}(\llbracket B\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})\big)\geq q\vee\mu\big(\Pi_{f}(\llbracket B\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})\big)\geq q\text{ iff }f\in\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X}.

There are two cases to be considered:

•

f\in\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X}
. For Lemma
40
, (
a\not\in X
),
\Pi_{f}
(
\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}}
) = (
2^{\omega}
)
{a}
, so

\displaystyle\mu\big(\Pi_{f}(\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})\cup\Pi_{f}(\llbracket B\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})\big)

\displaystyle=\mu\big((2^{\omega})^{\{a\}}\cup\Pi_{f}(\llbracket B\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})\big)

\displaystyle=\mu\big((2^{\omega})^{\{a\}}\big)

\displaystyle=1.\normalsize

•

f\not\in\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X}
. For Lemma
40
,
\Pi_{f}
(
\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}}
) =
\emptyset^{X}
, so

\displaystyle\mu\big(\Pi_{f}(\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})\cup\Pi_{f}(\llbracket B\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})\big)

\displaystyle=\mu\big(\emptyset^{\{a\}}\cup\Pi_{f}(\llbracket B\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})\big)

\displaystyle=\mu\big(\Pi_{f}(\llbracket B\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})\big).

Therefore,

\Leftarrow

If
f\in\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X},
then, for the first clause,
\mu
(
\Pi
(
\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}}
)
\cup

\Pi_{f}
(
\llbracket B\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}}
)) = 1.
So, for every
q
,
\mu
(
\Pi_{f}
(
\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}}
)
\cup

\Pi
(
\llbracket B\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}}
))
\geq q
.

If
\mu
(
\Pi_{f}
(
\llbracket B\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}}
))
\geq q
, then also
\mu
(
\Pi_{f}
(
\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}}
)
\cup

\Pi_{f}
(
\llbracket B\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}}
))
\geq q
.

\Rightarrow

If
f\not\in\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X}
and
\mu
(
\Pi_{f}
(
\llbracket B\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}}
))
<q
, then, for the second clause,
\mu
(
\Pi_{f}
(
\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}}
)
\cup

\Pi_{f}
(
\llbracket B\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}}
)) =
\mu
(
\Pi_{f}
(
\llbracket B\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}}
))
<q
.

It is now possible to deal with:

\mu\big(\Pi_{f}(\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})\cup\Pi_{f}(\llbracket B\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})\big)<q\wedge\mu\big(\Pi_{f}(\llbracket B\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})\big)<q\text{ iff }f\not\in\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X}.

Let us take into account two cases:

•

f\in\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X}
.
By Lemma
40
(
a\not\in X
),

\Pi_{f}\big(\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}}\big)
= (
2^{\omega}
)
{a}
, so

\displaystyle\mu\big(\Pi_{f}(\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})\cup\Pi_{f}(\llbracket B\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})\big)

\displaystyle=\mu\big((2^{\omega})^{\{a\}}\cup\Pi_{f}(\llbracket B\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})\big)

\displaystyle=\mu\big((2^{\omega})^{\{a\}}\big)

\displaystyle=1

•

f\not\in\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X}
.
By Lemma
40
,
\Pi_{f}\big(\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}}\big)
=
\emptyset^{\{a\}}
, so

\displaystyle\mu\big(\Pi_{f}(\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})\cup\Pi_{f}(\llbracket B\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})\big)

\displaystyle=\mu\big(\emptyset^{\{a\}}\cup\Pi_{f}(\llbracket B\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})\big)

\displaystyle=\mu\big(\Pi_{f}(\llbracket B\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})\big).

Therefore,

\Leftarrow

Let
f\not\in\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X}
and

\mu\big(\Pi_{f}(\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})\big)<q
.
For the second clause,

\mu\big(\Pi_{f}(\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})\cup\Pi_{f}(\llbracket B\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})\big)

=
\mu\big(\Pi_{f}(\llbracket B\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})\big)
.
Since for hypothesis
\mu\big(\Pi_{f}(\llbracket B\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})\big)<q
, also

\mu\big(\Pi_{f}(\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})\cup\Pi_{f}(\llbracket B\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})\big)<q
.

\Rightarrow

Let
f\in\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X}
.
For the first clause

\mu\big(\Pi_{f}(\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})\cup\Pi_{f}(\llbracket B\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})\big)=1
.
Thus, for every
q
,

\mu\big(\Pi_{f}(\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})\cup\Pi_{f}(\llbracket B\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})\big)>q
.

Let
f\not\in\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X}
and
\mu\big(\Pi_{f}(\llbracket B\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})\big)\geq q
.
For the second clause,
\mu\big(\Pi_{f}(\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})\cup\Pi_{f}(\llbracket B\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})\big)

=
\mu\big(\Pi_{f}(\llbracket B\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})\big)
.
For hypothesis,
\mu\big(\Pi_{f}(\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})\big)\geq q
,
thus also
\mu\big(\Pi_{f}(\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})\cup\Pi_{f}(\llbracket B\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})\big)\geq q
.

∎

Proof.

Let us show:

\llbracket A\wedge\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}B\rrbracket_{X}=\llbracket\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}(A\wedge B)\rrbracket_{X}.

For the Definition
4.3
,
17
17

17

\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}
is well-defined is guaranteed by the fact that
X
=

so, for hypothesis,

.

\llbracket A\wedge\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}B\rrbracket_{X}=\llbracket A\rrbracket_{X}\cap\llbracket\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}B\rrbracket_{X}.

Let
f\in
(
2^{\omega}
)
X
. There are two cases:

•

.

\{f\in(2^{\omega})^{X}\ |

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}(A\wedge B)\rrbracket_{X}

.

\{f\in(2^{\omega}

Let us now prove:

\llbracket A\wedge\mathbf{D}^{q}_{a}B\rrbracket_{X}=\llbracket\mathbf{D}^{q}_{a}(\neg A\vee B)\rrbracket_{X}

Well-definition is guaranteed by the fact that, for hypothesis,

.

)

\mu\big(\Pi_{f}(\llbracket B\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})\big)\geq q

\{f\in(2^{\omega})^{X}\ |\ \mu\big(\Pi_{f}(\llbracket B\rrbracket_{X\cup\{a\}})\big)<q\}
